Because it makes feel their structural importance (from the point of view of topology/algebra equivalence), as well as the link with a classification of the gauge theories built on it, with respect to the link between this classification, and the gauge theories, via the idempotents of the algebra, as we will see in section
11.1
with the Serre-Swan theorem. Indeed, for any
AF
algebra, at each rank of the inductive process, we can construct a K-theory and obtain the corresponding invariants. The fact that this K-theory is linked to the one of the next rank induces the possibility to compute the K-theory of the
AF
algebra at the limit of the inductive process. The interest is therefore to use this fact to make gauge theories in the same way,
i.e.
by constructing a gauge theory at each given rank, starting from the previous rank. The fact that K-theory allows us to fully classify
AF
algebras will thus allow us to characterize the limit gauge theory associated with the induced
AF
algebra. We propose here to show the construction of the functor
K_{0}
from an
AF

C^{*}
-algebra to the associated abelian group.

(
Rordam et al., 2000
)

Let us now illustrate how the defining sequence
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\phi_{n,n+1})\,/\,n\geq 0\}
can be used to construct “approximations” of the
K_{0}
group of
\mathcal{A}
, using the same notations as in section
1.3
. The definition (see
(
Rørdam et al., 2000
;
Davidson, 1996
)
for details) of the
K_{0}
group of a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
starts with an equivalence class of projections in
M_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
. The space
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon=\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})/\sim_{0}
of equivalence classes
[P]
of projections in
M_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
is an Abelian semigroup for the additive law
[P]+[Q]\vcentcolon=[P\oplus Q]
where
P\oplus Q\vcentcolon=\begin{pmatrix}P&0\\
0&Q\end{pmatrix}\in M_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
. Then
K_{0}(\mathcal{A})
is the Grothendieck group of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
.
1
1

1

The Grothendieck group
G(S)
of an Abelian semigroup
(S,+)
is the unique Abelian group
K
which satisfies the following universal property: there is a morphism of semigroups
\iota:S\to G(S)
such that for any morphism of semigroups
\varphi:S\to T
for any Abelian group
T
, there is a morphism of groups
\widetilde{\varphi}:G(S)\to T
with
\varphi=\widetilde{\varphi}\circ\iota
.
We denote by
\iota:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})\to K_{0}(\mathcal{A})
the map defining the universal property of
K_{0}(\mathcal{A})
. Then, for any
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
, one has
(
Rørdam et al., 2000
, Prop. 3.1.7)

\iota([P\oplus Q])=\iota([P])+\iota([Q])
;
\iota([P])=\iota([Q])
if and only if there exists
R\in\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
such that
P\oplus R\sim_{0}Q\oplus R
; and
K_{0}(\mathcal{A})=\{\iota([P])-\iota([Q])\,/\,P,Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})\}
. So, describing all the
\iota([P])
is sufficient to get
K_{0}(\mathcal{A})
.

For a matrix algebra
\mathcal{A}=M_{n}
, one has
M_{p}(M_{n})=M_{p}\otimes M_{n}=M_{pn}
and, for any
P,Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(M_{n})
,
P\sim_{0}Q
iff
\rank(P)=\rank(Q)
. So
\mathcal{D}(M_{n})\simeq\mathbb{N}
,
K_{0}(M_{n})=\mathbb{Z}
and
\iota
is the inclusion
\iota:\mathbb{N}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Z}
and so it can be omitted in that case. For a finite dimensional algebra
\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}
, this result generalizes as
P=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}P_{i}\sim_{0}Q=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}Q_{i}
iff
\rank(P_{i})=\rank(Q_{i})
for any
i
, so that
\mathcal{D}(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}})\simeq\mathbb{N}^{r}
and
K_{0}(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}})=\mathbb{Z}^{r}

(
Davidson, 1996
, Ex. IV.2.1)
. Here again
\iota
is the natural inclusion and it can be omitted.

Any morphism of
C^{*}
-algebras
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
induces a canonical morphism of groups
\phi_{*}:K_{0}(\mathcal{A})\to K_{0}(\mathcal{B})
by
\phi_{*}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{A}}([P])=\iota_{\mathcal{B}}([\phi(P)])
where
\phi(P)\in\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{B})\subset M_{\infty}(\mathcal{B})
is defined by applying
\phi
to the entries of the matrix
P\in M_{p}(\mathcal{A})\subset M_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
. So, from the defining inductive sequence
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\phi_{n,m})\,/\,0\leq n<m\}
of an
AF

C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}=\varinjlim\mathcal{A}_{n}
, we get an inductive sequence
\{(K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{n}),\phi_{n,m\,*})\,/\,0\leq n<m\}
. Then one has
K_{0}(\mathcal{A})=\varinjlim K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{n})

(
Rørdam et al., 2000
, Thm 6.3.2)
.

To compute
\varinjlim K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{n})
, one has to describe the morphisms
\phi_{n,n+1\,*}:K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{n})\to K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{n+1})
. This can be done easily in terms of the multiplicity matrices
A_{n,n+1}
associated to the morphisms
\phi_{n,n+1}:\mathcal{A}_{n}\to\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
. In order to do that, we switch from projections to finitely generated projective modules.

Let
P\in M_{p}(M_{n})
be a projection with
\rank(P)=\alpha\ (\leq pn)
. Then
P
can be diagonalized as
P=U^{*}E_{\alpha}U
for a unitary
U\in M_{pn}
and
E_{\alpha}=\begin{pmatrix}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha}&0\\
0&0\end{pmatrix}\in M_{pn}
where
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha}
is the unit
\alpha\times\alpha
matrix. Then
S\vcentcolon=E_{\alpha}U
satisfies
S^{*}S=(E_{\alpha}U)^{*}(E_{\alpha}U)=U^{*}E_{\alpha}^{*}E_{\alpha}U=U^{*}E_{\alpha}U=P
and
SS^{*}=(E_{\alpha}U)(E_{\alpha}U)^{*}=E_{\alpha}UU^{*}E_{\alpha}^{*}=E_{\alpha}
so that
[P]=[E_{\alpha}]
. Consider the free left
M_{n}
-module
(M_{n})^{p}\simeq M_{pn,n}
(row of
p
copies of
M_{n}
or rectangular matrices
pn\times n
). Then up to the unitary equivalence by
U
(acting on the right on rectangular matrices),
P
defines the submodule
\mathcal{M}_{P}\simeq M_{pn,n}E_{\alpha}\simeq M_{\alpha,n}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{n}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha}
(a finitely generated projective module over
M_{n}
).

In the same way, a class
[P=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}P_{r}]\in\mathcal{D}(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}})
defines a class (modulo isomorphisms) of left (finitely generated projective) modules
[\mathcal{M}_{P}]
with
\mathcal{M}_{P}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{r}}
where
\alpha_{i}=\rank(P_{i})
. Indeed, if
P\in M_{p}(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{p}\otimes M_{n_{i}}
then
P_{i}\in M_{p}\otimes M_{n_{i}}=M_{p}(M_{n_{i}})
and then we are in the previous situation for every
P_{i}
. The map
\phi_{*}:\mathcal{D}(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}})\to\mathcal{D}(\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{i}})
induced by
\phi:\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}\to\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{k}}
with multiplicity matrix
A=(\alpha_{ki})
sends
[P]
to
[Q=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}Q_{k}]
where every entry along
M_{p}
in
Q_{k}\in M_{p}(M_{m_{k}})
contains
\alpha_{ki}
copies, distributed along the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
, of the entry at the same position along
M_{p}
in
P_{i}
. Since the rank of a matrix projection is its trace, one gets
\beta_{k}\vcentcolon=\rank(Q_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{ki}\alpha_{i}
and the associated module is then
\mathcal{N}_{Q}=\mathbb{C}^{m_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathbb{C}^{m_{s}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{s}}
by the previous construction. So, in terms of modules,
\phi_{*}
sends the class of
\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{r}}
to the class of
\mathbb{C}^{m_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathbb{C}^{m_{s}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{s}}
, where
\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{i}}=M_{\alpha_{i},n_{i}}
is repeated
\alpha_{ki}
times on the diagonal of
\mathbb{C}^{m_{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{k}}=M_{\beta_{k},m_{k}}
. The diagonals are filled thanks to the relations
m_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{ki}n_{i}
and
\beta_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{ki}\alpha_{i}
. See
(
Davidson, 1996
, Ex. IV.3.1)
where the identification of
\phi_{*}
with
A
is also presented using projections.

This describes the maps
\phi_{n,n+1\,*}:\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n})\to\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n+1})
in terms of (finitely generated projective) modules. For
AF

C^{*}
-algebras,
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
equals the space
K_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
of stable equivalence classes of projections in
\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
,
2
2

2

P,Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
are stably equivalent,
P\approx Q
, if there is a projection
R\in\mathcal{P}_{\infty}(\mathcal{A})
such that
P\oplus R\sim_{0}Q\oplus R
.
and this is a cone in
K_{0}(\mathcal{A})
such that
K_{0}(\mathcal{A})=K_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{A})-K_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{A})

(
Davidson, 1996
, Thm IV.1.6, Thm IV.2.3, Thm IV.2.4)
. So, for
AF

C^{*}
-algebras, it can be of practical importance to know what it means to approximate elements of
\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})=K_{0}^{+}(\mathcal{A})
. A class
[P]\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
can be looked at as a sequence of classes
[P_{n}]\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A}_{n})
for
n\geq n_{0}
, related step-by-step by the maps
\phi_{n,n+1\,*}
. The sequence
\{[P_{n}]\}_{n\geq n_{0}}
corresponds then to a sequence
\{[\mathcal{M}_{n}]\}_{n\geq n_{0}}
of equivalence classes of isomorphisms of finitely generated projective modules on the algebras
\mathcal{A}_{n}
. Notice that it is only the sequence in the whole that permits to reconstruct the target element
[P]\in\mathcal{D}(\mathcal{A})
. We could say that, for some
n\geq n_{0}
, the module
\mathcal{M}_{n}
“approximates” (as a representative element in
[\mathcal{M}_{n}]
) the class
[P]
, but some information are encoded in the embedding maps
\phi_{n,n+1\,*}:\mathcal{M}_{n}\to\mathcal{M}_{n+1}
which then participate to this notion of approximation. As seen before, concretely, the maps
\phi_{n,n+1\,*}
are written in terms of the multiplicity matrices
A_{n,n+1}
associated to the
\phi_{n,n+1}
.
3
3

3

The full sequence of multiplicity matrices
A_{n,n+1}
is provided by the
AF

C^{*}
-algebra. It can be represented graphically by a Bratteli diagram, and it is known that two
AF

C^{*}
-algebras with the same Bratteli diagram are isomorphic
(
Davidson, 1996
, Prop. III.2.7)

It is well-known (Elliott’s Theorem, see for instance
(
Davidson, 1996
, Thm IV.4.3)
) that the
K_{0}
-group, supplemented with a structure of scaled dimension group, is sufficient to classify
AF

C^{*}
-algebras. So there is no more information outside of the one encoded in the sequence of multiplicity matrices
A_{n,n+1}
to be expected in the constructions described before since it determines a unique
AF

C^{*}
-algebra and it permits to construct its scaled dimension group.
4
4

4

Keep in mind that an
AF

C^{*}
-algebra can be obtained from different sequences of multiplicity matrices.
, and so to go on from the sequence:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{1}\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\phi_{1}}\mathcal{A}_{2}\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{\phi_{2}}\mathcal{A}_{3}\quad...\ \rightsquigarrow\mathcal{A}_{AF}

to the sequence:

\displaystyle K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{1})\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{K_{0}(\phi_{1})}K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{2})\lhook\joinrel\xrightarrow{K_{0}(\phi_{2})}K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{3})\quad...\ \rightsquigarrow K_{0}(\mathcal{A}_{AF}).

In the part
IV
, we will use again these
\phi_{n}
morphisms to link the essential structures of the gauge theories which will be associated to each
\mathcal{A}_{i}
, in order to build inductive sequences of NCGFTs on top AF-algebras.

Chapter 3
Noncommutative Differential Structures

We have seen with the Gelfand Naimark theorem that properties of an algebra can be mapped to topological properties of an underlying space in the commutative case. Therefore, we know how to reconstruct the topological manifold
M
from
\mathcal{A}
, and then obtain the algebra
\mathcal{C}^{0}(M)
of continuous functions on
M
. It should be noted that this connection provides only incomplete information about the geometry of this underlying “space”, and thus for example does not allow one to distinguish between two equivalent topological spaces such as the ones of torus and teacup for example. The additional information required to fully characterize the geometry would be to introduce an object enabling the elaboration of a differential structure (on the algebraic side), and from which one could extract a metric structure (geometric side). It is therefore very important to complete the algebraic/geometric equivalence by finding techniques to define such differential and metric structures, and thus construct the algebra
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
of differentiable functions. Having done this with tools that do not depend on the commutativity of the algebra, the generalization to NC algebras can be started.

The study of differential structure can be seen as the study of variations of what is observed. This can be achieved according to any abstract or concrete parameter like time, space, another observable, or numbers. This can be done infinitesimally or not for the variation of this parameters. In QM, the observational outcomes are given by

O_{a}(\psi)=\langle\psi|a|\psi\rangle
(in the equivalent bra-ket notation). Then if we denote by
\delta O_{a}(\psi)
its variation, they are two equivalent ways to see its origin:

•

The 1st one is to consider that it comes from a variation of the state:
\psi^{\prime}=\psi+\epsilon\delta\psi
which gives at first order
O_{a}(\psi^{\prime})=\langle\psi|a|\psi\rangle+\epsilon(\langle\delta\psi|a|\psi\rangle+\langle\psi|a|\delta\psi\rangle)=O_{a}(\psi)+\epsilon\delta^{\psi}O_{a}(\psi)
if we neglect the terms of order two in
\epsilon
. We can call this the Schrödinger picture view of variations.

•

The 2nd one is to consider that it comes from a variation at the level of the algebra:
a^{\prime}=a+\tilde{\epsilon}\delta a
which give
O_{a^{\prime}}(\psi)=O_{a}(\psi)+\tilde{\epsilon}\langle\psi|\delta a|\psi\rangle=O_{a}(\psi)+\tilde{\epsilon}\delta^{a}O_{a}(\psi)
which can be called in the same way the Heisenberg picture view of variations..

Taking these two variations to be the same:
O_{a}(\psi^{\prime})=O_{a^{\prime}}(\psi)
(via the equivalence between Schrödinger and Heisenberg pictures) which mean that
\delta^{\psi}O_{a}(\psi)=\delta^{a}O_{a}(\psi)=\delta O_{a}(\psi)
, we are left with two (in principle) equivalent ways to study differential structures in this context. As we will see in sections
3.1
and
7.3
, this works also in the classical framework since it can also be describe in the framework of Hilbert spaces an operator algebras.

Different techniques for elaborating differential calculus have been developed, the most general being the universal differential calculus. Differential calculi based on derivations of the algebra, as well as those based on the Dirac operator can be obtained as quotients of this universal structure in favorable cases. Thus the derivation technique can be said to study differential structure directly on
\mathcal{A}
while the spectral triple technique is at the level of operators on
\mathcal{H}
. Then, the two main approaches explored to build NC differential structures in NCG belong respectively to the two categories of differential structures which will be presented below. Indeed in this view, the derivation approach to algebra can be seen as a kind of Heisenberg picture (where only variations of the observables are taken into account), whereas the spectral triplet approach belongs to the Schrödinger picture (variation of the states).

From a mathematical point of view, the derivation approach seems to be more economic since it has the good feature that it can be defined from the algebra itself, without added structures (like the Dirac), and that it considers only the algebra, which is more fundamental than the Hilbert space according to GNS construction. However, strong mathematical motivations have also motivated the spectral triplet approach, whose mathematical and physical developments are quite remarkable. Giving the usual Riemannian geometrical framework and having nice realizations in physics as we will see with the NCSMPP in section
12
.

3.1
Usual Differential Structures, open door to Noncommutative Extension

In what follows I will present these three ways of proceeding. But let us first clarify what we mean by differential structure, in order to feel as much as possible the potential meaning of it’s NC generalization.

Differential calculus
is the study of the rate at which quantities like functions evolve. The main object of differential calculus is the
derivative
, which measures this rate according to the evolution of its argument, which can be a coordinate for example. Using spatial coordinate, the derivative is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{df(x)}{dx}=\lim_{dx\to 0\atop dx\neq 0}\frac{f(x+dx)-f(x)}{dx}

(3.1.1)

The identification of a derivative is called
differentiation
.

Remark 3.1.1
.

In practice, the study of the variation of an observable can only be done by correlating it with another observable, known as the reference observable, such as the pointer of a clock or the graduations of a ruler, it is not intrinsic!

Derivatives are of great importance in physics, and in almost all scientific fields studying quantities and their evolution. For example, velocity is the derivative corresponding to the displacement of a moving body with respect to time, the derivative of velocity is the acceleration, and the derivative of momentum (function of velocity) is equal to the sum of forces acting on the body. Moreover, most of the fundamental equations used to describe phenomenon are differential equations, whose constituents are once again derivatives. As soon as it is a question of the evolution of a quantity, or position, according to certain degrees of freedom of the associated space, derivations associated with these infinitesimal degrees of freedom come into play, this is why differential geometry is of such great importance and universality in physics. More general definitions of derivative, not involving the notion of a limit as in eq.(
3.1.1
) (which becomes limited in the NC framework) can be defined, using the so-called
q
or
h
-calculus, taking
q=\exp(ih)
:

\displaystyle d_{q}(f(x))=f(qx)-f(x)\qquad\text{or}\qquad d_{h}(f(x))=f(x+h)-f(x)

When
h\to 0
or equivalently
q\to 1
, these formulas become equivalent to the usual derivative
3.1.1
.

Taking a derivative, we can construct the corresponding
differential
:

\displaystyle df(x,dx)=\frac{df(x)}{dx}dx

(3.1.2)

If
f
is a real differentiable function, its differential
df
is a differential 1-form (called exact) which at each point
x
is the linear form
df(x)
. Locally, the differential 1-forms are expressed as combinations of function differentials.

As mentioned before, we can express all these results in the world of operator algebras and Hilbert spaces. Taking the differential over
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, the Hilbert space can be defined as the completion in the
L^{2}
norm of the module of 1-forms (considered as vector space here). The derivative
d
can then be considered as an operator on this Hilbert space (densely defined) such that
\forall\psi\in\mathcal{H}
and
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
:

\displaystyle(df)\psi=[d,f]\psi=d(f\cdot\psi)-f\cdot(d\psi)\qquad\qquad\text{Heisenberg picture}

\displaystyle f.(d\psi)=d(f\cdot\psi)-(df)\psi\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{Schrodinger picture}

With this way of expressing things, with commutators in the framework of operators algebras, we are therefore able to explore the NC framework extension, adopting more general notions, when needed.

A
derivation
is a linear map over an algebra that generalizes the derivative operator, but this time on the algebraic side. Taking an algebra
\mathcal{A}
, a derivation is defined to be a linear map
\mathfrak{X}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
which respects the Leibniz’s rule:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}(ab)=a\mathfrak{X}(b)+\mathfrak{X}(a)b

For example, partial and Lie derivatives with respect to a vector field are derivations over algebras on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
or a differentiable manifold. This formula can be deduced from this simple argument, if we take
\mathfrak{X}(a)
and
\mathfrak{X}(b)
as representing small variations of
a
and
b
, then the variation
\mathfrak{X}(ab)
can be written:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}(ab)=(a+\mathfrak{X}(a))(b+\mathfrak{X}(b))-ab=\mathfrak{X}(a)b+a\mathfrak{X}(b)

because
\mathfrak{X}(a)\mathfrak{X}(b)
is negligible since we consider small variations. As we will see, this notion is of great importance to extend the usual derivative to the NCG framework, partly because Leibniz rule still makes sense on NC algebras the novelty being that because of now
[a,\mathfrak{X}(b)]\neq 0
, then:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}([a,b])=a\mathfrak{X}(b)+\mathfrak{X}(a)b-\mathfrak{X}(b)a-b\mathfrak{X}(a)\neq 0

Differential calculus can therefore also be constructed on the basis of such derivations.

It is important to realize that derivations on the algebraic side can be viewed to be equivalent to tangent spaces on the geometric side. Indeed, directional derivatives can be defined as follow: if we take a point
x\in M
, and a smooth curve
\gamma:\mathbb{R}\,\to\,M
parameterized by
t
such that
\gamma(0)=x
, therefore, taking any
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
we have

\displaystyle\frac{df(\gamma(t))}{dt}=\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}\frac{df(\gamma(t))}{d\gamma(t)}\qquad\qquad\text{at}\qquad t=0

Where, by comparison to eq.(
3.1.2
), the derivation
\frac{df(x)}{dx}
can be identified to
\frac{df(\gamma(t))}{d\gamma(t)}
and the infinitesimal displacement
dx
to
\frac{d\gamma(t)}{dt}
. Therefore, for a given
x
, taking the set of all derivations
\frac{df(\gamma(t))}{dt}
(at
t=0
) corresponding to all curves
\gamma
gives the tangent space
T_{x}M
. This is interesting since, we can see that in this way, the differential structure on the algebraic side contains the tangent space which is an essential geometric structure. These tangent spaces being at the hearth of most of the natural geometric constructions. This is why derivations can offer the algebraic equivalent of tangent spaces, “styling” the points provided by pure states through the Gelfand Naimark theorem, and fulfilling the algebraic to geometric correspondence. In a more general context than commutative algebras, some derivations will not correspond to directional derivatives in the usual meaning, but to derivations along inner degrees of freedom.

Furthermore, derivations can be viewed as infinitesimal generators of the automorphisms in the algebra, as discussed in
(
Sakai, 1991
)
, and later in section
3.3
, taking an algebra
\mathcal{A}
, and
\phi_{t}\,:\,\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
an automorphism with parameter
t
such that
\phi_{0}={\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}}
and naturally
\phi_{t}(ab)=\phi_{t}(a)\phi_{t}(b)
, then the map
a\,\to\,\frac{d}{dt}|_{t=0}\phi_{t}(a)
is a derivation. You can therefore think of derivations as a kind of vector fields, linked to the potential infinitesimal displacements which are allowed in the geometry. In the context of differential geometry (then general relativity), it is possible to show that diffeomorphisms of
M
are equivalent to automorphisms of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, therefore derivations can be seen to be in relation with the set of infinitesimal displacements generating these diffeomorphisms. As we will see in chapter
11
automorphisms of the algebra can be seen as linked with the symmetries of a theory, and are of great importance in the framework of Gauge field theories since they will correspond to gauge transformations.

An important structure can be built from the algebra and its derivations, that of the differential calculus. Let
\mathcal{A}
be an unital associative algebra, a differential calculus on
\mathcal{A}
is a graded differential algebra
(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}})
, with
\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})=\toplus_{n\geq 0}\Omega^{n}(\mathcal{A})
,
\Omega^{0}=\mathcal{A}
and by definition
\text{{d}}:\Omega^{\bullet}\,\to\,\Omega^{\bullet+1}
. The derivation must satisfy
\text{{d}}^{2}=0
together with the graded Leibniz rule:

\displaystyle\text{{d}}(\omega\eta)=(\text{{d}}\omega)\eta+(-1)^{n}\omega(\text{{d}}\eta)

for all
\omega\in\Omega^{n}
and
\eta\in\Omega^{m}
. A direct consequence is that
\text{{d}}{\text{\char 49\relax}}=0
. The space
\Omega^{n}
is called the space of non-commutative
n
-forms, which is a
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule. In general, every element of
\Omega^{n}
can be expressed as a combination of elements
a_{0}\text{{d}}a_{1}\dots\text{{d}}a_{n}
, and we have:

\displaystyle\text{{d}}(a_{0}\text{{d}}a_{1}\dots\text{{d}}a_{n})=\text{{d}}a_{0}\text{{d}}a_{1}\dots\text{{d}}a_{n}.

There are many possibilities to make such a differential calculus on
\mathcal{A}
, these possibilities are distinguished by the nature of the chosen derivation. In section
3.2
, I will present the most general of them, based on the differential algebra of universal forms
\Omega_{U}(\mathcal{A})

(
Coquereaux, 1998
)
. Then in sections
3.3
and
3.4
I will present the methods which use the derivations of the algebra
\Omega_{\Der}(\mathcal{A})
, and the one which uses the spectral triples
\Omega_{D}(\mathcal{A})
, both being obtained as a quotient of
\Omega_{U}(\mathcal{A})
. More details can be found in
(
Müller-Hoissen, 1998
)
,
(
François et al., 2014
)
,
(
Gracia-Bondía et al., 2013
)
and
(
Masson, 2001
)
.

3.2
Universal Differential Structures

The universal differential calculus can be seen as the more general and abstract way to look at differential structures. In the following, we will need a convenient presentation of the differential graded algebra
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}}_{U})
. We follow the presentation in
(
Masson, 1995
)
and
(
Gracia-Bondía et al., 2013
)
.
1
1

1

We owe this presentation to Thierry Masson who owed this presentation to Michel Dubois-Violette.

Taking an associative algebra
\mathcal{A}
, we define the tensor algebra of degree
n
on
\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\vcentcolon=\underbrace{\mathcal{A}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}}_{n+1\text{ times }}=\mathcal{A}^{\otimes^{n+1}}

with
\mathcal{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}
. We define
\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}
by
\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
. These are bimodules on
\mathcal{A}
and a graded algebra for the product
\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{T}^{n^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{T}^{n+n^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}
defined by
(a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n})(a^{\prime 0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{\prime n^{\prime}})\vcentcolon=a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}a^{\prime 0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{\prime n^{\prime}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
is a bimodule over
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}
. Define
\text{{d}}_{U}:\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{T}^{n+1}\mathcal{A}
as

\displaystyle\text{{d}}_{U}(a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n})=

\displaystyle{\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}

\displaystyle+\tsum_{p=1}^{n}(-1)^{p}a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{p-1}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes a^{p}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}

\displaystyle+(-1)^{n+1}a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}.

Then
\text{{d}}_{U}
is a derivation of degree
1
on the graded algebra
\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
such that
\text{{d}}_{U}^{2}=0
. Notice that
\text{{d}}_{U}(a)={\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes a-a\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}
on
\mathcal{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}
. It satisfies the Leibniz rule since:

\displaystyle\text{{d}}_{U}(ab)={\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes ab-ab\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}={\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes ab-a\otimes b+a\otimes b-ab\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\text{{d}}_{U}(a)b+a\text{{d}}_{U}(b).

Let first see how this relates to derivations on the algebra. A derivation
\mathfrak{X}
of an algebra
\mathcal{A}
into a bimodule
\mathcal{M}
, is a linear map satisfying the Leibniz rule.

Proposition 3.2.1
.

For any derivation
\mathfrak{X}
of
\mathcal{A}
, in a bimodule
\mathcal{M}
, there is a unique homomorphism of bimodules:

\displaystyle h_{\mathfrak{X}}\,:\,\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\,\to\,\mathcal{M}

such that
\mathfrak{X}=h_{\mathfrak{X}}\circ\text{{d}}_{U}
.

Proof

Taking
h_{\mathfrak{X}}(\text{{d}}_{U}a)=\mathfrak{X}(a)
, as
\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
is generated by the
\text{{d}}_{U}a
taking all
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we see that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}(ab)=h_{\mathfrak{X}}(\text{{d}}_{U}(ab))=h_{\mathfrak{X}}(\text{{d}}_{U}(a))h_{\mathfrak{X}}(b)+h_{\mathfrak{X}}(a)h_{\mathfrak{X}}(\text{{d}}_{U}(b))=\mathfrak{X}(a)h_{\mathfrak{X}}(b)+h_{\mathfrak{X}}(a)\mathfrak{X}(b).

are compatible structures iff
\forall a\in\mathcal{A}
we have
h_{\mathfrak{X}}(a)=a
, this requirement inducing the unicity of this homomorphism.

It is convenient to introduce the maps
i^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}(a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n})\vcentcolon=a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{p-1}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes a^{p}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}
for any
p=0,\dots,n+1
, with the convention that for
p=0
, the tensor factor
1
is added before
a^{0}
(for
p=n+1
, it is added after
a^{n}
). Then
\text{{d}}_{U}=\tsum_{p=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{p}\,i^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}:\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{T}^{n+1}\mathcal{A}
.

Let
\mu:\mathcal{T}^{1}\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}
be the multiplication map
a^{0}\otimes a^{1}\mapsto a^{0}a^{1}
, and define
\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon=\ker\mu\subset\mathcal{T}^{1}\mathcal{A}
. Then
\text{{d}}_{U}
maps
\mathcal{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}
into
\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
and
\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
is generated, as a bimodule on
\mathcal{A}
, by the
\text{{d}}_{U}a
’s for
a\in\mathcal{A}
:
2
2

2

If
\tsum_{i}a_{i}^{0}\otimes a_{i}^{1}\in\mathcal{T}^{1}\mathcal{A}
is such that
\mu(\tsum_{i}a_{i}^{0}\otimes a_{i}^{1})=\tsum_{i}a_{i}^{0}a_{i}^{1}=0
, then
\tsum_{i}a_{i}^{0}\otimes a_{i}^{1}=\tsum_{i}a_{i}^{0}({\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes a_{i}^{1}-a_{i}^{1}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}})=\tsum_{i}a_{i}^{0}\text{{d}}_{U}a_{i}^{1}
.

\displaystyle a\text{{d}}_{U}b=a\otimes b-ab\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}\in\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}^{\otimes^{2}}

We can check that this belongs to
\ker\mu
:
\mu(a\text{{d}}_{U}b)=ab-ab=0
. Then, elements in
\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
will take the form:

\displaystyle a\text{{d}}_{U}b_{1}\text{{d}}_{U}b_{2}\dots\text{{d}}_{U}b_{n}\,\in\,\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{A}^{\otimes^{n}}.

These definitions are very abstract and give the feeling that many intuitions that we usually associate with derivations are lost. Let’s see what this can represent in the case of commutative algebras, in order to get an idea of what this universal differential calculus actually means.
Let’s take
\mathcal{A}
to be a commutative algebra. Since the elements of
\mathcal{A}
can be represented as functions on
M
, the element of
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}
are of the form
c(x)\otimes d(y)
. Then
a\text{{d}}_{U}b
can be written as:

\displaystyle[a\text{{d}}_{U}b](x,y)=a(x)\otimes b(y)-a(x)b(x)\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}(y)

We see that
\mu([a\text{{d}}_{U}b](x,y))=a(x)(b(x)-b(y))
is equal to
0
if
x=y
. When
M
is a differentiable manifold, we recognize the usual differential
[\text{{d}}_{U}b](x,y)=b(x)-b(y)
, taking the limit
y\to x
. we recover the usual differential one-form of equation (
3.1.2
):

\displaystyle\text{{d}}b(x,dx)=\frac{\text{{d}}b(x)}{dx}dx\qquad\qquad\text{with}\qquad\qquad\text{{d}}x=y-x

As mentioned in
(
Coquereaux, 1998
)
, when considering general
\text{{d}}x
, this can be seen as a non local differential calculus.

Let come back to general associative algebras, taking
\Omega^{0}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon=\mathcal{A}
and
\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon=\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\cdots\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
(
n
times tensor product over
\mathcal{A}
) for any
n\geq 2
and
\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon=\toplus_{n\geq 0}\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
. Equivalently,
\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
is the graded sub-algebra of
\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
generated by
\Omega^{0}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
and
\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
. One can then check that
\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
is generated by the
a^{0}\text{{d}}_{U}a^{1}\cdots\text{{d}}_{U}a^{n}
for
a^{0},\dots,a^{n}\in\mathcal{A}
, so that
\text{{d}}_{U}
restricts to maps
\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\to\Omega^{n+1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
, and then
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}}_{U})
is a graded differential sub-algebra of
(\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A},\text{{d}}_{U})
.

Let us consider the case
\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{A}_{i}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{i}
are unital algebras with units
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
. It will be useful for future discussions to use explicit presentations of
(\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A},\text{{d}}_{U})
and
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}}_{U})
constructed as follows. Let

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}\vcentcolon=\left\{\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a_{1}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&a_{2}&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&&\ddots&\\
0&0&\cdots&a_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\mid a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

For any
n\geq 1
and any
1\leq i_{0},\dots,i_{n}\leq r
, let us introduce the notation
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{i_{0},\dots,i_{n}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{A}_{i_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathcal{A}_{i_{n}}
. Now, let
\mathfrak{T}^{n}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}\mathcal{A}
be the set of matrices with entries in
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{i,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},j}
at row
i
and column
j
. This can be schematically visualized as

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{1,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},1}&\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{1,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},2}&\cdots&\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{1,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},r}\\
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{2,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},1}&\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{2,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},2}&\cdots&\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{2,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},r}\\
\vdots&\vdots&&\vdots\\
\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{r,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},1}&\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{r,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},2}&\cdots&\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{r,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},r}\end{pmatrix}

where the first and last algebras in the tensor products will play a crucial role in the following. Combining the products

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{i,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},k}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{k,j_{1},\dots,j_{n^{\prime}-1},j}\to\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{i,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},k,j_{1},\dots,j_{n^{\prime}-1},j},

defined by the product in
\mathcal{A}_{k}
, and the usual rules for matrix multiplications, one gets products

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}^{n}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathfrak{T}^{n^{\prime}}_{j_{1},\dots,j_{n^{\prime}-1}}\mathcal{A}\to\toplus_{k=1}^{r}\mathfrak{T}^{n+n^{\prime}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},k,j_{1},\dots,j_{n^{\prime}-1}}\mathcal{A}.

Let us introduce

\displaystyle\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\vcentcolon=\toplus_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}=1}^{r}\mathfrak{T}^{n}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}\mathcal{A}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}\vcentcolon=\toplus_{n\geq 0}\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}

then
\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
is a graded algebra for the global product induced by the products defined above. Explicitly, for
\toplus_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}=1}^{r}\big(a^{0}_{i_{0}}\otimes a^{1}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n-1}_{i_{n-1}}\otimes a^{n}_{i_{n}}\big)_{i_{0},i_{n}=1}^{r}\in\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
and
\toplus_{j_{1},\dots,j_{n^{\prime}-1}=1}^{r}\big(b^{0}_{j_{0}}\otimes b^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes b^{n^{\prime}-1}_{j_{n^{\prime}-1}}\otimes b^{n^{\prime}}_{j_{n^{\prime}}}\big)_{j_{0},j_{n^{\prime}}=1}^{r}\in\mathfrak{T}^{n^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}
, their product in
\mathfrak{T}^{n+n^{\prime}}\mathcal{A}
is

\displaystyle\toplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},i_{n},\\
j_{1},\dots,j_{n^{\prime}-1}=1\end{subarray}}^{r}\big(a^{0}_{i}\otimes a^{1}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n-1}_{i_{n-1}}\otimes a^{n}_{i_{n}}b^{0}_{i_{n}}\otimes b^{1}_{j_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes b^{n^{\prime}-1}_{j_{n^{\prime}-1}}\otimes b^{n^{\prime}}_{j}\big)_{i,j=1}^{r}

(3.2.1)

Let
\bm{\mu}
be the component-wise product on
\mathfrak{T}^{1}\mathcal{A}
. Since multiplications by elements in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\mathcal{A}_{j}
are zero for
i\neq j
, the resulting matrix is diagonal, and so one gets a natural map
\bm{\mu}:\mathfrak{T}^{1}\mathcal{A}\to\mathfrak{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}
. Let
\mathbf{\Omega}^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon=\ker\bm{\mu}\subset\mathfrak{T}^{1}\mathcal{A}
and
\mathbf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
be the graded sub-algebra generated by
\mathbf{\Omega}^{0}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}
and
\mathbf{\Omega}^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
. For any
p=0,\dots,n+1
, define
\mathbf{i}^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}:\mathfrak{T}^{n}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}\mathcal{A}\to\toplus_{k=0}^{r}\mathfrak{T}^{n+1}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p-1},k,i_{p},\dots,i_{n-1}}\mathcal{A}
by inserting
{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\toplus_{k=1}^{r}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}
component-wise,
i.e.

\mathbf{i}^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}=\toplus_{k=1}^{r}\mathbf{i}^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{k}}}
with obvious notations. Then one can define
\text{{d}}_{U}\vcentcolon=\tsum_{p=0}^{n+1}(-1)^{p}\,\mathbf{i}^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}:\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\to\mathfrak{T}^{n+1}\mathcal{A}
.

Proposition 3.2.2
.

The map
\text{{d}}_{U}
is a differential on
\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
and there is an isomorphism
t:\mathcal{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}\to\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
of graded differential algebras which induces an isomorphism of the graded differential (sub)algebras
\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathbf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
.

Proof

For
n=0
, one defines
t(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i})=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}a_{1}&\cdots&0\\
&\ddots&\\
0&\cdots&a_{r}\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\mathfrak{T}^{0}\mathcal{A}
for any
\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
. For
n\geq 1
, consider any
a^{0}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}\in\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
with
a^{p}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a^{p}_{i}
where
a^{p}_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
. Expanding the tensor products along these direct sums, one gets a sum of terms of the form
a^{0}_{i_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}_{i_{n}}\in\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{i_{0},\dots,i_{n}}
that we assemble as elements in
\mathfrak{T}^{n}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}}\mathcal{A}
. This defines the map
t:\mathcal{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\to\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
, which, for any
n\geq 0
, is by construction an isomorphism of vector spaces. A straightforward computation shows that the product on
\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
is such that
t
is a morphism of graded algebras.

By construction of
\mathbf{i}^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}
, one has
t\circ i^{p}_{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\mathbf{i}^{p}_{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}\circ t
, so that
\text{{d}}_{U}
is a differential on
\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}
and
t
is an isomorphism of differential algebras.

Finally, the map
\bm{\mu}
has been defined such that
t\circ\mu=\bm{\mu}\circ t
so that
t
identifies
\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
with
\mathbf{\Omega}^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
, and so
\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
with
\mathbf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
.

Notice that, with
\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}\vcentcolon=t({\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}})\in\mathfrak{T}^{1}\mathcal{A}
, one has
\text{{d}}_{U}t(a)=[\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}},t(a)]
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

3.3
Derivation-based Differential Structures

The derivation-based differential calculus was defined by Dubois-Violette in
(
Dubois-Violette, 1988
)
and studied for various algebras, by Dubois-Violette, Kerner, Madore, Masson, Michor, Vitale, de Goursac, Wallet, Wulkenhaar, see for instance
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990a
;
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
;
Masson, 1996
;
Dubois-Violette and Masson, 1998
;
Masson, 1999
;
Dubois-Violette and Michor, 1994
;
Dubois-Violette and Michor, 1996
;
Dubois-Violette and Michor, 1997
;
Cagnache et al., 2011a
;
de Goursac et al., 2007
;
de Goursac et al., 2008
;
Martinetti et al., 2013
)
. Some reviews can also be found in
(
Dubois-Violette, 2001
;
Masson, 2008b
;
Masson, 2008a
;
Masson, 2008c
)
. The main ingredient is the space of derivations on an associative algebra on which a natural differential calculus can be based.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an associative algebra with unit
1
, and let
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
be its center. The space of derivations of
\mathcal{A}
is

\Der(\mathcal{A})=\{\mathfrak{X}:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}\ /\ \mathfrak{X}\text{ linear},\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}(ab)=(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a)b+a(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}b),\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}\}.

This vector space is a Lie algebra for the bracket
[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]a=\mathfrak{X}\mathfrak{Y}\mathord{\cdot}a-\mathfrak{Y}\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a
for all
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
, and a
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-module for the product
(f\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a=f(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a)
for all
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
. The subspace

\Int(\mathcal{A})=\{\ad_{a}:b\mapsto[a,b]\ /\ a\in\mathcal{A}\}\subset\Der(\mathcal{A})

is called the vector space of inner derivations: it is a Lie ideal and a
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-submodule. The fact that the map
\mathcal{A}\,\to\,\Int(\mathcal{A})
defined by
a\,\to\,\ad_{a}
is surjective with kernel
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
implies that
\Int(\mathcal{A})
can take the corresponding form:

\displaystyle\Int(\mathcal{A})\simeq\mathcal{A}/\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}).

(3.3.1)

Taking any
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
we have
[\mathfrak{X},\ad_{a}]=\ad_{\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a}
, then
\Int(\mathcal{A})
is an ideal of
\Der(\mathcal{A})
and so,
\Der(\mathcal{A})/\Int(\mathcal{A})
is also a Lie algebra. This define the subspace of Outer derivations with the quotient
\Out(\mathcal{A})=\Der(\mathcal{A})/\Int(\mathcal{A})
. Then there is a short exact sequence of Lie algebras and
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-modules:

\displaystyle 0\,\longrightarrow\,\Int(\mathcal{A})\,\longrightarrow\,\Der(\mathcal{A})\,\longrightarrow\,\Out(\mathcal{A})\,\longrightarrow\,0

(3.3.2)

Commutative algebras only have outer derivations.

Suppose that
\mathcal{A}
has an involution
a\mapsto a^{*}
. Then a real derivation on
\mathcal{A}
is a derivation
\mathfrak{X}
such that
(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a)^{*}=\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a^{*}
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

Let
\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
be the vector space of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-multilinear antisymmetric maps from
\Der(\mathcal{A})^{p}
to
\mathcal{A}
, with
\Omega_{\Der}^{0}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}
. Then the total space

\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})=\toplus_{p\geq 0}\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})

gets a structure of
\mathbb{N}
-graded differential algebra for the product

\displaystyle(\omega\wedge\eta)(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p+q})\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{p!q!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+q}}(-1)^{\lvert\sigma\rvert}\omega(\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma(1)},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma(p)})\eta(\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma(p+1)},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{\sigma(p+q)})

for any
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
, any
\eta\in\Omega_{\Der}^{q}(\mathcal{A})
and any
\mathfrak{X}_{i}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
where
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is the group of permutations of
n
elements. A differential
d
is defined by the so-called Koszul formula

\displaystyle\text{{d}}\omega(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p+1})\vcentcolon=\sum_{i=1}^{p+1}(-1)^{i+1}\mathfrak{X}_{i}\mathord{\cdot}\omega(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{i}}}}}\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p+1})+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq p+1}(-1)^{i+j}\omega([\mathfrak{X}_{i},\mathfrak{X}_{j}],\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{i}}}}}\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{j}}}}}\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p+1}).

This makes
(\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}})
a graded differential algebra.

Let us take the more intuitive commutative algebra
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, we have that
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
. We can see that the algebra of derivations correspond to
\Gamma(M)
the Lie algebra of smooth vector fields on
M
such that
\Der(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))=\Gamma(M)=\Out(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))
since
\Int(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))=0
. In this case, we recover the graded differential algebra of de Rham forms on
M
:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))=\Omega^{\bullet}(M)

This shows how natural is this construction.

Proposition 3.3.1
(Transport of forms by automorphisms).

Let
\Psi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}
be an algebra automorphism. Then
\Psi
induces an automorphism on
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
.

The map
\Psi_{\Der}:\Der(\mathcal{A})\to\Der(\mathcal{A})
defined by
\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a\vcentcolon=\Psi(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(a))
for any
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
, is an automorphism of the Lie algebra
\Der(\mathcal{A})
and
\Psi_{\Der}(f\mathfrak{X})=\Psi(f)\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})
for any
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
(so
\Psi_{\Der}
is not necessary an automorphism for the structure of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-module). For inner derivations, one has
\Psi_{\Der}(\ad_{a})=\ad_{\Psi(a)}
.

The maps
\Psi:\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})\to\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
defined by

\displaystyle\Psi(\omega)(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p})\vcentcolon=\Psi\left(\omega(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}_{1}),\dots,\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}_{p}))\right)

for any
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathfrak{X}_{i}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
, defines an automorphism of the graded differential algebra
(\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}})
.

For
p=0
,
\Psi
defined on
\Omega_{\Der}^{0}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{A}
is exactly the original automorphism
\Psi
of
\mathcal{A}
, so that the notation is justified.

Proof

For any
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
, one has
\Psi(f)a=\Psi(f\Psi^{-1}(a))=\Psi(\Psi^{-1}(a)f)=a\Psi(f)
so that
\Psi(f)\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
.

With obvious notations, one has

\displaystyle\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}(ab)

\displaystyle=\Psi\left(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(ab)\right)=\Psi\left(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}(\Psi^{-1}(a)\Psi^{-1}(b))\right)=\Psi\left(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(a)\right)b+a\Psi\left(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(b)\right)

\displaystyle=(\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a)b+a(\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}b)

so that
\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})
is a derivation.

In the same way, one has
\Psi_{\Der}(f\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a=\Psi(f(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(a)))=\Psi(f)\Psi(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(a))=\Psi(f)\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a
.

For
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
, one has

\displaystyle\Psi_{\Der}([\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}])\mathord{\cdot}a

\displaystyle=\Psi(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}(\mathfrak{Y}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(a)))-\Psi(\mathfrak{Y}\mathord{\cdot}(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(a)))=\Psi(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{Y})\mathord{\cdot}a))-\Psi(\mathfrak{Y}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a))

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}(\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{Y})\mathord{\cdot}a)-\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{Y})\mathord{\cdot}(\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a)=[\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X}),\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{Y})]\mathord{\cdot}a

so that
\Psi_{\Der}([\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}])=[\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X}),\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{Y})]
. The inverse
\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}
is defined by
\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a\vcentcolon=\Psi^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi(a))
as it can be easily checked. For inner derivations, one has
\Psi_{\Der}(\ad_{a})\mathord{\cdot}b=\Psi([a,\Psi^{-1}(b)])=[\Psi(a),b]=\ad_{\Psi(a)}\mathord{\cdot}b
.

For any
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
, it is easy to check that
\Psi(\omega)
is a
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-multilinear antisymmetric map from
\Der(\mathcal{A})^{p}
to
\mathcal{A}
. For any
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
and any
\eta\in\Omega_{\Der}^{q}(\mathcal{A})
, the relation
\Psi(\omega)\wedge\Psi(\eta)=\Psi(\omega\wedge\eta)
is a direct consequence of the definition of
\Psi
on forms. The proof of
\Psi(\text{{d}}a)=\text{{d}}\Psi(a)
is a straightforward computation:
\Psi(\text{{d}}a)(\mathfrak{X})=\Psi(\text{{d}}a(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})))=\Psi(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}a)=\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi(a)
on the one hand and
(\text{{d}}\Psi(a))(\mathfrak{X})=\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi(a)
on the other hand. To prove
\text{{d}}\Psi(\omega)=\Psi(\text{{d}}\omega)
for
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
, one has to use a similar computation and the fact that
\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}
is a morphism of Lie algebras.

Example 3.3.2
(Transport of derivations by inner automorphisms).

Let
u\in\mathcal{A}
be an invertible element (one can take
u
to be unitary when
\mathcal{A}
has an involution). The map
\Psi(a)\vcentcolon=uau^{-1}
defines an automorphism of
\mathcal{A}
and a simple computation shows that
\Psi_{\Der}(\mathfrak{X})=\mathfrak{X}+\ad_{u(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}u^{-1})}
for any
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
. In particular, if
\mathfrak{X}=\ad_{a}
is an inner derivation, then
\Psi_{\Der}(\ad_{a})=\ad_{uau^{-1}}=\ad_{\Psi(a)}
as expected. Notice also that
\Psi(f)=f
for any
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
so that
\Psi_{\Der}:\Der(\mathcal{A})\to\Der(\mathcal{A})
is an automorphism of
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-module in that case.

\Diamond

3.4
Dirac-based Differential Structures

The notion of Spectral triple, elaborated by A. Connes, mainly motivated by the Atiyah-Singer index theorem, is a set of data that permits to encode algebraically and analytically all geometric features. In this subsection, we recall some main facts about the construction of differential structures using spectral triples. We refer to
(
Connes and Marcolli, 2008
;
van Suijlekom, 2015
;
Masson, 2012
)
for further details.

The spectral triple way to construct differential structure consists in adding an operator
D
to the couple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H})
, this is a differential operator called Dirac operator in analogy with the Dirac operator introduced by P. Dirac to make the Schrödinger equation consistent with relativity. It can also be viewed as the square root of the Laplacian, the inverse line element
D=ds^{-1}
, and contain the data of the metric.

Definition 3.4.1
(Spectral triple).

A Spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D)
is the data of an involutive unital algebra

\mathcal{A}
represented by bounded operators on a Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
, and of a self-adjoint operator
D
acting on
\mathcal{H}
such that the resolvent
(i+D^{2})^{-1}
is compact and that for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
[D,a]

is a bounded operator.

Let
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D)
be a spectral triple and denote by
\pi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
the representation on the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
. This makes
\mathcal{H}
a left
\mathcal{A}
-module.

An even spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D,\gamma)
is equipped with a
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-grading linear map
\gamma
on
\mathcal{H}
such that
\gamma^{\dagger}=\gamma
,
\gamma^{2}=1
,
\gamma D+D\gamma=0
(
D
is odd),
\gamma\pi(a)=\pi(a)\gamma
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
(
\mathcal{A}
is even). The grading
\gamma
induces a decomposition
\mathcal{H}=\mathcal{H}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{H}^{-}
according to the eigenvalues
\pm 1
of
\gamma
. Spectral triples without such a grading are referred to as odd spectral triples.

A real spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D,J)
is equipped with a map
J:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
which is an anti-unitary operator:
\langle J\psi_{1},J\psi_{2}\rangle=\langle\psi_{2},\psi_{1}\rangle
for any
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in\mathcal{H}
such that
\forall a,b\in\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle[a,Jb^{*}J^{-1}]=0

commutant property

\displaystyle[[D,a],Jb^{\ast}J^{-1}]=0

first-order condition.

The map
\mathcal{H}\times\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{H}
defined by
(\xi,a)\mapsto Ja^{\ast}J^{-1}\xi
defines a right module structure on
\mathcal{H}
so that
\mathcal{H}
is a
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule. We denote by
a^{\circ}
the element in the opposite algebra
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
which corresponds to
a\in\mathcal{A}
(
\mathcal{A}\simeq\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
as vector spaces by the formal map
\mathcal{A}\ni a\mapsto a^{\circ}\in\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
and the new product in
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
is
a^{\circ}b^{\circ}\vcentcolon=(ba)^{\circ}
). Then, using
a^{\circ}\mapsto Ja^{\ast}J^{-1}
as a left representation of
\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
,
\mathcal{H}
becomes a left
\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
-module. We will frequently write
a^{\circ}\psi=Ja^{\ast}J^{-1}\psi=\psi a
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{H}
. We define
\mathcal{A}^{e}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{A}\otimes\mathcal{A}^{\circ}
. An even real spectral triple is an uplet
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D,J,\gamma)
with
\gamma
as before. Notice then that
\gamma a^{\circ}=a^{\circ}\gamma
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, and so
\gamma
commutes with the left representation of
\mathcal{A}^{e}
on
\mathcal{H}
.

In the odd and even cases, the
KO
-dimensions
n\mod 8
are given in Table
3.1
, where the numbers
\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=\pm 1
are defined by the requirements:

\displaystyle J^{2}=\epsilon\qquad\qquad JD=\epsilon^{\prime}DJ\qquad\qquad J\gamma=\epsilon^{\prime\prime}\gamma J

The last requirement holding only in the even case.

n

0

1

2

3

4

5

6

7

\epsilon

1

1

-1

-1

-1

-1

1

1

\epsilon^{\prime}

1

-1

1

1

1

-1

1

1

\epsilon^{\prime\prime}

1

-1

1

-1

Table 3.1:
KO
-dimensions of real spectral triples.

Remark 3.4.2
.

When
J^{2}=-1
and
\mathcal{H}
is finite dimensional, the dimension of the spectral triple is even (see
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
, Lemma 3.8)
for instance).

Given a finite spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D)
, the
\mathcal{A}
-bimodule of Connes’s differential 1-forms is given by

\displaystyle\Omega_{D}^{1}(\mathcal{A}):=\left\{\sum_{k}a_{k}\left[D,b_{k}\right]:a_{k},b_{k}\in\mathcal{A}\right\}.

(3.4.1)

Thus, we can define the differential as the map
d:\mathcal{A}\to\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{A})
defined by
d(.)=\left[D,.\right]
and
d^{2}\neq 0
.

Remark 3.4.3
.

This definition connects with the usual differential presented in section
3.1
, if we take the usual Dirac operator
D_{M}=i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
,
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
and
\psi
in the corresponding Hilbert space, then:

\displaystyle[D_{M},f]\psi=D_{M}(f.\psi)-f.(D_{M}\psi)=i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}(f).\psi

We can see that even if the Dirac operator acts on the Hilbert space, it is equivalent to an action on the algebra (through the action on the Hilbert space acted on by the algebra), and therefore equivalent to an Heisenberg picture of the differential structure.

Therefore, as mentioned in the end of section
3.2
, we can construct the exterior algebra of differential forms associated to the Dirac operator using the corresponding representation
\pi_{D}\,:\,\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\,\to\,\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
of the universal differential algebra:

\displaystyle\pi_{D}(a\text{{d}}_{U}b_{1}\text{{d}}_{U}b_{2}\dots\text{{d}}_{U}b_{n})=a[D,b_{1}][D,b_{2}]\dots[D,b_{n}]\qquad\qquad a,b_{1},b_{2},\dots b_{n}\,\in\,\mathcal{A}

The Dirac operators gives a geometric structure to
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D)
, allowing to reconstruct lengths between states. Indeed, taking two states
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
with
\psi_{(1,2)}:\,\mathcal{A}\,\to\,\mathbb{C}
, we can define the distance between these two states:

\displaystyle d(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=sup\{|\psi_{1}(a)-\psi_{2}(a)|;\,a\,\in\,\mathcal{A},\,\,\,||[D,a]||\leq 1\}

If we consider the commutative algebra
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, and the usual Dirac operator
D_{M}=i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
, a pure state leads to the notion of points trough Gelfand Naimark theorem
\psi_{1,2}\,\to\,x_{1,2}\in M
, then we recover the usual distance corresponding to the metric
g
on
M
:

\displaystyle d_{g}(x_{1},x_{2})=\sup\{|f(x_{1})-f(x_{2})|;\,f\,\in\,\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M),\,\,\,||[D_{M},f]||\leq 1\}.

The link between
\hat{x}(f)
and
f(x)
being given by the Gelfand transform eq.(
1.2.1
). The proof of this can be found in
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
[page
\sim
66].The link between the Dirac operator and the metric is given by the gamma matrices trough
\{\gamma^{\mu},\gamma^{\nu}\}=2g^{\mu\nu}
. This illustrates how the slope of functions and the distance are linked trough the Dirac operator.

Chapter 4
Differential Structure on Matrix Algebras

In this chapter, we will make the differential structures presented in chapter
3
more “concrete" by showing how they find realization on finite algebras in matrix representations, the first case being
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and the second
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}M_{n_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
. This will be done only for derivation and spectral triples based differential calculus since it has already been done in a general way for universal differential calculus in section
3.2
. This is a very important chapter since as mentioned in chapter
2
, finite algebras are the algebras of interest here, and that constructed NC gauge theories will be based on such differential calculus defined on these finite algebras. The case of AF-algebras is left to chapters
14
and
15
since it requires the notion of
\phi
-compatibility which will be presented in chapter
13
in order to relate differential structures between two steps in the inductive sequence.

4.1
Differential Structure on
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
and
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}M_{n_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
using Derivations

Since the understanding the situation
\mathcal{A}_{i}=M_{n_{i}}(\mathbb{C})
is our main objective for
AF
-algebras, we give here a series of notations and results for later use when this specific situation will be considered in the case of NC gauge theories in the derivation framework. This is for instance the case in Sect.
11.5
and chapter
14
. We refer to
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
;
Masson, 1995
;
Dubois-Violette and Masson, 1998
;
Masson, 2008a
;
Masson, 2012
)
for more details.

4.1.1
Derivations, Differential Structure, Metric, Integration and Hodge
\star
-operator on
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})

The center of the algebra
M_{n}\vcentcolon=M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
is
\mathcal{Z}(M_{n})=\mathbb{C}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}
where
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}
is the unit matrix in
M_{n}
. Let
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
be the Lie algebra (for the commutator) of traceless matrices in
M_{n}
. Then the map
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\ni a\mapsto\ad_{a}\in\Int(M_{n})
realizes an isomorphism
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}\simeq\Der(M_{n})=\Int(M_{n})
(with
\Out(M_{n})=0
).

Let
\{E_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
be a basis of
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, where
I
is a totally ordered set with
\card(I)=n^{2}-1=\dim\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
. Choosing an abstract totally ordered set
I
to label this basis will be convenient when the inductive sequence defining the
AF
-algebra will be considered since then the
\alpha
’s will be constructed as cumulative multi-indices. The ordering will be used to order basis forms (for instance to define volume forms). Let us introduce the unique multiplet
(\alpha^{0}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1})\in I^{n^{2}-1}
such that
\alpha^{0}_{1}<\cdots<\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}
. We will use the notation
C(n)_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}=C_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}
for the structure constants of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
in the basis
\{E_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
:
[E_{\alpha},E_{\beta}]=C_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}E_{\gamma}
.

The basis
\{E_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
induces a basis
\{\partial_{\alpha}\vcentcolon=\ad_{E_{\alpha}}\}_{\alpha\in I}
of
\Der(M_{n})=\Int(M_{n})
. Let
\{\theta^{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
be its dual basis in
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}^{*}
. The derivation
\partial_{\alpha}
is real if and only if
E_{\alpha}
is anti-Hermitean and one has
[\partial_{\alpha},\partial_{\beta}]=C_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\partial_{\gamma}
.

The space of NC forms on
M_{n}
has a simple structure:

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(M_{n})

\displaystyle=M_{n}\otimes{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{\bullet}\mathfrak{sl}_{n}^{*}

and the differential is the Chevalley-Eilenberg differential for the differential graded algebra associated to the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
with values in
M_{n}
using the adjoint representation. Identifying
\theta^{\gamma}
with
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}\otimes\theta^{\gamma}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{1}(M_{n})=M_{n}\otimes{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{1}\mathfrak{sl}_{n}^{*}
, one has
\text{{d}}\theta^{\gamma}=-\tfrac{1}{2}C_{\alpha\beta}^{\gamma}\theta^{\alpha}\wedge\theta^{\beta}
.

Let us consider the canonical metric
g:\Der(M_{n})\times\Der(M_{n})\to\mathcal{Z}(M_{n})\simeq\mathbb{C}
defined by
g(\ad_{a},\ad_{b})\vcentcolon=\tr(ab)
for
a,b\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
. This is not the metric defined in
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
)
where a factor
\tfrac{1}{n}
was put in front of the trace (to get the
normalized
trace). The reason for this convention is linked to our constructions on AF-algebras and will be explained below (see (
14.2.1
) and comments after). Once the basis
\{\partial_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
is given, one introduces the components
g_{\alpha\beta}\vcentcolon=g(\partial_{\alpha},\partial_{\beta})=\tr(E_{\alpha}E_{\beta})
of
g
.

Let
\lvert g\rvert
be the determinant of the matrix
g
. We define the (NC) integral
\int_{M_{n}}
on
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(M_{n})
by the following rule. For any
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(M_{n})
with
p<n^{2}-1
,
\int_{M_{n}}\omega=0
. Any
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{n^{2}-1}(M_{n})
can be written as
\omega=a\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}
for a unique
a\in M_{n}
which is independent of the chosen basis
\{E_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in I}
and we define

\displaystyle\int_{M_{n}}\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{M_{n}}a\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}\vcentcolon=\tr(a)

(4.1.1)

Once again, this is not the convention used in
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
)
where a factor
\tfrac{1}{n}
was put in front of the RHS. In our convention,
\omega_{\text{{vol}}}\vcentcolon=\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}
is the volume form whose integral is normalized to
n
.

The metric permits to define the Hodge
\star
-operator

\displaystyle\star:\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(M_{n})\to\Omega_{\Der}^{n^{2}-1-p}(M_{n})

defined by

\displaystyle\star(\theta^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha_{p}})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\tfrac{1}{(n^{2}-1-p)!}\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}g^{\alpha_{1}\beta_{1}}\cdots g^{\alpha_{p}\beta_{p}}\epsilon_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n^{2}-1}}\theta^{\beta_{p+1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\beta_{n^{2}-1}}

(4.1.2)

where
\epsilon_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n^{2}-1}}
is the completely antisymmetric tensor such that
\epsilon_{\alpha^{0}_{1},\dots,\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}=1
.

Example 4.1.1
(Transport by inner automorphisms).

Let us consider the situation described in Example
3.3.2
in the context of the matrix algebra. Let
\omega=\frac{1}{p!}\omega_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p}}\theta^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha_{p}}
be a
p
-form. To compute
\omega^{u}\vcentcolon=\Psi(\omega)
, let us introduce the matrix
U=(U_{\alpha}^{\beta})
defined by
u^{-1}E_{\alpha}u=U_{\alpha}^{\beta}E_{\beta}
, so that
\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\partial_{\alpha})=\ad_{u^{-1}E_{\alpha}u}=U_{\alpha}^{\beta}\partial_{\beta}
. Since
SL_{n}
is unimodular, one has
\det(U)=1
. Notice also that
u^{-1}[E_{\alpha_{1}},E_{\alpha_{2}}]u=[u^{-1}E_{\alpha_{1}}u,u^{-1}E_{\alpha_{2}}u]=U_{\alpha_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}U_{\alpha_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}[E_{\beta_{1}},E_{\beta_{2}}]=U_{\alpha_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}U_{\alpha_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}C_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}^{\beta_{3}}E_{\beta_{3}}
on the one hand and
u^{-1}[E_{\alpha_{1}},E_{\alpha_{2}}]u=C_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\alpha_{3}}u^{-1}E_{\alpha_{3}}u=C_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\alpha_{3}}U_{\alpha_{3}}^{\beta_{3}}E_{\beta_{3}}
on the other hand, so that
U_{\alpha_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}U_{\alpha_{2}}^{\beta_{2}}C_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}^{\beta_{3}}=C_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}^{\alpha_{3}}U_{\alpha_{3}}^{\beta_{3}}
. By definition,
(\theta^{\alpha})^{u}(\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}})=u\theta^{\alpha}(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}}))u^{-1}=uU_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\beta^{\prime}}\delta_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\alpha}u^{-1}=U_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\alpha}=U_{\beta}^{\alpha}\theta^{\beta}(\partial_{\alpha^{\prime}})
so that
(\theta^{\alpha})^{u}=U_{\beta}^{\alpha}\theta^{\beta}
. In the same way,
\omega^{u}_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p}}=\omega^{u}(\partial_{\alpha_{1}},\dots,\partial_{\alpha_{p}})=u\omega(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\partial_{\alpha_{1}}),\dots,\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\partial_{\alpha_{p}}))u^{-1}=U_{\alpha_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots U_{\alpha_{p}}^{\beta_{p}}u\omega_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{p}}u^{-1}
, so that
\omega^{u}=\frac{1}{p!}U_{\alpha_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}\cdots U_{\alpha_{p}}^{\beta_{p}}u\omega_{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{p}}u^{-1}\theta^{\alpha_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha_{p}}=\frac{1}{p!}u\omega_{\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{p}}u^{-1}(\theta^{\alpha_{1}})^{u}\wedge\cdots\wedge(\theta^{\alpha_{p}})^{u}
.

Now, the metric
g(\ad_{a},\ad_{b})=\tr(ab)
, for
a,b\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, is invariant by the transport associated to the inner automorphism
\Psi(a)=uau^{-1}
, and so one has
g_{\alpha\beta}=U_{\alpha}^{\alpha^{\prime}}U_{\beta}^{\beta^{\prime}}g_{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
and
g^{\alpha\beta}=U_{\alpha^{\prime}}^{\alpha}U_{\beta^{\prime}}^{\beta}g^{\alpha^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}}
for the inverse metric. In particular, all the conditions and properties concerning orthonormality associated to
g
are transported by
\Psi
. For
\omega=a\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}
, one has
\omega^{u}=uau^{-1}\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}(\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{1}})^{u}\wedge\cdots\wedge(\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}})^{u}=uau^{-1}\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}U_{\beta_{1}}^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\cdots U_{\beta_{n^{2}-1}}^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}\theta^{\beta_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\beta_{n^{2}-1}}=uau^{-1}\sqrt{\lvert g\rvert}\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}
where we have used
\theta^{\beta_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\beta_{n^{2}-1}}=\epsilon^{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n^{2}-1}}\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge\theta^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}
and
\epsilon^{\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n^{2}-1}}U_{\beta_{1}}^{\alpha^{0}_{1}}\cdots U_{\beta_{n^{2}-1}}^{\alpha^{0}_{n^{2}-1}}=\det(U)=1
. This implies that
\int_{M_{n}}\omega^{u}=\tr(uau^{-1})=\tr(a)=\int_{M_{n}}\omega
. Since the metric
g
is invariant, the Hodge
\star
-operator is also invariant according to (
4.1.2
), and since the inverse metric is also invariant under the action of
U
, a straightforward computation of
\star((\theta^{\alpha_{1}})^{u}\wedge\cdots\wedge(\theta^{\alpha_{p}})^{u})
shows that the relation (
4.1.2
) is also valid when one replaces all the
\theta^{\alpha}
by
(\theta^{\alpha})^{u}
on both sides. Combining all these results and the explicit relation (
4.1.7
), one can show that for any
p
-forms
\omega
and
\omega^{\prime}
, one has
\int_{M_{n}}(\omega\wedge\star\omega^{\prime})^{u}=\int_{M_{n}}\omega^{u}\wedge\star\omega^{\prime u}=\int_{M_{n}}\omega\wedge\star\omega^{\prime}
.

\Diamond

4.1.2
Derivations, Differential Structure, Metric, Integration and Hodge
\star
-operator on
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}M_{n_{i}}(\mathbb{C})

In this subsection we consider the derivation-based differential calculus on algebras decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{1}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathcal{A}_{r}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{A}_{i}

The results are given for these general algebras in order to see how direct sum of matrix algebras are particular. We define respectively

\displaystyle\pi^{i}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{A}_{i},\text{ and }\iota_{i}:\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}

as the natural projection on the
i
-th term and the natural inclusion of the
i
-th term
1
1

1

The notation
\pi
is taken to be the same as the notation for representation, but always with indices
.

Some results are presented using this full generality but others will require
\mathcal{A}_{i}=M_{n}
.

Let’s start with center and derivations.

Lemma 4.1.2
(Center of
\mathcal{A}
).

The center of
\mathcal{A}
is
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
.

Proof

Every
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
must commute with any
b=0\toplus\cdots 0\toplus b_{j}\toplus 0\toplus 0
for any
j=1,\dots,r
and any
b_{j}\in\mathcal{A}_{j}
. This implies that
a_{j}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{j})
for any
j
. The result follows since
\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})\subset\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
.

Let us introduce the convenient notation for the elements

\displaystyle\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{i}\vcentcolon=\iota_{i}({\text{\char 49\relax}})=0\toplus\cdots\toplus 0\toplus\overbrace{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}^{\text{i}}\toplus 0\toplus\cdots\toplus 0\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathcal{A}.

Notice that we use the fact that the
\mathcal{A}_{i}
’s are unital.

Proposition 4.1.3
(Decomposition of derivations).

One has

\displaystyle\Der(\mathcal{A})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i}),

(4.1.3)

i.e. for any
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
, one has
\mathfrak{X}(a)=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}(a_{i})
.

This decomposition holds true as Lie algebras and modules over
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
on the left and over
\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
on the right.

If
\,\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})=\Int(\mathcal{A}_{i})
for any
i=1,\dots,r
, then

\displaystyle\Der(\mathcal{A})=\Int(\mathcal{A})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\Int(\mathcal{A}_{i})

(4.1.4)

Proof

The vector space decomposition (
4.1.3
) can be established using the maps
\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{j}\vcentcolon=\pi^{j}\circ\mathfrak{X}\circ\iota_{i}:\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{A}_{j}
. For any
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}
and
b=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}b_{i}
, one has

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}(a)

\displaystyle=\toplus_{j=1}^{r}\left(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{j}(a_{i})\right)

and the Leibniz rule
\mathfrak{X}(ab)=\mathfrak{X}(a)b+a\mathfrak{X}(b)
can then be written as

\displaystyle\toplus_{j=1}^{r}\left(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{j}(a_{i}b_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\toplus_{j=1}^{r}\left(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{j}(a_{i})\right)b_{j}+\toplus_{j=1}^{r}a_{j}\left(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{j}(b_{i})\right).

For a fixed
k
, take
a_{i}=b_{i}=0
for
i\neq k
. Then this relation reduces to

\displaystyle\toplus_{j=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{k}^{j}(a_{k}b_{k})

\displaystyle=0\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathfrak{X}_{k}^{k}(a_{k})b_{k}\toplus 0\toplus\cdots\toplus 0+0\toplus\cdots\toplus a_{k}\mathfrak{X}_{k}^{k}(b_{k})\toplus 0\toplus\cdots\toplus 0.

The
k
-th term shows that
\mathfrak{X}_{k}^{k}\in\Der(\mathcal{A}_{k})
, which implies in particular that
\mathfrak{X}_{k}^{k}({\text{\char 49\relax}})=0
. Then, with
b_{k}={\text{\char 49\relax}}
, one gets
\mathfrak{X}_{k}^{j}=0
for
j\neq k
. This shows that
\mathfrak{X}(a)=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}(a_{i})
with
\mathfrak{X}_{i}\vcentcolon=\mathfrak{X}_{i}^{i}\in\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})
and we write

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}

For any
f=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}f_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
, one has

\displaystyle(f\mathfrak{X})(a)

\displaystyle=f(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}(a_{i}))=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}(f_{i}\mathfrak{X}_{i})(a_{i})

so that (
4.1.3
) holds true as modules over
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
on the left and over
\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
on the right.

For
\mathfrak{Y}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{Y}_{i}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
, one has

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}](a)

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{X}\circ\mathfrak{Y}(a)-\mathfrak{Y}\circ\mathfrak{X}(a)=\mathfrak{X}\left(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{Y}_{i}(a_{i})\right)-\mathfrak{Y}\left(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}(a_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}\circ\mathfrak{Y}_{i}(a_{i})-\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{Y}_{i}\circ\mathfrak{X}_{i}(a_{i})

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}[\mathfrak{X}_{i},\mathfrak{Y}_{i}](a_{i})

so that

\displaystyle[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}[\mathfrak{X}_{i},\mathfrak{Y}_{i}]

This shows that (
4.1.3
) holds true as Lie algebras.

The proof of (
4.1.4
) is then a direct consequence: assuming
\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})=\Int(\mathcal{A}_{i})
, one has
\Int(\mathcal{A})\subset\Der(\mathcal{A})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\Int(\mathcal{A}_{i})\subset\Int(\mathcal{A})
where the last inclusion follows from
\ad_{a_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\ad_{a_{r}}=\ad_{a_{1}\toplus\cdots\toplus a_{r}}
.

We define
\iota^{\Der}_{i}:\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})\to\Der(\mathcal{A})
as the inclusion
\mathfrak{X}_{i}\mapsto 0\toplus\cdots\toplus 0\toplus\mathfrak{X}_{i}\toplus 0\cdots\toplus 0
in the
i
-th term. This is a morphism of Lie algebras and for any
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
f_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
, one has
\iota_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i}(a_{i}))=\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})(\iota_{i}(a_{i}))
and
\iota^{\Der}_{i}(f_{i}\mathfrak{X}_{i})=\iota_{i}(f_{i})\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})
. Notice also that for any
\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}
, one has
\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{i}\mathfrak{X}=\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})=\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{i}\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})
.

Using results given in Sect.
4.1.1
, we obtain the direct Corollary of Prop.
4.1.3
:

Corollary 4.1.4
.

For
\mathcal{A}=M_{n_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus M_{n_{r}}
, one has
\Der(\mathcal{A})=\Int(\mathcal{A})\simeq\mathfrak{sl}_{n_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathfrak{sl}_{n_{r}}
.

Let’s see now how it works for the Derivation-based differential calculus. A useful result concerning the structure of the derivation-based differential calculus associated to
\mathcal{A}
is the following.

Proposition 4.1.5
(Decomposition of forms).

For any
p\geq 0
, one has

\displaystyle\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A}_{i}),

that is, any
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
decomposes as
\omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}
with
\omega_{i}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
and

\displaystyle\omega(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p})

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,i},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i})\quad\text{for any $\mathfrak{X}_{k}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{k,i}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})$.}

This decomposition is compatible with the
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-linearity on the left and the
\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
-linearity on the right, and it is compatible with the products in
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
and
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
.

The differential
d
on
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
decomposes along the differentials
\text{{d}}_{i}
on
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
as

\displaystyle\text{{d}}\omega

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\text{{d}}_{i}\omega_{i}

We will extend the projection map
\pi^{i}:\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})\to\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
with the same notation.

Proof

Let
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})
and define, for any
i_{1},\dots,i_{p},j=1,\dots,r
,

\displaystyle\omega^{j}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}:\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i_{1}})\times\cdots\times\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i_{p}})\to\mathcal{A}_{j}

by

\displaystyle\omega^{j}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,i_{1}},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})=\pi^{j}\circ\omega(\iota^{\Der}_{i_{1}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,i_{1}}),\dots,\iota^{\Der}_{i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}}))

for any
\mathfrak{X}_{k,i_{k}}\in\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i_{k}})
(
k=1,\dots,p
). Then one has

\displaystyle\omega(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p})

\displaystyle=\toplus_{j=1}^{r}\left(\tsum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,i_{1}},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})\right)

with
\mathfrak{X}_{k}=\toplus_{i_{k}=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{k,i_{k}}
. Let
f_{k}=\toplus_{i_{k}=1}^{r}f_{k,i_{k}}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
, then applying
\omega
on the
p
derivations
f_{k}\mathfrak{X}_{k}
, the
\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
-linearity of
\omega
gives

\displaystyle\toplus_{j=1}^{r}

\displaystyle\left(\tsum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}(f_{1,i_{1}}\mathfrak{X}_{1,i_{1}},\dots,f_{p,i_{p}}\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})\right)

\displaystyle=\toplus_{j=1}^{r}f_{1,j}\cdots f_{p,j}\left(\tsum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,i_{1}},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})\right)

(4.1.5)

The arbitrariness on the
f_{k}
’s permits to simplify this relation in successive steps. Let us fix
j_{1}
and take
f_{1}=\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j_{1}}
.
Then on the LHS, the term at
j
in
\toplus_{j=1}^{r}
reduces to

\displaystyle\tsum_{i_{2},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j}_{j_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,j_{1}},f_{2,i_{2}}\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}},\dots,f_{p,i_{p}}\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}}).

On the RHS, the only non zero term along
\toplus_{j=1}^{r}
occurs at
j=j_{1}
and gives

\displaystyle f_{2,j_{1}}\cdots f_{p,j_{1}}\left(\tsum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j_{1}}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,i_{1}},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})\right).

By arbitrariness on the
\mathfrak{X}_{k}
’s, this implies that for
j\neq j_{1}
one has
\omega^{j}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}=0
and the remaining non trivial relation becomes (substituting
j_{1}
to
j
)

\displaystyle\tsum_{i_{2},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}

\displaystyle\omega^{j}_{j,i_{2},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,j},f_{2,i_{2}}\mathfrak{X}_{2,i_{2}},\dots,f_{p,i_{p}}\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})=f_{2,j}\cdots f_{p,j}\left(\tsum_{i_{2},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j}_{j,i_{2},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,j},\mathfrak{X}_{2,i_{2}},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})\right).

Let us now fix
j_{2}
and take
f_{2}=\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j_{2}}
. Then the relation first gives

\displaystyle\tsum_{i_{3},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j}_{j,j_{2},i_{3},\dots,i_{p}}

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{X}_{1,j},\mathfrak{X}_{2,j_{2}},f_{3,i_{3}}\mathfrak{X}_{3,i_{3}},\dots,f_{p,i_{p}}\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})=0\qquad\text{ for $j\neq j_{2}$},

which implies
\omega^{j}_{j,i_{2},i_{3},\dots,i_{p}}=0
for
i_{2}\neq j
. Then, with this relation, the non vanishing term (at
j=j_{2}
) simplifies to (substituting
j_{2}
to
j
)

\displaystyle\tsum_{i_{3},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}

\displaystyle\omega^{j}_{j,j,i_{3},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,j},\mathfrak{X}_{2,j},f_{3,i_{3}}\mathfrak{X}_{3,i_{3}},\dots,f_{p,i_{p}}\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})=f_{3,j}\cdots f_{p,j}\left(\tsum_{i_{3},\dots,i_{p}=1}^{r}\omega^{j}_{j,j,i_{3},\dots,i_{p}}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,j},\mathfrak{X}_{2,j},\mathfrak{X}_{3,i_{3}},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i_{p}})\right).

We can repeat this argument for
i_{k}
up to
k=p
and conclude that the only non zero maps
\omega^{j}_{i_{1},\dots,i_{p}}
are
\omega^{j}_{j,\dots,j}
for
j=1,\dots,r
. Defining
\omega_{i}\vcentcolon=\omega^{i}_{i,\dots,i}
, one then gets

\displaystyle\omega(\mathfrak{X}_{1},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p})

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{1,i},\dots,\mathfrak{X}_{p,i})

and (
4.1.5
) reduces to

\displaystyle\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}(f_{1,i}\mathfrak{X}_{1,i},\dots,f_{p,i}\mathfrak{X}_{p,i})
