\mathfrak{g}
. It is then possible to locally trivialize
E
on small open sets of
x\in M
in order to obtain the usual gauge connection expressed here in spacetime coordinates
A_{\mu}\,\in\,\Omega^{1}(x)\otimes\mathfrak{g}
and it’s curvature
F_{\mu\nu}\,\in\,\Omega^{2}(x)\otimes\mathfrak{g}
which transform in the same way as in section
10.1
,
g\in\mathcal{G}
being parameterized by
M
.

The SMPP consists in the understanding of two things, what are matter fields, and through which mediators they interact. In the fiber bundle framework, these two fields will correspond to well identified structures in the fiber bundle formalism:

•

Matter fields will correspond to sections in the fiber bundle

•

Interaction fields will correspond to connection in the principal bundle

Because of their statistics, matter fields will correspond to fermions and interaction fields to bosons.

In the field of developments in particle physics, Yang-Mills theories can be considered as one of the most important breakthroughs coming from the application of the Gauge principle, they are now at the heart of the structure of the SMPP. Indeed, exploring how non-abelian Lie groups like
SU(n)
are implemented in the gauge potential can lead to building gauge theories that permit a better understanding of strong and weak forces, later allowing the electro-weak unification. Now, all forces of the SMPP are expressed as Yang-Mills theories.

In the case of gauge symmetry under the action of a non-Abelian (locally parameterized) group
\mathcal{G}
, the situation is slightly more complicated, as said, it is described by a Yang-Mills theory. If we consider
\mathcal{G}=SU(n)
, it acts on the fermionic field
\psi(x)\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
, we note
\{T^{a}\}_{a=1\dots n-1}
the infinitesimal generators, then we have:

\displaystyle\psi(x)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{1}(x)\\
\vdots\\
\psi_{n}(x)\end{array}\right);\qquad\qquad A_{\mu}(x)=A_{\mu}^{a}(x)T^{a};\qquad\qquad g(x)=e^{i\alpha^{a}(x)T^{a}}.

The curvature being given by
F_{\mu\nu}=F_{\mu\nu}^{a}T^{a}
with
F_{\mu\nu}^{a}=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}^{a}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}^{a}-(ie/\hbar)f^{abc}A_{\mu}^{b}A_{\nu}^{c}
with
f^{abc}
the structure constants of
\mathfrak{g}
.

To build an invariant from
F
, we have to understand how the inner product work on indices forms. Taking the couple
(M,\tilde{g})
of a manifold with it’s metric, then
\tilde{g}
induce the bilinear form
\langle\,\,|\,\,\rangle\,:\Omega^{k}(M)\otimes\Omega^{k}(M)\,\to\,\Omega^{0}(M)
. For compact manifold, an integral against the volume form
vol_{\tilde{g}}
can be defined, this is called the Hodge inner product
(\,\,|\,\,)\,:\,\Omega^{k}(M)\otimes\Omega^{k}(M)\,\to\,\mathbb{R}
, it is defined by:

\displaystyle(F_{1}|F_{2})\ \,\vcentcolon=\,\int_{M}\langle F_{1}|F_{2}\rangle vol_{\tilde{g}}

This allows to define the Hodge star operator
\star\,:\,\Omega^{k}(M)\,\to\,\Omega^{n-k}(M)
,
i.e.
this is the unique linear function such that:

\displaystyle F_{1}\wedge\star F_{2}=\langle F_{1}|F_{2}\rangle vol_{\tilde{g}}

Then the bosonic action associated with
A
can be given by the Yang-Mills action:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{b}=\int_{M}F\wedge\star F

This number must be left invariant by two kinds of process, gauge transformations, change of coordinate (according to general relativity framework), and trough any transformation corresponding to the equarions of motion, these last ones being given by computing the solutions of:

\displaystyle\delta\mathcal{S}_{b}=2\int_{M}\delta F\wedge\star F=0.

for one shell solutions. The fermionic action is given by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{f}=-\int_{M}i\overline{\psi}(\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}+imc/\hbar)\psi d^{4}x

This provides a nice link between physical objects and mathematical structures. But as mentioned in
(
François et al., 2014
)
, the Higgs field is an object with ambiguous status in terms of the mathematical structure representing it. Indeed, it is both considered as a Boson, and as a section in a vector bundle, which typically represents Fermions fields. Moreover, its potential is not deduced from the formalism but added by hand. This appears to be slightly inconsistent from the mathematical structures’ point of view. As we will see in section
11.4
, and then in sections
11.5
and
11.6
, the reformulation of the SMPP in the NC framework will make the Higgs field more consistent as a mathematical structure, with a natural potential coming from the formalism.

10.3
The Standard Model of Particle Physics

The purpose of this section is to highlight the structure of the SMPP, making the connection between it’s Lagrangian, the meanings and interpretations of its terms, it’s symmetries according to the gauge principle, and in which way equations of motion can be obtained from it.

A Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
is a quantity that takes a particular value at each point in space and time. The Lagrangian is one of the most fundamental objects in theoretical physics. It is a functional which can depend for example on fields (in field theory) or on the metric of space-time (in general relativity), and which has the property of having its integral on the space-time (
i.e.
the action) minimized for a physical state and its evolution. This condition is expressed by the Euler-Lagrange equation, the so-called equation of motion.
The Lagrangian also allows us to define and study the crucial notion of symmetry. We call global symmetry under the action of a group
\mathcal{G}
a transformation that leaves the Lagrangian invariant and depends on a single parameter
g\in\mathcal{G}
for the whole space-time. Conversely, a local symmetry depends on a parameter
g\in\mathcal{G}
for each
point in space-time or, more precisely, on a smooth function
g(x)
.

The Lagrangian of the SMPP is the following:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}=-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}+i\overline{\psi}\not{D}\psi+\overline{\psi}_{i}y_{ij}\Phi\psi_{j}+h.c.+\left|D^{\mu}\Phi\right|^{2}+V(\Phi)

(10.3.1)

with
\overline{\psi}=\psi^{\dagger}\gamma_{0}
,
\not{D}=i\gamma^{\mu}D_{\mu}
,
y_{ij}
the Yukawa coupling matrix,
\Phi
the Higgs field and
V(\Phi)=\lambda\Phi^{2}+\mu\Phi^{4}
its potential.

The quantum version of the Standard Model Lagrangian consists of several quantum fields, each associated with a Standard Model particle. There are actually 3 types of fields classified by their spins:

•

those for Fermions (quarks and leptons) designated by
\psi
with spin
1/2
.

•

those for the mediator bosons (photon, gluons,
W^{\pm}
and
Z^{0}
bosons), designated by
A_{\mu}
, with spin
1
.

•

The Higgs bosonic field is designated by
\Phi
with spin
0
.

These fields are functions of the space-time points. They are not simple functions with numerical values, but operators acting on field’s state, adding or removing particles. Consequently the Lagrangian and its associated action become operators too, this is called quantization and consists in promoting these fields, which are for the moment only functions, to the status of operators on a Hilbert space, the Fock space, which then verifies certain imposed commutation relations. There is no canonical method to quantize fields, this leads to many difficulties such as the need of renormalization. An important physical point that the Lagrangian makes it possible to determine is how the various particles propagate between points in space-time. The corresponding terms are those defining the creation of a particle at a given point and its annihilation at another one, the ones with two fields.

Einstein’s relation (
E=mc^{2}
) allows the conversion between energy and mass, and thus the possibility of changing the number and nature of particles during a process. Unlike in classical and pure QM, the nature of the system can then change, and visit many intermediate states. The possible transitions and their probabilities are constrained by the structure of the Lagrangian. All independent groupements of terms of
\mathcal{L}
describe the interaction between particles (potentials and interactional terms) or propagators (kinetics terms):

•

Kinetic terms:
They correspond to the free propagation of the particle along space-time degrees of freedom, it implies spacetime derivatives and mass terms. For Fermions
i\overline{\psi}\not{\partial}\psi
, for Bosons these are terms like
(\partial^{\mu}A_{\nu})^{2}
and for the Higgs Boson
\left|\partial^{\mu}\Phi\right|^{2}

•

Potential and international terms:
For Fermions
i\overline{\psi}\not{D}\psi-i\overline{\psi}\not{\partial}\psi
and
\overline{\psi}_{i}y_{ij}\Phi\psi_{j}
, for Bosons
-\frac{1}{4}F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}-(\partial^{\mu}A_{\nu})^{2}
and
\left|D^{\mu}\Phi\right|^{2}-\left|\partial^{\mu}\Phi\right|^{2}
and
V(\Phi)

\mathcal{L}
correspond then to the difference between potential and Kinetics terms.

Remark 10.3.1
.

\overline{\psi}_{i}y_{ij}\Phi\psi_{j}
containing the Yukawa coupling matrix
y_{ij}
which specifies the strength of the interaction (and then it’s probability to occur) between fermions and Higgs field. This term gives both the fermion’s masses and the probability strength of the flavor-changing process. We will see in section
12.2
, mainly in remarks
12.2.3
and
12.2.2
, that in the context of the NCSMPP, this term acquires a kind of kinetic interpretation in the same way as
i\overline{\psi}\not{D}\psi
.

The SMPP is written in the framework of gauge theory, we will denote by
\text{GFT}_{\text{SMPP}}
the actual gauge field theory of the SMPP.

10.4
Gravitation and Gauge Theories

Actually, we know 4 fundamental forces, the ones of the SMPP, and Gravitation. Many physicists try to unify Gravitation to the three other forces, in order to understand how to quantize gravitation and obtain a more unified picture. Some basic hints can make us think that these two blocks share some similarities:

•

The position coordinate is not observable, only the difference of coordinate between two positions is. The same hold for phases and then connections, only relative difference in phases are observable. Making gauge potential and coordinate on the same footing for the fact that they do not directly represent an observable, but rather a relationship between observables.

•

Like gauge transformations, general coordinate transformations have no consequences on physics, they represent symmetries of the theory.

•

The curvature of spacetime curves the trajectories of test particles. Interactions symbolized by gauge potentials acting on fermionic fields of test particles curve their trajectories, in a similar way as gravitation when we compute the curvature of the gauge potential (see the gravitational Aharonov-Bohm effect).

But the problem is that gravitation is a geometric theory contrary to other forces and that they are expressed in different mathematical fields.

Historically, gravitation was elaborated by Einstein in the framework of pseudo-Riemannian geometry and not in the one of gauge theory. The basic principle of this framework is to define an affine connection
\nabla\,:\,\Gamma(T(M))\times\Gamma(T(M))\,\to\,\Gamma(T(M))
by the constraints:

\displaystyle\nabla_{f\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu}=f\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}}f\partial_{\nu}=\partial_{\mu}f\partial_{\nu}+f\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu}

\forall f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
and
\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu}\in\Gamma(T(M))
. These locally connect tangent spaces.

In order to make this connection compatible with the metric structure
\tilde{g}
, we define the metric connection by

\displaystyle\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{g}(\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu})=\tilde{g}(\nabla_{\lambda}\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu})+\tilde{g}(\partial_{\mu},\nabla_{\lambda}\partial_{\nu})

(10.4.1)

which can be seen as a sub-case of equation (
11.2.1
) that will be defined later.

Adding the torsion-free condition (
\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu}-\nabla_{\partial_{\nu}}\partial_{\mu}=[\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu}]
) we obtain the Levi-Civita connection, which is at the heart of general relativity.

Using these Levi-Civita connection, we can construct a covariant derivative (in local coordinate) on the tangent bundle
\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}}\,:\,\Gamma(T(M))\,\to\,\Gamma(T(M))
, with curvature being given by
[\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}},\nabla_{\partial_{\nu}}]-\nabla_{[\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu}]}
. Using the Christoffel symbols defined by
\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu}=\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}\partial_{\lambda}
, the curvature can be computed, giving the curvature tensor
R_{\mu\nu\rho}^{\lambda}
, then the Ricci tensor
R_{\mu\nu}=R_{\mu\nu\lambda}^{\lambda}
, and then the scalar curvature
\tilde{g}^{\mu\nu}R_{\mu\nu}
. This last scalar can be taken to define the action of the Gravitation theory:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\tilde{g}]\propto\int_{M}R{\sqrt{|\tilde{g}|}}\,\mathrm{d}^{4}x

But the fact that the primary field taken to be the metric
\tilde{g}
is not a 1-form makes the Lagrangian given by
R
only invariant under the group
\Diff(M)
, taken to be the gauge group of the theory. Therefore,
R
is only a scalar for the geometric structure defined on
M
, but not for general Gauge transformation, then different to gauge theory in the general meaning.

In the same way, we can see that forces in gauge theories differ from the gravitation one since this last has been given a geometric interpretation, promoting it to the status of pseudo-force
i.e.
fictitious force induced by the effect of an irregular background geometry. This is not the case with Gauge’s theories, for which forces are considered as pure forces, without any underlying geometrical representation explaining them.

But subsequent developments reformulated this theory in the field of Cartan geometry making this theory be of gauge type. More details on the construction can be found in
(
François et al., 2014
;
Bennett, 2021
)
. We will see in chapter
12
that the NCSMPP offers such a way to reformulate both Gravitation and SMPP forces into the same framework, at the same time being gauge theory and pseudo forces induced by pure geometry.

10.5
Open Doors to go Beyond the SMPP

In physical theories, unification can be seen as how two theories, previously understood as independent become sub-manifestations of a larger theory, according to some parameters like energy change, formalism change inside the same framework, framework change… It is perceived as an enhancement of the theoretical understanding.

Taking a physical theory and its mathematical structure
\mathfrak{M}
, not all theoreticians will agree on the fact that it is complete and consistent or not, and in this last case, they can differ on which way this enhancement must be done. The enhancement of a physical theory can be thinking according to several criteria and arguments. I propose here 3 kinds of enhancement arguments:

1.

The first concern is the diminution of the number of constant inputs. Like for example the 18 inputs of the SMPP.

2.

The second one is the diminution of the number of independent formal inputs. The equations of the theory are deduced from minimal principle, the number of “put by hand” elements is minimal. A good framework can help to obtain this result.

3.

Consistency with experiment, with other theories on their crossings area, and on the conceptual and epistemological level.

In what will follow (see section
12.1
), I will highlight according to which one of these arguments NCG improves our understanding of the SMPP.

Concerning the SMPP, here is a non-complete list of what can be seen as missing-points with potential enhancement needed in the actual description:

•

Find a mechanism that determines the origin of fermion masses, and thus explains the three-generation existence. (12 inputs over 18 are concerned)

•

About the Cabibbo Kobayashi-Maskawa (CKM) matrix
M_{CKM}
:

1.

The eigenstates of the free Hamiltonian are also the ones of the gravitational, strong and electromagnetic interactions, but they are not eigenstates of weak one. There are no explanations for this fact
(
Hübsch, 2015
)
.

2.

The non-unity of the measured CKM matrix. According to the theoretical framework in which this matrix is constructed, it must be unitary.

3.

There is no theory predicting the value of the angles parameterizing the CKM matrix.

•

Neutrino mixing and oscillations

•

Potential unification with gravitation, find a better formalism implementing naturally the Higgs field and its potential as mentioned, explain and/or unify the coupling constants…

Some of these points are questionable on whether they are truly problems in the actual description.

In this regard, Grand Unified Theories (GUT) can be considered as the most significant attempt for solving such problems. The search for unification in field theory is a very old and fruitful field of investigation in theoretical physics. Some nice achievements are the unification of electric and magnetic fields in the electromagnetic one, and later of this last with weak force in the electroweak unification. GUT are extensions of the SMPP with larger symmetry groups than the existing ones. They must contain the SMPP group as a subgroup and possess complex representations (fermionic fields) in accordance with the ones of the SMPP… Actually, GUTs theorists try to exploit the successful arguments of gauge symmetries and group theory to unify electroweak and strong interactions into a single one. A hint for such hope of unification is that the flows of the gauge couplings “constants” of the SMPP at a high energy scale seem to converge.

Therefore, the question is what are the ways in which the SMPP fields can be embedded into an unified field? This unified field (and its groups) must be large enough to encompass the current groups of the SMPP, but not too, so that there will not be too much new physics, likely not to fit with the experiment. It must correspond to the effective theory at higher energies, and render the actual SMPP within a certain limit, with some physical process (like spontaneous symmetry breaking for example). By introducing new fields with their symmetries, GUTs can improve many features of the SMPP. For example, they can provide a gauge coupling unification, give an explanation for the lightness of neutrino masses, introduce contributions that can solve some flavor anomalies, potentially explain charge quantization and predict values for weak mixing angles.

The first and simplest GUT being explored was the one of the Georgi–Glashow model corresponding to the gauge group
SU(5)
. Many other groups like
SU(5)\times U(1),SO(10),E_{6}\dots
have been explored, without succeeding. A nice review of GUTs attempts can be found in
(
Croon et al., 2019
)
.

Let us try to explain why this kind of attempt seems to offer a set of natural paths for unification, by trying to think in a general way about unification. The first assumption to make is that this can be done in the current formalism of gauge theories, or in any more or less equivalent formalisms based on field theories, such as the NCSMPP presented in chapter
12
.
The basic ingredients of the SMPP are gauge potentials, not fermionic fields, which are secondary representations, so the structure of the standard model is primarily driven by the nature of these bosonic fields, this is the second assumption. A procedure to create such a model is to choose the right gauge field according to these invariance groups and symmetry arguments, and then to choose among the possible set of representations (fermionic fields, chiral structure…), which one is the most adequate. Assumption three is that the same procedure should be used in attempts at unification, but taking into account the basic ingredients of the old theory,
i.e.
those fermionic and bosonic degrees of freedom (dofs), which should be linked according to certain criteria. It is now a question of understanding the (conceptual) passage from the gauge theory of the SMPP,
i.e.

\text{GFT}_{\text{SMPP}}
, to the grand unified theory:

\displaystyle\text{GFT}_{\text{SMPP}}\,\rightarrow\,\text{GFT}_{\text{GUT}}

in the first time, because the
\text{GFT}_{\text{GUT}}
must be built from the one of the SMPP. Then we have to understand the (physical) mechanism
\text{GFT}_{\text{SMPP}}\,\leftarrow\,\text{GFT}_{\text{GUT}}
which renders
\text{GFT}_{\text{SMPP}}
from
\text{GFT}_{\text{GUT}}
trough a suitable physical process, like spontaneous symmetry breaking mechanism SSBM. This can be seen into a 3 step process: first find the good gauge field algebra, then a suitable (fermionic field) representation and check that the corresponding mechanism restitutes the actual SMPP. There are many ways to imagine GUTs in this way.
In this thesis, the construction of gauge fields based on inductive sequences of algebras (which correspond to these gauge potentials taken as input) will offer a framework to elaborate such GUT and understand the
\text{GFT}_{\text{SMPP}}\,\rightarrow\,\text{GFT}_{\text{GUT}}
transition. This transition will correspond to a step in the inductive sequence. This will be presented in section
13
, and done in part
IV
. The implementation of assumption three (condition between the two theories, at least on its elementary degrees of freedom
i.e.
gauge and fermionic fields) will be given by the
\phi
-compatibility condition defined along the sequence and presented in section
13
.

Chapter 11
Noncommutative Gauge Field Theories (NCGFT)

In this chapter, I propose to show how the concepts presented in the previous chapter can be generalized in NCG. It will be done in a general way for the notions of connection, curvature, fiber bundle, and symmetries, but using derivation in section
11.5
then spectral triples in section
11.6
based GFT. Then the actual NCSMPP will be presented in chapter
12
, with some discussions. From now on, we will always suppose that
\mathcal{A}
is unital, with unit
1
.

11.1
Fiber Bundle and Serre-Swan Theorem

Another important topological structure possess a well identified algebraic counterpart, that of vector bundles. Indeed, the Serre-Swan theorem states that any vector bundle on a manifold
M
defines a
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-projective module of finite type on the algebra
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
by considering the set of smooth sections of this fiber; and conversely, if
M
is connected, any
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-projective module of finite type comes from a vector bundle on
M
. As for the Gelfand-Naimark theorem this duality is more than a coincidence. It results from the category equivalence between that of vector bundles on
M
and that of
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
-projective modules of finite type on
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
. Let first define what is a projective module.

Definition 11.1.1
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be an algebra, a module
\mathcal{M}
over
\mathcal{A}
is projective iff we have
\mathcal{M}\oplus\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{A}^{n}
, for some module
\mathcal{N}
(over
\mathcal{A}
), and an integer
n\geq 1
.

We build a free module
\mathcal{A}^{n}
over
\mathcal{A}
.

Lemma 11.1.2
.

Taking a projection
p\in M_{n}(\mathcal{A})
such that
p^{2}=p=p^{*}
, then we can define the projective module
\mathcal{M}_{p}=\mathcal{A}^{n}p
.

Proof

If
p
is a projection, then
1-p
is also a projection, and they are mutually orthogonal. Therefore
\mathcal{M}_{1-p}=\mathcal{A}^{n}(1-p)
is such that we have
\mathcal{A}^{n}=\mathcal{M}_{p}\oplus\mathcal{M}_{1-p}
so that according to definition
11.1.1
, this two modules are projective.

This module is said to be finitely generated when
n
is finite.

Let illustrate this by using our favorite commutative algebra
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
,
p
is therefore a function of
x\in M
. Thus
\mathcal{M}_{p(x)}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)^{n}p(x)
define the module associated to
x
.

Then any module
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{A}
will be considered as the non-commutative equivalent of an associated vector bundle, through the associated section. The set of sections of a trivial vector bundle whose fiber is of dimension
n
is isomorphic to the module
(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))^{n}
. When the fiber is not trivial, we place ourselves in a larger space, which means adding dimensions to trivialize it, the initial fiber is then obtained using a projection
p
:
(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))^{n}p
. In the commutative case, the space of matter fields can therefore be represented by a right module on
\mathcal{A}
, in the NC case, care should be taken to distinguish between the left and right modules. In NCG, bi-module will frequently be more adapted than module.

As we shall see, this new extending framework for the usual gauge theories is entirely algebraic, and thus leaves us with more freedom. This motivates the introduction of
C^{*}
-algebras as non-commutative generalizations of locally compact topological spaces, and modules as non-commutative generalizations of vector bundles.

It is interesting to notice that when
\mathcal{A}
is unital, there is a correspondence between projective modules of finite type and idempotents of
M_{n}(\mathcal{A})
. We can define (in an equivalent way to what has been done in section
1.3
) a notion of algebraic equivalence between idempotents which is equivalent to the notion of isomorphism between projective modules. As we have seen in section
1.3
, these idempotents allow us to classify algebras via K-theory, and thus in our context to classify the associated vector bundles. This is why the Serre-Swan theorem allowed to relate topological and algebraic K-theory (see
(
Swan, 2006
)
for more details).

Then if we take
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, we have
M_{n}(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M,M_{n}(\mathbb{C}))=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
, we recognize the AC-manifold. Thus the Serre-Swan theorem tells us that it is possible to construct a vector bundle using this AC-manifold, with base space
M
. We will see in section
11.4
that the AC-manifold is the basic structure of NCGFT, and that it has led to the current non-commutative standard model of particle physics in chapter
12
. The fact that K-theory allows to classify
AF
-algebras (which are generalizations of
M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
) provides a strong mathematical motivation to develop NCGFTs on AC-manifolds where the finite part algebra is taken to be an
AF
algebra. Indeed, K-theory and Serre-Swan theorem are intimately related through their use of the idempotents of
M_{n}(\mathcal{A})
. Then K-theory can be used to classify the corresponding gauge theories and “even more” the ones based on AF-algebras offering a strong mathematical reason to do NCGFTs based on AF-algebras.

11.2
Generalization of Vector Fields, Connections and Curvature

As said before, the Serre-Swan theorem asserts that finitely generated projective modules are linked to vector bundles.
Therefore we have to replace usual vector fields
\mathcal{V}
constituting sections by modules
\mathcal{M}
over an algebra. A very nice fact, proved by Cuntz and Quillen in
(
Cuntz and Quillen, 1995
)
is that every projective module admits the existence of connection and conversely, any module having a connection is projective. Defining a connection requires the pre-existence of a differential structure. In the usual framework, this can be the covariant derivative or the de Rham differential. As mentioned in chapter
3
, these can be replaced by derivation and spectral triples-based differential structures.

In the case of the universal differential calculus
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}}_{U})
, a NC connection can be defined as a
1
-form
\omega=\sum_{i}a^{0}_{i}\text{{d}}_{U}a^{1}_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
, but it is a too general definition. Now if we take an algebra
\mathcal{A}
with an involution
∗
and the associated differential calculus
(\Omega^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}})
with
\omega\in\Omega^{n}
and
\eta\in\Omega^{m}
, we will suppose that the graded algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}
possess an involution such that
(\omega\eta)^{*}=(-1)^{nm}\eta^{*}\omega^{*}
, and that
d
is real
(\text{{d}}\omega)^{*}=\text{{d}}(\omega^{*})
.

Definition 11.2.1
.

let
\mathcal{M}
be a left
\mathcal{A}
-module, and
\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{A})
be a first-order differential calculus over
\mathcal{A}
, with differential
d
. A connection is defined by a map
\nabla\,:\,\mathcal{M}\,\to\,\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}
such that,
\forall a\in\mathcal{A}
and
e\in\mathcal{M}
:

\displaystyle\nabla(ae)=a\nabla(e)+da\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}e.

Then the associated curvature is given by
R=\nabla^{2}
with
R\,:\,\mathcal{M}\,\to\,\Omega^{2}(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}
.

A Hermitian structure on
\mathcal{M}
is a
\mathbb{R}
-linear map
h:\mathcal{M}\otimes\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{A}
such that
h(a_{1}e_{1},a_{2}e_{2})=a_{1}h(e_{1},e_{2})a_{2}^{*}
for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
and
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
. A connection
\nabla
is Hermitian if for any real
\mathfrak{X}
of
\mathcal{A}
and any
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
, one has

\displaystyle\text{{d}}h(e_{1},e_{2})

\displaystyle=h(\nabla(e_{1}),e_{2})+h(e_{1},\nabla(e_{2}))

(11.2.1)

This can be viewed as a generalization of the metric condition given in equation (
10.4.1
).

It turns out that the module concept is not sufficient to elaborate nice structures, in particular concerning hermiticity problems. The bimodule structure (a module that is both left and right, making left and right multiplications compatible) is therefore preferable, especially in the context of spectral triples.

11.3
Automorphisms of Finite Noncommutative Spaces

If you cannot change your condition, change your perception.

D. Mridha

In the framework of NCGFT, automorphisms will provide gauge and coordinate change. As mentioned in chapter
3
, they are two ways to consider such changes. The first can be seen as change at the level of the Hilbert space (
i.e.
it’s generalization as module here), and the other as being at the level of the algebra.

To be general, let
\mathcal{A}
be an
∗
algebra with unit, and
\mathcal{M}
a left
\mathcal{A}
-module. Let’s start with the automorphisms which are implemented at the level of
\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 11.3.1
(Automorphism of a module
\mathcal{M}
).

An automorphism of a module
\mathcal{M}
is a
\mathcal{A}
-linear map
\Pi\,:\,\mathcal{M}\,\to\,\mathcal{M}
which is invertible and preserves the module structure:

\displaystyle\Pi(ae)=a\Pi(e)\qquad\qquad\forall e\in\mathcal{M}\qquad\text{and}\qquad a\in\mathcal{A}.

Let’s suppose that
\mathcal{M}
is equipped with a Hermitian structure
h
. The gauge group
\mathcal{G}
of
\mathcal{M}
is the group of automorphisms of
\mathcal{M}
as a left module that preserve the Hermitian structure such that for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}
,
g\in\mathcal{G}
satisfies

\displaystyle g(ae)

\displaystyle=ag(e),\quad h(g(e),g(e^{\prime}))=h(e,e^{\prime}).

(11.3.1)

The action of
g
on a connection is defined by the compositions
\nabla\mapsto\nabla^{g}\vcentcolon=g\circ\nabla\circ g^{-1}
. It is easy to check that
\nabla^{g}
is a connection and that
\nabla^{g_{2}\circ g_{1}}=(\nabla^{g_{1}})^{g_{2}}
for any
g_{1},g_{2}\in\mathcal{G}
.

Now, for the automorphisms implemented on the algebra:

Definition 11.3.2
(
*
-automorphism of a
*
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
).

An
*
-automorphism of a
*
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is a linear map
\Lambda\,:\,\mathcal{A}\,\to\,\mathcal{A}
which is invertible and preserve the
*
-algebra structure:

\displaystyle\Lambda(ab)=\Lambda(a)\Lambda(b)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Lambda(a^{*})=\Lambda(a)^{*}\qquad\qquad\qquad\forall a\in\mathcal{A}.

Given
\mathcal{A}
a
*
-algebra, we denote by
\Aut(\mathcal{A})
its automorphism group.

\Aut(\mathcal{A})
will be related to the gauge group associated to
\mathcal{A}
.
We call
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
the group of unitary elements in
\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})=\left\{u\in\mathcal{A}:uu^{*}=u^{*}u={\text{\char 49\relax}}\right\}

Definition 11.3.3
(Inner automorphism of
\mathcal{A}
).

An automorphism
\Lambda
is called inner if it is of the form
\Lambda(a)=\ad_{u}(a)=uau^{*}
with
u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and
a\in\mathcal{A}
. The group of inner automorphisms is denoted
by
\Inn(\mathcal{A})
.

In a similar way as for derivations with equation (
3.3.1
), the map
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})\,\to\,\Inn(\mathcal{A})
defined by
u\,\to\,\ad_{u}
is surjective with kernel the elements
u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\ad_{u}(a)=a\,\,\forall a
, which correspond to
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}))
. Then we have:

\displaystyle\Inn(\mathcal{A})\simeq\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})/\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}))

Definition 11.3.4
(Outer automorphism of
\mathcal{A}
).

The group of outer automorphisms of
\mathcal{A}
is defined by the quotient

\displaystyle\Out(\mathcal{A}):=\Aut(\mathcal{A})/\Inn(\mathcal{A})

For
M
a smooth compact manifold, and
\mathcal{A}=C^{\infty}(M)
, we have
\Aut(\mathcal{A})=\Out(\mathcal{A})\simeq\Diff(M)
, the group of diffeomorphisms of
M
. Taking a diffeomorphism
\Xi\,:\,M\,\to\,M
, the link between diffeomorphism and (outer) automorphism is given by the pullback over function space:

\displaystyle\Xi^{*}(f)(x)=f(\Xi(x))\qquad\qquad\forall f\in\mathcal{A}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\forall x\in M

Conversely for
\mathcal{A}=M_{n}(\mathbb{C})\simeq\mathcal{B}(\mathcal{H})
for a given Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}
of dimension
n
, all automorphisms will be inner.

Taking an algebra
\mathcal{A}
, we have the short exact sequence of groups:

\displaystyle 1\,\longrightarrow\,\Inn(\mathcal{A})\,\longrightarrow\,\Aut(\mathcal{A})\,\longrightarrow\,\Out(\mathcal{A})\,\longrightarrow\,1

from which
3.3.2
can be seen as an infinitesimal version.

In sections
11.5
and
11.6
, we will see that the first kind of automorphisms will be implemented for NCGFT based on derivations and that the second kind will be used for its spectral triples counterpart.

11.4
General Principles of NCGFT and the AC-Manifold

Let’s try to understand how the principle of gauge theory can be implemented using this extended framework. The general procedure (which includes usual gauge theories) will be to:

Find a differential structure linked to degrees of freedom associated with some “space”

Build connection to implement parallel transport along these degrees of freedom

Find an automorphism invariant scalar built from this connection

Now, suppose that we are interested in finding a space for which the diffeomorphism group is a compact Lie group. But they are no such a manifold. As mentioned in the previous section
11.3
, taking a manifold
M
, its diffeomorphism group is equivalent to
\Aut(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))
. Thus, by moving into the algebraic equivalent, a very important observation can be made, automorphisms of algebras can be extended to provide compact Lie groups, these being the automorphisms of matrix algebras. The question then becomes, what is the appropriate structure of “space”
i.e.
algebra that could both contain
\Diff(M)
and the gauge Lie group
\mathcal{G}
(potentially the one of the SMPP) in its automorphisms group?

An answer was given by the almost commutative manifold, alternatively designed by the “space”
\widehat{M}=M\times F
or its algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{F}
,
F
being a finite space. The automorphisms group
\Aut(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
is then equivalent to
\Diff(M)\ltimes\mathcal{G}
, with
\mathcal{G}=Map(M,G)
,
G
being the standard model group
U(1)\times SU(2)\times SU(3)
for example. We recognize the different structures at the heart of the implementation of the gauge principle mentioned in section
10.1

i.e.
the geometric structure
M
, the algebraic structure
\mathcal{A}_{F}
and the global one
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{F}
.

Subsequently, the AC-manifold’s algebra was taken to be the fundamental structure that allowed to obtain the NCSMPP. The first use of the AC-Manifold was made in 1990, in
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990a
)
and
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
)
by M. Dubois-Violette, R. Kerner and J. Madore with the algebra
\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathcal{M})\otimes M_{n}(\mathbb{C})
. This model shows for the first time that gauge theories arise naturally from a NC structure, with the advantage to consider the gauge group as the set of unitary elements of an
∗
-algebra. In addition, it allowed us to understand that Higgs and gauge fields could be gathered in the same non-commutative object, with a more natural interpretation for the Higgs field, the potential of the Higgs being naturally given in the formalism, addressing the problem raised in section
10.2
. Indeed, the Higgs field can be seen as a connection along the internal NC degrees of freedom, its potential is equivalent to both the “Einstein-Hilbert action” and the usual bosonic potential. Higgs scalar then acquires a geometrical interpretation, it gives both a connection in the finite part of the AC-manifold and the fermionic mass matrix. This model was the basis of later developments, and in particular of the NCSMPP mainly developed by A. Connes about which I will speak in chapter
12
.

Remark 11.4.1
.

Notice that we chose the terminology
F
both for the finite space and the fiber. This is because the finite space is a kind of replacement for this fiber in the context of NCG. The finite space is constituted of
n
elements, each one being able to take several values. These elements correspond to the different distinguishable characteristics (like charge) the fermionic field can possess
i.e.
the
i
of the
\psi_{i}(x)
defined in section
10.2
which represents the value taken by the field for the element
i
at spacetime location
x
. Fixing its values for each
i
and
x\in M
will define a section. And if we visit all its potential values for every
i=1\dots n
at a given
x
, then we recover the fiber
F_{x}
defined in section
10.2
. The finite space and the fiber are the same spaces but seen differently by the formalism. We will call
F
the fermionic representation space.

Therefore, doing NCGFT will consist in working with the algebraic counterpart of the usual gauge theory structures defined in section
10.2
, the main equivalences being given in table
11.1
.

Usual gauge field theories:

NCGFT:

(differential geometry)

(algebra)

symmetry group
G
(Lie group)

associative algebra
\mathcal{A}

algebra
C^{\infty}(M)

center of the algebra

vector field/sections on the fiber bundle

finitely generated projective module
\mathcal{M}
on
\mathcal{A}

gauge group: vertical automorphisms
G

gauge group:
\Aut(\mathcal{M})

differential structure: de Rham

differential structure: universal,

derivations, spectral triples

Covariant derivative

NC connection 1-form associated to the

differential structure and based on
\mathcal{M}

Fig. 11.1:
Equivalences between topological and algebraic properties for gauge field theories.

The general procedure will then be to find a differential calculus based on
\mathcal{A}_{F}
or
\mathcal{M}
, then find a notion of connection, and then compute the action functional. Having done this, the road will be taken to obtain more concrete gauge theories, using the AC-manifold’s algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{F}
, and then construct the associated NCGFT which will be called
\text{NCGFT}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}
, the finite algebra
\mathcal{A}_{F}
being the only variable input. Then we will obtain the associated action functional
\mathcal{S}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}
(which is not only a function of
\mathcal{A}_{F}
as we will see), doing this in the derivation framework in section
11.5
and in the spectral triples one in section
11.6
. In chapter
12
, using spectral triples-based NCGFT and taking an appropriate algebra
\mathcal{A}_{F}
, we will see that the action of the SMPP can be extracted, this NCGFT thus being named the NCSMPP.

11.5
Derivation-based NCGFT

As we will see, the derivation’s framework offers a way to do NCGFT, implementing the Higgs boson in its generalized connection, and naturally implementing a SSBM-like mechanism. Initial developments can be found in
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990a
;
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
)
.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital associative algebra equipped with an involution
a\mapsto a^{*}
and let
\mathcal{M}
be a left
\mathcal{A}
-module. A (NC) connection
\nabla
on
\mathcal{M}
is a family of linear maps
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
defined for any
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
such that

1.

\nabla_{f\mathfrak{X}}=f\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}
and
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}+\mathfrak{Y}}=\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}+\nabla_{\mathfrak{Y}}
for any
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
and
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
.

2.

\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}(ae)=(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a)e+a\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}e
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
e\in\mathcal{M}
and
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
.

The connection must be Hermitian:
\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}h(e_{1},e_{2})=h(\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}e_{1},e_{2})+h(e_{1},\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}e_{2})
for any
a_{1},a_{2}\in\mathcal{A}
and
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathcal{M}
.

The curvature of
\nabla
is the family of maps
R(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}):\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
defined for any
e\in\mathcal{M}
and
\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
by

\displaystyle R(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})e\vcentcolon=(\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}\nabla_{\mathfrak{Y}}-\nabla_{\mathfrak{Y}}\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}-\nabla_{[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]})e

It can be easily shown that
R(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})(ae)=aR(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})e
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
so that
R(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})\in\Hom_{\mathcal{A}}(\mathcal{M},\mathcal{M})
(space of homomorphisms of left modules).

A special case of interest is the left module
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
for the multiplication in
\mathcal{A}
equipped with the canonical Hermitian structure
h(a,b)\vcentcolon=ab^{*}
for any
a,b\in\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
. Then, since
\mathcal{A}
is unital, the connection
\nabla
is completely given by its values on the unit
{\text{\char 49\relax}}\in\mathcal{A}
: for any
e\in\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
and any
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
,

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}e=\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}(e{\text{\char 49\relax}})=(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}e)+e(\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}{\text{\char 49\relax}})=(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}e)+e\omega(\mathfrak{X})

where we define
\omega(\mathfrak{X})\vcentcolon=\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}{\text{\char 49\relax}}
. Then one has
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{1}(\mathcal{A})
and
\omega
is called the connection
1
-form of
\nabla
. The compatibility of
\nabla
with
h
implies that for any real derivation
\mathfrak{X}
, one has
\omega(\mathfrak{X})+\omega(\mathfrak{X})^{*}=0
since
0=\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}h({\text{\char 49\relax}},{\text{\char 49\relax}})=\omega(\mathfrak{X})+\omega(\mathfrak{X})^{*}
.

The curvature can be computed in terms of
\omega
as
R(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})e=e\Omega(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})
where

\displaystyle\Omega(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})\vcentcolon=(\text{{d}}\omega)(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})-[\omega(\mathfrak{X}),\omega(\mathfrak{Y})]

using the fact that
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
is also a right
\mathcal{A}
-module. The
2
-form
\Omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{2}(\mathcal{A})
is the curvature
2
-form of
\nabla
.

The gauge group
\mathcal{G}
is the space
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
of unitary elements in
\mathcal{A}
which act on the right on
\mathcal{M}
and respect conditions defined in
11.3.1
. Indeed, any
g\in\mathcal{G}
is defined by its value
u\vcentcolon=g({\text{\char 49\relax}})\in\mathcal{A}
so that
g(e)=g(e{\text{\char 49\relax}})=eg({\text{\char 49\relax}})=eu
. Since
\mathcal{G}
is a group, the element
u
is invertible in
\mathcal{A}
and the unitary condition comes from the compatibility with the Hermitian structure:
{\text{\char 49\relax}}=h({\text{\char 49\relax}},{\text{\char 49\relax}})=h({\text{\char 49\relax}}u,{\text{\char 49\relax}}u)=uu^{\ast}
. A computation shows that the connection
1
-form associated to
\nabla^{g}_{\mathfrak{X}}=g\circ\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}\circ g^{-1}
is given by:

\displaystyle\omega^{u}\vcentcolon=u^{-1}\omega u-u^{-1}(\text{{d}}u)

and its curvature
2
-form is
\Omega^{u}\vcentcolon=u^{-1}\Omega u
. A very simple NC connection is given by
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}^{0}\vcentcolon=a\,\to\,\mathfrak{X}a
.

Proposition 11.5.1
(Transport of connections by automorphisms).

Let us consider the hypothesis of Prop.
3.3.1
.

Let
\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
be an invertible linear map such that
\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}(ae)=\Psi(a)\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}(e)
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
e\in\mathcal{M}
.

Let
\nabla
be a connection on
\mathcal{M}
compatible with a Hermitian structure
h
on
\mathcal{M}
. Then, for any
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
and
e\in\mathcal{M}
, the maps
\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}e\vcentcolon=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)
define a connection on
\mathcal{M}
which is compatible with the Hermitian structure
h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
defined by
h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e_{1},e_{2})\vcentcolon=\Psi\left(h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2}))\right)
. Its curvature
R^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
satisfies
R^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})e=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(R(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}),\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Y}))\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)
where
R
is the curvature of
\nabla
.

Let
g\in\mathcal{G}
be a gauge transformation on
\mathcal{M}
. Then
g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e)\vcentcolon=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)
belongs to
\mathcal{G}
. If
g
is compatible with
h
then
g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
is compatible with
h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
. One has
(\nabla^{g})^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}=(\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}})^{g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}}
.

For
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
, let
\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}=\Psi
. Let
\omega
(resp.
\omega^{\Psi}
) be the connection
1
-form of
\nabla
(resp. of
\nabla^{\Psi}
). Then one has
\omega^{\Psi}=\Psi(\omega)
. Let
u=g({\text{\char 49\relax}})
and
u^{\Psi}=g^{\Psi}({\text{\char 49\relax}})
, then
u^{\Psi}=\Psi(u)
.

Proof

For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
f\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
,
\mathfrak{X}\in\Der(\mathcal{A})
and
e\in\mathcal{M}
, one has

\displaystyle\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}(ae)

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(ae)\right)=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi^{-1}(a)\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left((\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}\Psi^{-1}(a))\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)+\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\Psi^{-1}(a)\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\Psi^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a)\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)+a\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)=(\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}a)e+a\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}e

and

\displaystyle\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{f\mathfrak{X}}e

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(f\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\nabla_{\Psi^{-1}(f)\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\Psi^{-1}(f)\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)

\displaystyle=f\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)=f\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}e

so that
\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
is a connection. The compatibility with
h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
is proved by

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e_{1},e_{2})

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{X}\mathord{\cdot}\Psi\left(h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2}))\right)=\Psi\left(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})\mathord{\cdot}h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2}))\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\Psi\left(h(\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2}))\right)+\Psi\left(h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2}))\right)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\Psi\left(h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2}))\right)+\Psi\left(h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}e_{2}))\right)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}e_{1},e_{2})+h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e_{1},\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}e_{2}).

The relation for the curvature
R^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
is a straightforward computation:

\displaystyle R^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})e

\displaystyle=\left(\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{Y}}-\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{Y}}\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}-\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]}\right)e

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Y})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\\
-\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Y})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)-\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}([\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}])}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Y})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)-\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Y})}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\\
-\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{[\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}),\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Y})]}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(R(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}),\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{Y}))\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)

The map
g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}
is obviously invertible with inverse
(g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}})^{-1}=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}
. It is a morphism of modules:
g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(ae)=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(ae)=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\left(\Psi^{-1}(a)\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\left(\Psi^{-1}(a)g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e)\right)=ag^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e)
. One has

\displaystyle h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e_{1}),g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e_{2}))

\displaystyle=\Psi\left(h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\Psi\left(h(g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2})\right)=\Psi\left(h(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{1}),\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}(e_{2})\right)=h^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}(e_{1},e_{2})

and

\displaystyle(\nabla^{g})^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}

\displaystyle=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla^{g}_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}=\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\circ g^{-1}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1}

\displaystyle=(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1})\!\circ\!(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1})\!\circ\!(\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ g\circ\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}^{-1})^{-1}

\displaystyle=g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}\circ\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}\circ(g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}})^{-1}=(\nabla^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}})^{g^{\Psi_{\text{{Mod}}}}}_{\mathfrak{X}}.

Finally, one has

\displaystyle\omega^{\Psi}(\mathfrak{X})

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\Psi}_{\mathfrak{X}}{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\Psi\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}\Psi^{-1}{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\Psi\circ\nabla_{\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X})}{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\Psi\left(\omega(\Psi_{\Der}^{-1}(\mathfrak{X}))\right)=\Psi(\omega)(\mathfrak{X})

and
u^{\Psi}=g^{\Psi}({\text{\char 49\relax}})=\Psi\circ g\circ\Psi^{-1}({\text{\char 49\relax}})=\Psi\circ g({\text{\char 49\relax}})=\Psi(u)
.

11.5.1
Action in the Derivation-based Framework

To define a gauge field theory on
\mathcal{M}
, one considers the “fields” defining a connection
\nabla
on
\mathcal{M}
and a Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}(\nabla)
for these fields. This Lagrangian is usually constructed for the left module
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
using a Hodge star operator
\star
on the space of forms on
\mathcal{A}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(\nabla)\vcentcolon=-\Omega\wedge\star\Omega.

Then, using a trace
\int_{\mathcal{A}}
(which sends forms to scalars) we can define an action:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}[\nabla]\vcentcolon=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{L}(\nabla)=-\int_{\mathcal{A}}\Omega\wedge\star\Omega

the sign is necessary for positivity. The matter Lagrangian can be defined in a similar way. One first considers
\nabla
as a map
\nabla:\mathcal{M}\to\Omega_{\Der}^{1}(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M}
. Using a natural involution on
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
which extends the involution on
\mathcal{A}
(see
(
François et al., 2014
)
for instance), one can extend
h
to
(\Omega_{\Der}^{p}(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M})\otimes(\Omega_{\Der}^{q}(\mathcal{A})\otimes_{\mathcal{A}}\mathcal{M})\to\Omega_{\Der}^{p+q}(\mathcal{A})
by
h(\omega_{p}\otimes e,\omega_{q}\otimes e^{\prime})\vcentcolon=\omega_{p}h(e,e^{\prime})\wedge\omega_{q}^{*}
. Then
\int_{\mathcal{A}}h(\nabla e,\star\nabla e)
defines a Klein-Gordon type action for matter fields
e\in\mathcal{M}
.

Since we restrict our analysis to matrix algebras, we refer to Sect.
4.1.1
for the construction of an explicit Hodge star operator and a trace.

Let us describe the degrees of freedom in the gauge sector of a NCGFT defined on
M_{n}
and on
C^{\infty}(M)\otimes M_{n}
, see
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990a
;
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
;
Dubois-Violette and Masson, 1998
;
Masson, 2012
;
François et al., 2014
)
for some details. We use some notations from Sect.
4.1.1
.

11.5.2
Example of
\mathcal{A}=M_{n}

Let us consider
\mathcal{A}=M_{n}
. Then
\Der(\mathcal{A})=\Der(M_{n})\simeq\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
, and, for
k=1,\ldots,n^{2}-1
, let
\{E_{k}\}
be a basis of anti-Hermitean traceless matrices in
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
so that
\{\partial_{k}\vcentcolon=\ad_{E_{k}}\}
is a basis of real derivations of
M_{n}
.
1
1

1

We depart here from the conventions in many papers where the
E_{k}
are chosen to be Hermitean and
\partial_{k}
are defined as
\ad_{iE_{k}}
.
Let us consider the left module
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
with the canonical Hermitian structure
h(a,b)\vcentcolon=ab^{*}
. There is a canonical connection
\mathring{\nabla}
on
\mathcal{M}
defined by
\mathring{\nabla}_{\partial_{k}}a\vcentcolon=E_{k}a
for any
k=1,\ldots,n^{2}-1
and
a\in\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{M}
with connection
1
-form
\mathring{\omega}(\partial_{k})=E_{k}
. This canonical connection satisfies two important properties: firstly, its curvature is zero; secondly, it is gauge invariant (see also
(
Cagnache et al., 2011a
)
for another occurrence of such a canonical connection).
It is then convenient to compare any connection
1
-form
\omega
on
\mathcal{M}
to this canonical connection, by writing
\omega=\omega_{k}\theta^{k}=\mathring{\omega}-B_{k}\theta^{k}=(E_{k}-B_{k})\theta^{k}
. Then the curvature
2
-form
\Omega=\tfrac{1}{2}\Omega_{k\ell}\theta^{k}\wedge\theta^{\ell}
has components
\Omega_{k\ell}\vcentcolon=\Omega(\partial_{k},\partial_{\ell})=-([B_{k},B_{\ell}]-C_{k\ell}^{m}B_{m})
. This curvature vanishes iff
E_{k}\mapsto B_{k}
is a representation of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
(for instance
B_{k}=0
or
B_{k}=E_{k}
). The connection
\omega
is compatible with
h
iff
\omega_{k}+\omega^{*}_{k}=0
for any
k
. Since the
E_{k}
’s are anti-Hermitean, this compatibility condition is equivalent to
B_{k}+B_{k}^{*}=0
for any
k
and then
\Omega_{k\ell}^{*}=-\Omega_{k\ell}
. We can then decompose
B_{k}=B_{k}^{\ell}E_{\ell}+iB_{k}^{0}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}
with real functions
B_{k}^{\ell}
,
\ell=0,\ldots,n^{2}-1
, so that the number of degrees of freedom (number of real functions) in
\omega
is
n^{2}(n^{2}-1)
. The action of a gauge transformation
g\in U(n)
induces the transformation
B_{k}\mapsto g^{-1}B_{k}g
(the inhomogeneous part of the gauge transformation is absorbed by
\mathring{\omega}
).

Notice that this approach is only interesting for
n\geq 2
since for
n=1
,
M_{1}=\mathbb{C}
is commutative and so there is no derivation and so no degree of freedom
B_{k}
’s.

Suppose that the basis
\{\partial_{k}\}
is orthonormal for the metric
g
defined as in Sect.
4.1.1
. Since
\Omega\wedge\star\Omega=\tfrac{1}{2}\Omega_{k\ell}\Omega^{k\ell}\omega_{\text{{vol}}}
for
\Omega^{k\ell}=g^{kk^{\prime}}g^{\ell\ell^{\prime}}\Omega_{k^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}}
, the action is then
-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k,\ell}\tr(\Omega_{k\ell})^{2}=-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{k,\ell}\tr([B_{k},B_{\ell}]-C_{k\ell}^{m}B_{m})^{2}
. Notice that
-\tr(\Omega_{k\ell})^{2}=\tr(\Omega_{k\ell}\Omega_{k\ell}^{*})\geq 0
.

From Prop.
11.5.1
and Examples
3.3.2
and
4.1.1
, an inner automorphism defined by a unitary element
u
in
M_{n}
produces a transport of all the structures defining the NCGFT on
M_{n}
. One has
\omega^{u}=U_{k}^{\ell}u\omega_{\ell}u^{-1}\theta^{k}
, and since
\mathring{\omega}^{u}=U_{k}^{\ell}uE_{\ell}u^{-1}\theta^{k}=\mathring{\omega}
, this implies that
B_{k}
is mapped to
B^{u}_{k}=U_{k}^{\ell}uB_{\ell}u^{-1}
. One then has
[B^{u}_{k},B^{u}_{\ell}]-C_{k\ell}^{m}B^{u}_{m}=U_{k}^{k^{\prime}}U_{\ell}^{\ell^{\prime}}u^{-1}\left([B_{k^{\prime}},B_{\ell^{\prime}}]-C_{k^{\prime}\ell^{\prime}}^{m^{\prime}}B_{m^{\prime}}\right)u
and the Lagrangian in the
B^{u}_{k}
is the same as the Lagrangian in the
B_{k}
. We conclude that such an action of inner automorphisms is not relevant from a physical point of view.

11.5.3
Example of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes M_{n}

Let us consider the algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes M_{n}
for a manifold
M
. The space of derivations is
\Der(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})=[\Gamma(M)\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}]\oplus[C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathfrak{sl}_{n}]
where
\Gamma(M)=\Der(C^{\infty}(M))
is the space of vector fields on
M
, and
\mathcal{Z}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})=C^{\infty}(M)
. For
\mu=1,\ldots,\dim M
, let
\{\partial_{\mu}\}
(usual partial derivatives in a coordinate system given by a chart of
M
) be a basis of real derivations on the geometric part, and let
\{\text{{d}}x^{\mu}\}
be the dual basis of
1
-forms. We can define a metric on this almost commutative manifold:

\displaystyle\hat{g}(\partial_{\mu}+\partial_{\alpha},\partial_{\nu}+\partial_{\beta})=\tilde{g}(\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu})+g(\partial_{\alpha},\partial_{\beta})=\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}+g_{k\ell}

where we recognize
\tilde{g}_{\mu\nu}
the usual metric for manifold, and
g_{k\ell}
the metric for the inner degrees of freedom defined in section
4.1
.

Let us consider as before the left module
\mathcal{M}=\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
with the canonical Hermitian structure
h(a,b)\vcentcolon=ab^{*}
. Then a connection
1
-form
\omega
can be written as
\omega=\omega_{\mu}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}+\omega_{k}\theta^{k}=A_{\mu}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}+(E_{k}-B_{k})\theta^{k}
with
A_{\mu},B_{k}\in\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and this connection is compatible with
h
when
A_{\mu}+A_{\mu}^{*}=0
and
B_{k}+B_{k}^{*}=0
(since the
E_{k}
’s are anti-Hermitean). As before, we can decompose
A_{\mu}=A_{\mu}^{\ell}E_{\ell}+iA_{\mu}^{0}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}
and
B_{k}=B_{k}^{\ell}E_{\ell}+iB_{k}^{0}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}
so that the number of degrees of freedom in
\omega
is
n^{2}(\dim M+n^{2}-1)
. A gauge transformation given by
g\in C^{\infty}(M)\otimes U(n)
induces the transformations
A_{\mu}\mapsto g^{-1}A_{\mu}g-g^{-1}\text{{d}}_{\text{dR}}g
and
B_{k}\mapsto g^{-1}B_{k}g
where
\text{{d}}_{\text{dR}}
is the ordinary de Rham differential on
M
(to simplify, we used the notation
\text{{d}}x^{\mu}
instead of
\text{{d}}_{\text{dR}}x^{\mu}
). So
A_{\mu}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}
can be identified with an ordinary
U(n)
-connection.

The curvature of
\omega
can be decomposed into three parts:
\Omega=\tfrac{1}{2}\Omega_{\mu\nu}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}\wedge\text{{d}}x^{\nu}+\Omega_{\mu k}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}\wedge\theta^{k}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Omega_{k\ell}\theta^{k}\wedge\theta^{\ell}
with

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}-[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}],

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mu k}

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{\mu}B_{k}-[A_{\mu},B_{k}]),

\displaystyle\Omega_{k\ell}

\displaystyle=-([B_{k},B_{\ell}]-C_{k\ell}^{m}B_{m}).

The term
\Omega_{\mu\nu}
is the usual field strength of
A_{\mu}
,
\Omega_{\mu k}
is (up to a sign) the covariant derivative of
B_{k}
along the connection
A_{\mu}
and
\Omega_{k\ell}
is the expression obtained for the algebra
\mathcal{A}=M_{n}
. Using natural notions of metric and Hodge
\star
-operator in this context, a natural Lagrangian is the sum of 3 (positive) terms
-\tfrac{1}{2}\tr(\Omega_{\mu\nu}\Omega^{\mu\nu})-\tr(\Omega_{\mu k}\Omega^{\mu k})-\tfrac{1}{2}\tr(\Omega_{k\ell}\Omega^{k\ell})
. Finding a minimal configuration for such a Lagrangian is equivalent to minimizing independently these 3 terms. The last one vanishes if and only if
E_{k}\mapsto B_{k}
is a representation of
\mathfrak{sl}_{n}
. One possibility is the take
B_{k}=0
for all
k
(referred to as the “null-configuration” in the following), which cancels also the second term. Then one reduces the theory to massless gauge fields
A_{\mu}
. Another more stimulating configuration is to consider
B_{k}=E_{k}
(referred to as the “basis-configuration” in the following), and then the second term reduces to
-\tr([A_{\mu},E_{k}][A^{\mu},E^{k}])
, which, after developing, produces mass terms for the
A_{\mu}
fields, see Lemma
11.5.2
. This is similar to the SSBM implemented in the SMPP to give masses to some gauge fields.

Notice that for an ordinary Yang-Mills theory in the framework of fiber bundles and connections, with structure group
U(n)
, we have only the fields
A_{\mu}^{\ell}
,
\ell=0,\ldots,n^{2}-1
since there is no “algebraic part” which produces the
B_{k}
’s. With the structure group
SU(n)
, there is no field
A_{\mu}^{0}
(the matrices
E_{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\ldots,n^{2}-1
, generate the real Lie algebra
\mathfrak{su}(n)
).

Contrary to Example
11.5.2
, this case is also interesting for
n=1
. In that case, the degrees of freedom are only in the spatial direction (the
A_{\mu}
’s) and they can be used to construct an ordinary
U(1)
gauge field theory.

Once again, one can ask about the action of an inner automorphism defined by a unitary element in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
. The action of such an automorphism on a “spatial” derivation
X\in\Gamma(M)
is given by
\Psi_{\Der}(X)=X+\ad_{u(X\mathord{\cdot}u^{-1})}
(see Example
3.3.2
). If
u
is a unitary in
M_{n}
(not depending on
M
), then one gets
\Psi_{\Der}(X)=X
. This implies that the spatial directions (the
\partial_{\mu}
’s) are only affected by
u
through the action of
\Psi
,
\omega^{u}_{\mu}=u\omega_{\mu}u^{-1}
, while the “inner” directions (the
\partial_{k}
’s) change according to the rules given in Example
4.1.1
. This implies that the Lagrangian in the new fields is the same as the one in the original fields and so such an action of inner automorphism is not relevant from a physical point of view. When
u
is depending on
M
, the second term in
\Psi_{\Der}(X)
does not vanish and it produces mixing between spatial directions and inner directions: some degrees of freedom in the
B_{k}
’s are sent in the spatial part
\omega^{u}_{\mu}
. This situation will not be considered in the following.

Lemma 11.5.2
.

Let us consider a NCGFT as previously given. In the basis-configuration for the
B_{k}
’s, the masses induced on the fields
A_{\mu}^{\ell}
for
\ell=1,\dots,n^{2}-1
, in the decomposition
A_{\mu}=A_{\mu}^{\ell}E_{\ell}+iA_{\mu}^{0}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}
, are all the same and equal to
m_{\text{basis-config}}=\sqrt{2n}
, while the field
A_{\mu}^{0}
is mass-less.

Proof

Using the metric
g
defined as
g(E_{k},E_{\ell})=\tr(E_{k}E_{\ell})
(see Sect.
4.1.1
), the masses for the fields
A_{\mu}^{\ell}
,
\ell=1,\dots,n^{2}-1
, are given by the term

\displaystyle M^{2}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}A_{\mu}^{\ell_{1}}A^{\mu,\ell_{2}}

\displaystyle=-g^{k_{1}k_{2}}\tr([A_{\mu},E_{k_{1}}][A^{\mu},E_{k_{2}}])=-A_{\mu}^{\ell_{1}}A^{\mu,\ell_{2}}g^{k_{1}k_{2}}\tr([E_{\ell_{1}},E_{k_{1}}][E_{\ell_{2}},E_{k_{2}}])

\displaystyle=-A_{\mu}^{\ell_{1}}A^{\mu,\ell_{2}}g^{k_{1}k_{2}}C_{\ell_{1}k_{1}}^{m_{1}}C_{\ell_{2}k_{2}}^{m_{2}}\tr(E_{m_{1}}E_{m_{2}})=-A_{\mu}^{\ell_{1}}A^{\mu,\ell_{2}}g^{k_{1}k_{2}}g_{m_{1}m_{2}}C_{\ell_{1}k_{1}}^{m_{1}}C_{\ell_{2}k_{2}}^{m_{2}}

where
A_{\mu}=A_{\mu}^{\ell}E_{\ell}+iA_{\mu}^{0}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n}
. Since the field
A_{\mu}^{0}
disappears, its mass is
0
.

For any
X,Y\in\mathfrak{su}(n)
, the Killing form
K(X,Y)=\tr(\ad_{X}\circ\ad_{Y})
satisfies
K(X,Y)=2n\tr(XY)
so that, on the one hand,
K_{k\ell}\vcentcolon=K(E_{k},E_{\ell})=2n\,g_{k\ell}
and on the other hand,
K_{k\ell}=C_{km}^{n}C_{\ell n}^{m}
. Let us define
C_{k\ell m}\vcentcolon=g_{mn}C_{k\ell}^{n}
, so that
C_{k\ell}^{n}=g^{mn}C_{k\ell m}
and
C_{k\ell m}
is completely antisymmetric in
(k,\ell,m)
. This leads to
g^{k_{1}k_{2}}g_{m_{1}m_{2}}C_{\ell_{1}k_{1}}^{m_{1}}C_{\ell_{2}k_{2}}^{m_{2}}=g^{k_{1}k_{2}}C_{\ell_{1}k_{1}m}C_{\ell_{2}k_{2}}^{m}=-g^{k_{1}k_{2}}C_{\ell_{1}mk_{1}}C_{\ell_{2}k_{2}}^{m}=-C_{\ell_{1}m}^{k}C_{\ell_{2}k}^{m}
, so that
M^{2}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}=C_{\ell_{1}m}^{k}C_{\ell_{2}k}^{m}=K_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}=2n\,g_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}}
. This proves that the diagonalization of
(M^{2}_{\ell_{1}\ell_{2}})
gives a unique eigenvalue
2n
so that there is a unique mass
m_{\text{basis-config}}=\sqrt{2n}
.

Remark 11.5.3
.

The way to do NC gauge theories in the derivation framework can be seen as a geometrization of the generators
T_{a}
of the gauge potential introduced in chapter
10
. Making this object being at the same level of the
\partial_{\mu}
, in the covariant derivative (
10.1.2
) which becomes even more similar to a simple derivation, in a NCG. This looks like a nice and direct implementation of NCG in the gauge field theory framework.

This model lacks a fermionic action. More details can be found in
(
Masson, 2008a
;
François et al., 2014
)
.

11.6
Spectral Triple-based NCGFT

For models using spectral triples, we take the module to be a Hilbert space
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{H}
. As we will see, here the connection is obtained from the inner fluctuations of the Dirac operator. As the elaboration of spectral triples is intimately linked to the elaboration of a NCSMPP, we let the presentation of the history of the elaboration of spectral triples based NCGFT to chapter
12
.

Two spectral triples
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D,J,\gamma)
and
(\mathcal{A}^{\prime},\mathcal{H}^{\prime},D^{\prime},J^{\prime},\gamma^{\prime})
are unitary equivalent when
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}
, and there exists a unitary operator
U:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{\prime}
and an algebra isomorphism
\Lambda:\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\circ\Lambda=U\pi U^{-1}
,
D^{\prime}=UDU^{-1}
,
J^{\prime}=UJU^{-1}
, and
\gamma^{\prime}=U\gamma U^{-1}
.

A symmetry of a spectral triple is a unitary equivalence between two spectral triples such that
\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=\mathcal{H}
,
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}=\mathcal{A}
, and
\pi^{\prime}=\pi
, so that
U:\mathcal{H}\to\mathcal{H}
and
\Lambda\in\Aut(\mathcal{A})
,
i.e.
a symmetry acts only on
D
,
J
and
\gamma
. In the following, we will only consider automorphisms
\Lambda
which are
\mathcal{A}
-inner, that is, there is a unitary
u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
such that
\Lambda_{u}(a)=uau^{\ast}
. This unitary in
\mathcal{A}
defines the unitary
U=\pi(u)J\pi(u)J^{-1}:\mathcal{H}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}
, which can be interpreted as the conjugation with
\pi(u)
for the bimodule structure. A straightforward computation shows that such
U
acts as an automorphism on
\mathcal{A}
, leaves
J
and
\gamma
invariant:

\displaystyle UaU^{-1}=\pi(u)\pi(u)^{\circ}a(\pi(u)^{\circ})^{*}\pi(u)^{*}=\pi(u)a\pi(u)^{*}\pi(u)^{\circ}(\pi(u)^{\circ})^{*}=\pi(u)a\pi(u)^{*}=\Lambda_{\pi(u)}(a)

\displaystyle UJU^{-1}=\pi(u)J\pi(u)(\pi(u)^{*}(\pi(u)^{*})^{\circ})=\pi(u)JJ\pi(u)^{*}J^{-1}=\epsilon J^{-1}=J

\displaystyle U\gamma U^{-1}=\epsilon^{\prime\prime 2}\gamma UU^{-1}=\gamma

and modifies the Dirac operator
D
as:

D^{u}=UDU^{*}=D+\pi(u)[D,\pi(u)^{\ast}]+\epsilon^{\prime}J\left(\pi(u)[D,\pi(u)^{\ast}]\right)J^{-1}.

(11.6.1)

The usual way to look at this relation is to interpret the commutator with
D
as a kind of differential: this expression tells us that
D
is modified by the addition of two inhomogeneous terms of the form “
\pi(u)\text{{d}}\pi(u)^{-1}
”.

By definition, gauge transformations are inner symmetries of a spectral triple. In order to compensate for the inhomogeneous terms, we can use the same trick as in ordinary gauge field theory: add to the first order differential operator
D
a gauge potential. To do that, we need a convenient notion of NC connections.

We now suppose that there is an orthogonal decomposition of the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{H}_{i}
such that the representation decomposes along
\pi=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\pi_{i}
where
\pi_{i}
is a representation of
\mathcal{A}_{i}
on
\mathcal{H}_{i}
: for any
\psi=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}
and
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
,
\pi(a)\psi=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\pi_{i}(a_{i})\psi_{i}
. Then the Dirac operator
D
decomposes as a
r\times r
matrix of operators
D_{j}^{i}:\mathcal{H}_{i}\to\mathcal{H}_{j}
.
We propose to write the representation
\pi_{D}
as follows. Let us consider the universal differential calculus
(\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A}),\text{{d}}_{U})
and consider any
\bm{\omega}\in\mathbf{\Omega}^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\subset\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
which decomposes along a sum of typical terms
\toplus_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}=1}^{r}\big(a^{0}_{i}\otimes a^{1}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n-1}_{i_{n-1}}\otimes a^{n}_{j}\big)_{i,j=1}^{r}\in\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
. Then
\pi_{D}(\bm{\omega})
is the
r\times r
matrix of operators

\displaystyle\pi_{D}(\bm{\omega})_{i}^{j}=\!\!\!\!\!\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{all terms at the}\\
\text{$(i,j)$ entry in $\bm{\omega}$}\end{subarray}}\!\!\!\!\!\tsum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}=1}^{r}a^{0}_{i}D_{i}^{i_{1}}a^{1}_{i_{1}}D_{i_{1}}^{i_{2}}\cdots D_{i_{n-2}}^{i_{n-1}}a^{n-1}_{i_{n-1}}D_{i_{n-1}}^{j}a^{n}_{j}:\mathcal{H}_{j}\to\mathcal{H}_{i}

(11.6.2)

Notice that, since
\bm{\omega}\in\mathbf{\Omega}^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
, these sums define bounded operators because only commutators
[D,a]
could appear in
\pi_{D}(\bm{\omega})
(this is not necessarily the case for a generic element in
\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}
).

As mentioned in section
11.2
, a NC connection is defined as a
1
-form
\omega=\sum_{i}a^{0}_{i}\text{{d}}_{U}a^{1}_{i}\in\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
(finite sum). Elements in the vector spaces
\Omega^{n}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
can be represented as bounded operators on
\mathcal{H}
by

\pi_{D}\big(\tsum_{i}a^{0}_{i}\text{{d}}_{U}a^{1}_{i}\cdots\text{{d}}_{U}a^{n}_{i}\big)\vcentcolon=\tsum_{i}\pi(a^{0}_{i})[D,\pi(a^{1}_{i})]\cdots[D,\pi(a^{n}_{i})].

Notice that the map
\pi_{D}
is not a representation of the graded differential algebra
\Omega^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
. In particular,
\text{{d}}_{U}
is not represented by the commutator
[D,-]
as a differential. The representation
\pi_{D}
can also be used to represent
n
-forms on the right module structure of
\mathcal{H}
by
\tsum_{i}a^{0}_{i}\text{{d}}_{U}a^{1}_{i}\cdots\text{{d}}_{U}a^{n}_{i}\mapsto J\pi_{D}\left(\tsum_{i}a^{0}_{i}\text{{d}}_{U}a^{1}_{i}\cdots\text{{d}}_{U}a^{n}_{i}\right)J^{-1}
. The map
\pi_{D}
may have a non trivial kernel, this is why we will prefer to use
\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
instead of
\pi_{D}(\omega)
in some forthcoming constructions. We notice that the term
\pi(u)[D,\pi(u)^{\ast}]
in equation (
11.6.1
) can be interpreted as a particular connection.

Given
D
and
\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
, one defines the operator
D_{\omega}\vcentcolon=D+\pi_{D}(\omega)+\epsilon^{\prime}J\pi_{D}(\omega)J^{-1}
called the fluctuated Dirac operator. Where
\pi_{D}(\omega)
is an element of the space of Connes differential 1-forms
\Omega_{D}^{1}(\mathcal{A})
. By a gauge transformation
u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
,
D_{\omega}
is transformed into

\displaystyle(D_{\omega})^{u}=D+\pi(u)\pi_{D}(\omega)\pi(u)^{\ast}+\pi(u)[D,\pi(u)^{\ast}]+\epsilon^{\prime}J\pi(u)\pi_{D}(\omega)\pi(u)^{\ast}J^{-1}+\epsilon^{\prime}J\pi(u)[D,\pi(u)^{\ast}]J^{-1}.

This relation can be written as
D_{\omega^{u}}
, where
\omega^{u}\in\Omega^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A})
is a gauge transformation of
\omega
defined as
\omega^{u}\vcentcolon=u\omega u^{\ast}+u\text{{d}}_{U}u^{\ast}
.

This is how a gauge field (in the general meaning of the term) is extracted from the Dirac when it is undergoing inner fluctuation. Therefore, considering
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{F}
we see that
\Out(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M))
the group of Outer automorphisms of
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is enhanced by the inner automorphisms of
\mathcal{A}_{F}
. As the metric comes out from the Dirac, these new automorphisms give us deformation of the Dirac and then of metric for the spectral geometry of
(\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}},D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}})
, these deformations are interpreted as the gauge fields of Yang-Mills theory.

Thus, any real spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D,J,\gamma)
possesses a gauge group deduced from the unitaries of
\mathcal{A}
, linking this triple to other triples wherein the algebra is unitary equivalent to
\mathcal{A}
and where the Hilbert space is left unchanged. The gauge group of the triple can be defined as follows:

Definition 11.6.1
(Gauge group of a spectral triple).

We define by
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H};J)
the gauge groupe of the given spectral triple by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H};J)=\{U=uJuJ^{-1}|u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})\}

It is connected to the gauge group acting on
\omega
by the unitary
u:\omega\mapsto u\omega u^{*}+u\left[D,u^{*}\right]
. The element
U=uJuJ^{-1}
corresponds to the composition of the left and right actions
of
u
on
\mathcal{H}
:
U\,:\,\xi\,\to\,u.\xi.(u^{*})^{\circ}
which is called the adjoint action
of
u
.

Taking a spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D,J,\gamma)
, it is possible to construct another spectral triple with a given commutative subalgebra of
\mathcal{A}
. Let’s define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}_{J}\vcentcolon=\{\,a\,\in\,\mathcal{A}\,|\,aJ=Ja^{*}\}

Proposition 11.6.2
.

Then
(\mathcal{A}_{J},\mathcal{H},D,J,\gamma)
is also a spectral triple, we have that is
a^{*}\in\mathcal{A}_{J}
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}_{J}
, that
\mathcal{A}_{J}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A})
, that for any
\omega\in\Omega_{D}^{1}(\mathcal{A})
:
[a,\omega]=0
, and
\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H};J)=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})/\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{J})
.

Proof

If we take
a\in\mathcal{A}_{J}
and
b\in\mathcal{A}
then:

\displaystyle J^{-1}a^{*}J=(J^{-1}aJ)^{*}=(a^{*})^{*}=a\qquad\qquad\text{and then}\qquad a^{*}J=Ja

Using the fact that
a=J^{-1}a^{*}J
we have
JaJ^{-1}=a^{*}
and then
a\epsilon^{2}=a=Ja^{*}J^{-1}
, so that:

\displaystyle[a,b]=[Ja^{*}J^{-1},b]=0

thanks to commutant property. The fact that
\mathcal{A}_{J}
commutes with
\Omega_{D}^{1}(\mathcal{A})
is a direct consequence of the first order condition. For the last point, if we consider the surjective map
\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})\,\to\,\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H};J)
given by
u\,\to\,uJuJ^{-1}
, then this map is a group morphism with kernel given by:

\displaystyle\{u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})\,|\,uJuJ^{-1}={\text{\char 49\relax}}\,\leftrightarrow\,uJ=Ju^{*}\}=\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A}_{J})

which proves the last point.

Because of the Gelfand-Naimark theorem,
\mathcal{A}_{F}
can be seen as a subalgebra of
\mathcal{C}(X)
,
X
being a background space.

11.6.1
Dirac Operator on a Spin Manifold

No one fully understands spinors. Their algebra is formally understood but their general significance is mysterious. In some sense they describe the ’square root’ of geometry and, just as understanding the square root of -1 took centuries, the same might be true of spinors.

M. Atiyah

The motivation of this subsection is to introduce the structures behind GR from the NCG framework point of view and introduce some notations that will be important in subsection
11.6.2
and then chapter
12.1
.

Let’s consider the tangent bundle
T(M)
over
M
, as said the Levi-Civita connection
\nabla
is the only connection on
T(M)
which is both compatible with the inner product defined by the metric, and torsion-free. It can be written in local coordinate as
\nabla_{\partial_{\mu}}(\partial_{\nu})=\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}
, with
\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}
the Christoffel’s symbols.

Let
E
be a vector bundle over
M
, and
\nabla^{E}
the associated connection, the associated Laplacian is given by:

\displaystyle\Delta^{E}=-g^{\mu\nu}(\nabla_{\mu}^{E}\nabla_{\nu}^{E}-\Gamma_{\mu\nu}^{\lambda}\nabla_{\lambda}^{E}).

(11.6.3)

Now let
M
be a Riemannian spin
c
manifold,
S
be the spinor bundle define on
M
, and
\{e_{i}(x)\}
be a local orthonormal (
g(e_{i},e_{j})=\delta_{ij}
) basis of
\Gamma(T(M))|_{x}
. In this basis, the Christoffel symbols became
\nabla_{e_{i}}=\Gamma_{\mu i}^{j}dx_{\mu}\otimes e_{j}
. The spin connection
\nabla^{S}
is defined to be a lift of the Levi-Civita connection to the spinor bundle:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}^{S}=\partial_{\mu}-\frac{1}{4}\Gamma_{\mu i}^{j}\gamma^{i}\gamma_{j}

It acts on elements
\psi(x)
of the Hilbert space. Then, the Dirac operator
D_{M}\,:\,\Gamma^{\infty}(S)\,\to\,\Gamma^{\infty}(S)
on
M
is given by the composition of the spin connection with Clifford multiplication
c
by
D_{M}=-i\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{S}
.

Remark 11.6.3
.

Taking
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)
, the Dirac operator
D_{M}
is linked to the usual differential:

\displaystyle[D_{M},f]\psi=-ic(\nabla^{S}(f\psi)-f\nabla^{S}\psi)=-ic(df)\psi

As wanted, this Dirac operator connects to the Laplacian, taking
s=R_{\mu\nu}g^{\mu\nu}
the scalar curvature, and the Laplacian of the spin bundle
\Delta^{S}
defined according to the equation (
11.6.3
), we have that:

\displaystyle D_{M}^{2}=\Delta^{S}+\frac{1}{4}s.

(11.6.4)

The scalar product on
M
is defined as follows, taking the fiber of
S
above
x\in M
, we define
\langle\,,\,\rangle_{x}
the scalar product parameterized by
M
, and which varies continuously along
M
. Then we have the following scalar product on
\Gamma(S)
:

\displaystyle(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=\int_{M}\langle\psi_{1}(x),\psi_{2}(x)\rangle_{x}\sqrt{\det g}dx

(11.6.5)

The completion of
\Gamma(S)
