for the scalar product
(\,,\,)
is called the space of square integrable spinors, and will be denoted
L^{2}(S)
. As mentioned in
(
Besnard and Brouder, 2021
;
Lawson and Michelsohn, 2016
)
, the scalar product defined in (
11.6.5
) is not defined for pseudo Riemannian manifolds. More details can be found in
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
.

11.6.2
Action in the Spectral Triples Framework, case of the AC-Manifold

Math and music are intimately related. Not necessarily on a conscious level, but sure.

S. Sondheim

Given a spectral triple
(\mathcal{A},\mathcal{H},D)
it is possible to extract spectral invariants from this structure. These quantities do not vary under the action of unitaries, and thus of the gauge transformations. We will retain two invariants here, for their capacity to give precisely the action of the standard model coupled to gravity, as we will see in chapter
12
when an adequate spectral triple is chosen.

The first invariant, originally introduced by A. Chamseddine and A. Connes in
(
Chamseddine and Connes, 1997
)
is called the bosonic spectral action. Given
D_{\omega}=D+\omega+\epsilon^{\prime}J\omega J^{-1}
the (bosonic) spectral action is defined by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{b}[\omega]

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\Tr f(D_{\omega}/\Lambda)

(11.6.6)

where
f:\mathbb{R}\,\to\,\mathbb{R}
is a positive and even function which decay at
\pm\infty
and provided a trace class operator,
\Lambda\in\mathbb{R}
being a cutoff parameter. This is the only natural spectral invariant that respects additivity. More conditions on
f
can be founded in
(
Eckstein and Iochum, 2018
)
and
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
.

The second one will be called the fermionic spectral action. In the even case, when the spectral triple is equipped with
\gamma
and
J
operators, for any
\widetilde{\psi}\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
, where
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{+}
corresponds to Grassmann vectors associated to vectors
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{+}=\ker(\gamma-1)
(even elements in
\mathcal{H}
), the fermionic spectral action is defined by:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{f}[\omega,\widetilde{\psi}]

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\frac{1}{2}\langle J\widetilde{\psi},D_{\omega}\widetilde{\psi}\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}

(11.6.7)

Proposition 11.6.4
.

The fermionic action is invariant under gauge transformation.

Proof

Taking
U=uJuJ^{-1}
, we have
\mathcal{S}_{f}[\omega^{u},U\widetilde{\psi}]=\frac{1}{2}\langle JU\widetilde{\psi},UD_{\omega}U^{*}U\widetilde{\psi}\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}=\frac{1}{2}\langle JU\widetilde{\psi},UD_{\omega}\widetilde{\psi}\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}=\frac{1}{2}\langle UJ\widetilde{\psi},UD_{\omega}\widetilde{\psi}\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}=\mathcal{S}_{f}[\omega,\widetilde{\psi}]
.

For the bosonic action a “good” function
f
can be taken to be
f:D_{\omega}\,\to\,\exp(-tD_{\omega}^{2})
.

Remark 11.6.5
.

It is interesting to note that this invariant involves a function of the Laplacian constructed from the Dirac. This way of producing characteristic invariants of an underlying geometry is not new. As mentioned in section
8.4
, the study of the eigenvalues of differential operators such as the Laplacian defined on a manifold (to see what geometric data they contain) was at the heart of investigations in the field of Spectral geometry. The spectral action can therefore be seen as a way to sum the eigenvalues of the Laplacian, these eigenvalues reflecting objective physical quantities, such as energy, that are conserved during unitary transformations. As mentioned in section
8.4
, this operator is both connected to the geometry of the underlying space and to the stable planar waves allowed in that geometry. One can therefore interpret this spectral action as a means of summing up the possible stables modes of the fields in the geometry in question, these modes being linked to energies. It is thus quite credible that this action corresponds to a physical invariant. An advantage of the spectral triple approach is that it allows us to completely characterize the underlying geometry in the commutative case at least, unlike the original work done in spectral geometry on Riemannian manifolds where the same spectrum of the Laplacian could correspond to two different manifolds. What is very curious is that, as we will see in chapter
12
, this action provides the Lagrangian of the SMPP whereas the latter is made from the curvatures of the connections, which have a completely different interpretation as seen in section
10.1
.

To compute
\mathcal{S}_{b}
, an asymptotic expansion of this action can be computed from the existence of its heat kernel expansion, taking the limit
t\,\to\,0
:

\displaystyle\Tr(\exp(-tH))\sim\sum_{i}t^{i}c_{i}

All the formulas used here work also if we take
D
instead of
D_{\omega}
, but the induced spectral action is the one that leads to the SMPP. More analytic details can be found in
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
and
(
Eckstein and Iochum, 2018
)
.

It is interesting now to see what form this action takes in the case of an almost commutative manifold
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{F}
. The corresponding spectral triple can be seen as the tensor product of
(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(S),D_{M},J_{M},\gamma_{M})
with
(\mathcal{A}_{F},\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},\gamma_{\mathcal{A}_{F}})
defined by:

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{F},\,\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=L^{2}(S)\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},\,D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}+\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},

\displaystyle J_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=J_{M}\otimes J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},\,\gamma_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=\gamma_{M}\otimes\gamma_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}).

It is important to emphasize that given two real spectral triples, their product will not always be a spectral triple. For example, if we take the two triplets
(\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M),L^{2}(S),D_{M},J_{M},\gamma_{M})
and
(\mathcal{A}_{F},\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},\gamma_{\mathcal{A}_{F}})

with corresponding
(\epsilon_{M},\epsilon^{\prime}_{M},\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{M})
and
(\epsilon_{F},\epsilon^{\prime}_{F},\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{F})
, then a direct calculation shows that the signs
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime})
of the almost commutative manifold are defined if this relations are satisfied:

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{\prime}_{M}=\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{M}\epsilon^{\prime}_{F}\qquad\&\qquad\epsilon=\epsilon_{M}\epsilon_{F}\qquad\&\qquad\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{M}\epsilon^{\prime\prime}_{F}

and if
(\epsilon,\epsilon^{\prime},\epsilon^{\prime\prime})
exist in the table of KO-dimensions. Any AC-manifold must respect these relations to be associated with a definite spectral triple. We will then suppose that this is the case.

Now, taking any
\omega\in\Omega^{1}_{U}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
, the fluctuated Dirac operator takes the form:

\displaystyle D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}=D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}+\omega+\epsilon_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{\prime}J_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\omega J_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{-1}

\omega
can be represented as
\pi_{D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}(\omega)=a[D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}},b]
, with
a
and
b
in
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, we will take the notation
\omega
for
\pi_{D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}(\omega)
now. Computing this connection gives:

\displaystyle a[D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}},b]=\overbrace{a[D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}},b]}^{\text{ first term }}+\overbrace{a[\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},b]}^{\text{ second term }}

(11.6.8)

The first term give:

\displaystyle a[-i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}},b]=[-i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}},b]a=-i\gamma^{\mu}\otimes a\partial_{\mu}b\vcentcolon=\gamma^{\mu}\otimes\tilde{A_{\mu}}

(11.6.9)

Thanks to first-order condition and the fact that
[\gamma,\pi(a)]=0
. Then the second term:

\displaystyle a[\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},b]=\gamma_{M}\otimes a[D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},b]\vcentcolon=\gamma_{M}\otimes\tilde{\Phi}

Then
\omega=\gamma^{\mu}\otimes\tilde{A}_{\mu}+\gamma_{M}\otimes\tilde{\Phi}
with the Hermitian operators
\tilde{A}_{\mu}
and
\tilde{\Phi}
on
C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
. Let’s compute the fluctuated Dirac operator
\pi_{D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}}(\omega)
, it split into three terms. The first is
D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}
, then we have:

\displaystyle\gamma^{\mu}\otimes\tilde{A}_{\mu}+(\epsilon_{M}^{\prime}\otimes\epsilon_{F}^{\prime})(J_{M}\otimes J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}})\gamma^{\mu}\otimes\tilde{A}_{\mu}(J_{M}^{-1}\otimes J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\gamma^{\mu}\otimes(\tilde{A}_{\mu}+\epsilon_{M}^{\prime}\epsilon_{M}^{\prime\prime}\epsilon_{F}^{\prime}J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}\tilde{A}_{\mu}J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-1})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\gamma^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}

the last term being:

\displaystyle\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}+\gamma_{M}\otimes\varphi+(\epsilon_{M}^{\prime}\otimes\epsilon_{F}^{\prime})(J_{M}\otimes J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}})\gamma_{M}\otimes\tilde{\Phi}(J_{M}^{-1}\otimes J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-1})

\displaystyle=\gamma_{M}\otimes(D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}+\tilde{\Phi}+\epsilon_{M}^{\prime}\epsilon_{M}^{\prime\prime}\epsilon_{F}^{\prime}J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}\tilde{\Phi}J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-1})

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\gamma_{M}\otimes\Phi.

We obtain
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}=D_{M}\otimes 1+\gamma^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}+\gamma_{M}\otimes\Phi
. As we see, the action of an element
U\in\mathcal{G}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}};J_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}})
is equivalent to a transformation at the level of the connection given by
\omega^{u}=u\omega u^{*}+u[D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}},u^{*}]
. This gives:

\displaystyle\omega^{u}=u(\gamma^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}+\gamma_{M}\otimes\Phi)u^{*}+u[D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}+\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},u^{*}]

Using
[u,\gamma]=0
and
[\nabla_{\mu}^{S},u^{*}]=\partial_{\mu}u^{*}
, we recover the usual gauge transformation:

\displaystyle A_{\mu}\,\to\,uA_{\mu}u^{*}-iu\partial_{\mu}u^{*}\qquad\qquad\qquad\Phi\,\to\,u\Phi u^{*}+u[D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},u^{*}]

Now let
\nabla_{\mu}^{S}
be the spin connection on
S
introduced in the previous section
11.6.1
, and consider the vector bundle
E=S\otimes(M\times\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
such that
L^{2}(E)=\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
, and let
\nabla_{\mu}^{E}\vcentcolon=\nabla_{\mu}^{S}\otimes 1+1\otimes(\partial_{\mu}+iA_{\mu})
and
\Omega^{E}_{\mu\nu}=[\nabla_{\mu}^{E},\nabla_{\nu}^{E}]
be the natural twisted connection on
E
defined by the spectral triple, and it’s curvature. Then we have that
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}=-i\gamma^{\mu}\nabla_{\mu}^{E}+\gamma_{M}\otimes\Phi
.

Finally, let
D_{\mu}\vcentcolon=\partial_{\mu}+i\ad(A_{\mu})=\ad(\nabla_{\mu}^{E})
and its curvature
F_{\mu\nu}\vcentcolon=\partial_{\mu}A_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}A_{\mu}+i[A_{\mu},A_{\nu}]
or equivalently
[D_{\mu},D_{\nu}]=i\ad(F_{\mu\nu})
.
F_{\mu\nu}
and
\Omega^{E}_{\mu\nu}
are linked by the relation:

\displaystyle\Omega^{E}_{\mu\nu}=\Omega^{S}_{\mu\nu}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}+i{\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes F_{\mu\nu}.

As we will see,
\Phi
will correspond to the Higgs field. More complete computational details can be found in
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
.

Let’s compute
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2}=(D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}+\gamma^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu}+\gamma_{M}\otimes\Phi)^{2}
now. After some computation (details in
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
[p.146]) we find that

\displaystyle D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2}

\displaystyle=\Delta^{E}+\frac{1}{4}s\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}-i\frac{1}{2}\gamma^{\mu}\gamma^{\nu}\otimes F_{\mu\nu}+i\gamma_{M}\gamma^{\mu}\otimes D_{\mu}\Phi+{\text{\char 49\relax}}\otimes\Phi^{2}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=\Delta^{E}-F

which is called the generalized Laplacian,
\Delta^{E}
being given by
11.6.3
.

Inserting this in the heat kernel expansion, we have:

\displaystyle\Tr(\exp(-tD_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2}))\sim\sum_{k\geq 0}t^{\frac{k-n_{M}}{2}}a_{k}(D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})

where
n_{M}
corresponds to the dimension of
M
, and

\displaystyle a_{k}(D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})\vcentcolon=\int_{M}a_{k}(x,D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})\sqrt{g}d^{4}x

with the Seeley-DeWitt coefficients
a_{k}(x,D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})
. The
a_{k}
with odd
k
being zero, and
f_{i}=\int_{0}^{\infty}f(p)p^{i-1}dp
the moments of
f
. Doing the computation, we find:

\displaystyle a_{0}(x,D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})=(4\pi)^{\frac{-n_{M}}{2}}\Tr({\text{\char 49\relax}})

\displaystyle a_{2}(x,D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})=(4\pi)^{\frac{-n_{M}}{2}}\Tr((s/6)+F)

\displaystyle a_{4}(x,D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})=((4\pi)^{\frac{-n_{M}}{2}}/360)\Tr(-12\Delta s+5s^{2}-2R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu}+2R_{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}R^{\mu\nu\lambda\rho}+60sF

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+180F^{2}-60\Delta F+30\Omega^{E}_{\mu\nu}(\Omega^{E})^{\mu\nu})

\displaystyle a_{6}(x,D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})=\propto\text{43 terms of order 3: }F^{3},FR^{2},(\Omega^{E}_{\mu\nu})^{3}\dots

\displaystyle\dots

More details about this asymptotic expansion can be found in
(
Gilkey, 2011
)
. This permits us to compute the heat kernel expansion of the bosonic spectral action, taking
n_{M}=4
we have:

\displaystyle\Tr f(D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}/\Lambda)\sim f(0)a_{4}(D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})+2f_{2}\Lambda^{2}a_{2}(D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})+2f_{4}\Lambda^{4}a_{0}(D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2})+\mathcal{O}(\Lambda^{-1})

(11.6.10)

After some computations, we can see that if we define
n
to be the dimension of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}
, and define:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(g_{\mu\nu},A_{\mu},\Phi)\vcentcolon=n\mathcal{L}_{M}(g_{\mu\nu})+\mathcal{L}_{b}(A_{\mu})+\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}(g_{\mu\nu},A_{\mu},\Phi)

(11.6.11)

with:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{M}(g_{\mu\nu})=\frac{f_{4}\Lambda^{4}}{2\pi^{2}}-\frac{f_{2}\Lambda^{2}}{24\pi^{2}}s+\frac{f(0)}{480\pi^{2}}(s^{2}-3R_{\mu\nu}R^{\mu\nu})

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{A}(A_{\mu})=\frac{f(0)}{24\pi^{2}}\Tr(F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu})

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Phi}(g_{\mu\nu},A_{\mu},\Phi)=-\frac{2f_{2}\Lambda^{2}}{4\pi^{2}}\Tr(\Phi^{2})+\frac{f(0)}{8\pi^{2}}\Tr(\Phi^{4})+\frac{f(0)}{24\pi^{2}}\Delta(\Tr(\Phi^{2}))+\frac{f(0)}{48\pi^{2}}s\Tr(\Phi^{2})+\frac{f(0)}{8\pi^{2}}\Tr((D_{\mu}\Phi)(D^{\mu}\Phi))

then:

\displaystyle\Tr f(D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}/\Lambda)\sim\int_{M}\mathcal{L}(g_{\mu\nu},A_{\mu},\Phi)\sqrt{g}d^{4}x+\mathcal{O}(\Lambda^{-1})

Remark 11.6.6
.

Because of the Laplacian, the term
\frac{f(0)}{24\pi^{2}}\Delta(\Tr(\Phi^{2}))
will make no contribution in this boundary term integral.

Remark 11.6.7
.

We can see that in the case of the AC-manifold,
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}^{2}
contains the Laplacian
\Delta^{E}
over the Riemannian manifold defined in subsection
11.6.1
. We can therefore see that it extends in some way the usual Laplacian, and then the physics behind it, in the sense of the discussion made in section
8.4
.

More computational details can be found in
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
.

Let’s see what happens for the fermionic action. If we consider an element
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
, then it takes the form
\psi=\chi\otimes\xi
with
\chi\in L^{2}(S)
and
\xi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}
. Then we have
\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{+}=L^{2}(S)^{+}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{+}\oplus L^{2}(S)^{-}\otimes\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-}=\ker(\gamma_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}-1)
, so that any element
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{+}
will take the form:

\displaystyle\psi=\tilde{\chi}_{L}\otimes\xi+\tilde{\chi}_{R}\otimes\eta

(11.6.12)

with
\tilde{\chi}_{L}\in L^{2}(S)^{+}
for left handed spinors,
\tilde{\chi}_{R}\in L^{2}(S)^{-}
for right handed spinors,
\xi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{+}
,
\eta\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-}
. Taking
\{e_{v}\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{+}}
the orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{+}
and
\{e_{v}\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}
the one of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-}
, equation (
11.6.12
) can be written:

\displaystyle\psi=\bigoplus_{(v,\,v^{\prime})\in(\Gamma^{(0)}_{+},\,\Gamma^{(0)}_{-})}\chi_{L}^{(v)}\otimes e_{v}+\chi_{R}^{(v)}\otimes e_{v^{\prime}}

We restrict ourselves to the case
\epsilon^{\prime\prime}=-1
such that
J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}\,:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{\pm}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{\mp}
, this being the only interesting case in order to obtain the good fermionic action for the NCSMPP. In this case, we can consider that
J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}
connects the basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{-}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{+}

i.e.

\{J_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}e_{v}\vcentcolon=e_{\kappa(v)}\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{+}
. Then we have
\psi=\bigoplus_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}\chi_{L}^{(v)}\otimes e_{v}+\chi_{R}^{(v)}\otimes e_{\kappa(v)}
. Because
\gamma_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}=-D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\gamma_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}
the operator
D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}
and then
\Phi
will act as
D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}\,:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{\pm}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}_{F}}^{\mp}
. Therefore, taking
e=(v_{1},v_{2})
, we can define
\Phi_{e}\,:\mathcal{H}_{v_{1}}\,\to\,\mathcal{H}_{v_{2}}
as the restriction of
\Phi
on these corresponding subspaces such that
\Phi_{e}e_{v_{1}}=m_{e}e_{v_{2}}
with
m_{e}
a scalar. Furthermore, we define
\kappa(e)
by
\kappa(e)=(\kappa(v_{1}),\kappa(v_{2}))
.

Taking the corresponding Grassmann vector
\widetilde{\psi}
, the fermionic spectral action becomes:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{f}[\omega,\widetilde{\psi}]=\frac{1}{2}\langle J_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\widetilde{\psi},D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}\widetilde{\psi}\rangle_{\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}}

(11.6.13)

Using the fact that
A_{\mu}
take the form
\begin{pmatrix}Y_{\mu}&0\\
0&-Y_{\mu}\end{pmatrix}
in the
\{(e_{v},e_{\kappa(v)})\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}
basis, such that
Y_{\mu}e_{v}=Y_{\mu}^{(v)}e_{v}
and
Y_{\mu}e_{\kappa(v)}=-Y_{\mu}^{(v)}e_{\kappa(v)}
with
Y_{\mu}^{(v)}
a scalar and
v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}
,
(
11.6.13
) then contains the 4 terms:

\displaystyle J_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\tilde{\psi}=\bigoplus_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}J_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v)}\otimes e_{\kappa(v)}+\epsilon_{F}J_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v)}\otimes e_{v}

\displaystyle(D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}})\tilde{\psi}=\bigoplus_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}D_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v)}\otimes e_{v}+D_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v)}\otimes e_{\kappa(v)}

\displaystyle(\gamma^{\mu}\otimes A_{\mu})\tilde{\psi}=\bigoplus_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}\gamma^{\mu}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v)}\otimes Y_{\mu}e_{v}-\gamma^{\mu}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v)}\otimes Y_{\mu}e_{\kappa(v)}

\displaystyle(\gamma_{M}\otimes\Phi)\tilde{\psi}=\bigoplus_{v^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{+}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}\\
e=(v,v^{\prime})\\
e^{\prime}=(\kappa(v),v^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\gamma_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v)}\otimes\Phi_{e}e_{v}+\gamma_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v)}\otimes\Phi_{e^{\prime}}e_{\kappa(v)}

Taking the corresponding scalar products (the one defined in (
11.6.5
) for the spinor field, and the usual one associated with the inner product for the Hilbert space of the finite part), after some computations, we find that:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{f}[\omega,\widetilde{\psi}]=\sum_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}\Big(

\displaystyle(J_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v)},D_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v)})-(J_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v)},\gamma^{\mu}Y_{\mu}^{(v)}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v)})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}v^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}\\
e=(v^{\prime},\kappa(v))\end{subarray}}\big(m_{e}(J_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v)},\gamma_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{L}^{(v^{\prime})})+\epsilon_{F}m_{\kappa(e)}(J_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v)},\gamma_{M}\tilde{\chi}_{R}^{(v^{\prime})})\big)\Big)

Or equivalently
\mathcal{S}_{f}[\omega,\widetilde{\psi}]=\sum_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}}\mathcal{S}_{f}^{v}[\omega,\widetilde{\psi}]
, with
(\,\,,\,\,)
the spinor space’s scalar product defined in (
11.6.5
).

Chapter 12
The NCSMPP (in the Framework of Spectral Triples)

In the context of NCG’s applications in physics, the elaboration of a NCGFT yielding the Lagrangian of the SMPP can be considered as one of the most important achievements. This NCGFT is written using spectral triple techniques. This realization is the result of a long work in which 7 major steps can be highlighted:

1.

In 1990, M. Dubois-Violette, R. Kerner and J. Madore in
(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990a
)

(
Dubois-Violette et al., 1990b
)
showed that building NCGFTs on the AC-manifold model allowed to obtain a situation analogous to the usual gauge theories, with a natural interpretation of the Higgs field, and the potential naturally coming from the generalized curvature. This work was done via the framework of derivations of the algebra for the study of the purely NC part.

2.

Just after that, in 1990, A. Connes and J. Lott, inspired by this work, set up an equivalent thinking framework, but with the spectral triple technique, to build a non-commutative Standard Model
(
Connes and Lott, 1990
)
. The so-called Connes-Lott model did not incorporate gravitation.

3.

In 1996, an important step was reached by A. Chamseddine and A. Connes. In
(
Chamseddine and Connes, 1997
)
they propose an action principle (presented in section
11.6.2
), associated with the spectral triple of a NC space, which allows to incorporate gravitation into the previous model, but it suffers from the problem of fermion doubling and imposes null-mass for neutrinos.

4.

In 1996, F. Lizzi, G. Mangano, G. Miele and G. Sparano provide a way to overcome the fermion doubling problem
(
Lizzi et al., 1997
)
.

5.

In 2006, J. Barrett and A. Connes, in
(
Barrett, 2007
)
and
(
Connes, 2006
)
solved these two problems in one move, by allowing the metric dimension of a space to be independent of its KO-dimension, and making the choice of KO-dimension 6 for the finite NC part. It additionally gives a mass relation, which must be imposed at the unification scale, and therefore constrains potential models beyond the SMPP.

6.

In 2007, thanks to A. Chamseddine, A. Connes, and M. Marcolli, this works culminate in the full derivation of the (minimal) Standard Model, minimally coupled with gravity’s Lagrangian, directly computed from the spectral action on a given AC-manifold
(
Chamseddine et al., 2007a
)
, predicting a mass of 170 GeV for the Higgs boson, and giving a postdiction of the Top quark mass. I will succinctly describe this model in chapter
12
.

7.

In 2012 after the discovery of the Higgs boson, with a mass of 125 GeV, A. Chamseddine and A. Connes revisited their model to find an adequate Higgs mass, proposing not to neglect a certain scalar field appearing in the calculations of the NCSMPP
(
Chamseddine and Connes, 2012
)
.

8.

The achievement of the NCSMPP, corresponding to actual phenomenology opens the path to go beyond the SMPP in this framework. An important open door in this way was given in 2013 by A. Chamseddine, A. Connes, and W. van Suijlekom
(
Chamseddine et al., 2013
)
.

Other very important contributions to this programme can be mentioned, mainly due to D. Kastler, T. Schücker, B. Iochum, J. M. Gracia-Bondia, L. Carminati, C. Stephan, see for instance this nonexhaustive list
(
Carminati et al., 1996
;
Iochum et al., 1997
;
Iochum et al., 1995
;
Gracia-Bondıa et al., 1998
;
Iochum et al., 2004
)
.

In this chapter, I will show the elementary structure behind this NCSMPP, making some general remarks and highlighting how it allows a geometrization of all forces. Then I will mention how this non-commutative reformulation offers an adequate framework to think and constrain the creation of GUTs, preparing the last part of this thesis where I will explore a way to do it, using this framework and
AF
-algebras.

12.1
Obtaining the Full Lagrangian of the SMPP

As mentioned in section
11.6
, the development of the NCGFT in the framework of spectral triple allowed to set up a NC structure from which the SMPP Lagrangian can be extracted in a natural way. The goal of this section is to present the general structure behind the NCGFT which leads to the actual NCSMPP. This section aims to present the general idea of the NC structure behind this reformulation. Then a lot of technical details will not figure here in order to focus on the essential structures. A more complete presentation of the NCSMPP can be found in
(
Chamseddine et al., 2007b
;
van Suijlekom, 2015
;
Jureit et al., 2007
)
.

The NCSMPP is based on the following AC-manifold:

\displaystyle ST_{\mathcal{A}_{SM}}=\,

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{SM},\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=L^{2}(S)\otimes\mathcal{H}_{SM},D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}+\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{SM},

\displaystyle J_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=J_{M}\otimes J_{SM},\gamma_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=\gamma_{M}\otimes\gamma_{SM})

(12.1.1)

We note that
ST_{\mathcal{A}_{SM}}
is here only parameterized by the finite part, because it will be the only variable part in what will follow,
i.e.
the manifold part will always be the same as here.
J_{M}
and
\gamma_{M}
define the charge conjugation and chirality operator used in particle physics. To obtain a definite spectral triple for the AC-manifold, with good spectral action, KO dimension 6 will be the good choice for the finite part (the space of fermionic representations).

The algebra of the finite part is given by
\mathcal{A}_{SM}\simeq\mathbb{C}\oplus\mathbb{H}\oplus M_{3}(\mathbb{C})
, an element
a\in\mathcal{A}_{SM}
is given by the elements
(a_{1},a_{2},a_{3})\in(\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H},M_{3}(\mathbb{C}))
. The Hilbert space is given by
\mathcal{H}_{SM}=(\mathcal{H}_{l}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\overline{l}}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{q}\oplus\mathcal{H}_{\overline{q}})^{\oplus 3}
with
\mathcal{H}_{l}
for leptons,
\mathcal{H}_{\overline{l}}
for anti-leptons,
\mathcal{H}_{q}
for quarks,
\mathcal{H}_{\overline{q}}
for anti-quarks, and
\oplus 3
for the 3 generations. The ways elements of
\mathcal{A}_{SM}
act on the different parts of
\mathcal{H}_{SM}
can be found in
(
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
[p.188]. Imposing the unimodularity condition, the Gauge group of the finite part is then given by the quotient on the finite cyclic subgroup
\mu_{6}
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{G}(\mathcal{A}_{SM},\mathcal{H}_{SM};J_{SM})=U(1)\times SU(2)\times SU(3)/\mu_{6}.

To build the connection, we use the same procedure as done in section
11.6.1
concerning equation (
11.6.8
). Taking
a,b\in\mathcal{A}_{SM}
, the associated one form
a[D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}},b]
then split into two terms, one coming from
D_{SM}
and the other from
D_{M}
. Then, the equation (
11.6.9
) which corresponds to this second term now splits into 3 parts
\gamma^{\mu}\otimes\tilde{A}_{\mu,j}
with
\tilde{A}_{\mu,j}=-ia_{j}\partial_{\mu}b_{j}
and
j\in\{1,2,3\}
. Computing the fluctuated Dirac operator, we recover
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}=D_{M}\otimes 1+\gamma^{\mu}\otimes(A_{\mu,j})+\gamma_{M}\otimes\Phi
where
A_{\mu,1}
(resp
A_{\mu,2}
and
A_{\mu,3}
) correspond to the
U(1)
(resp
SU(2)
and
SU(3)
) gauge bosons. Having obtain
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}},\omega}
, we can therefore compute the bosonic spectral action
\mathcal{S}_{b}[\omega]

For the fermionic action, the novelty is that the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{SM}^{\pm}
now split into two pieces
\mathcal{H}_{SM,R/L}^{\pm}
, by pairing with the spinor field’s chiralities. Then, we have the basis
\mathcal{H}_{SM,R}^{-}
given by
\{e_{v}^{R}\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-,R}}
and
\{J_{SM}e_{v}^{R}\vcentcolon=e^{R}_{\kappa(v)}\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-,R}}
for a basis of
\mathcal{H}_{SM,R}^{+}
(Because we choose KO dimension 6 so that
\epsilon_{F}^{\prime\prime}=-1
, then
J_{SM}
change the chirality). The same being done with
\{e_{v}^{L}\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{+,L}}
for
\mathcal{H}_{SM,L}^{\pm}
.

It is possible to connect
\mathcal{H}_{SM,R}^{\pm}
and
\mathcal{H}_{SM,L}^{\mp}
basis using an operator
h\,:\,\Gamma^{(0)}_{\pm,R/L}\to\Gamma^{(0)}_{\mp,L/R}
such that
h^{2}={\text{\char 49\relax}}
,
h\circ\kappa\circ h\circ\kappa={\text{\char 49\relax}}
and that the basis of
\mathcal{H}_{SM,L/R}^{\pm}
are also given by
\{e_{h(v)}^{L/R}\}_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{\mp,R/L}}
. Another splitting can be given by the type of fermion field, with leptons (
\nu
for neutrinos type and
e
for the other leptons), and by quarks (
u
for quarks up type and
d
for quarks down). Because there are only 3 generations, each of the basis of the four Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{SM,R/L}^{\pm}
will possess 3 elements. Furthermore,
h
and
\kappa
operators cannot connect different types of fermions.
\Gamma^{(0)}_{-}
will then be the index set of fermions (resp.
\Gamma^{(0)}_{+}
for anti fermions), and
\Gamma^{(0)}_{R/L}
the index set of Right/Left handed fermions. Then any
\psi
in
\mathcal{H}_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}^{+}
will take the general form:

\displaystyle\psi=\bigoplus_{v\in\Gamma^{(0)}_{-,R}}

\displaystyle\chi_{e,R}^{(v)}\otimes e_{v}^{R}+\chi_{e,L}^{(v)}\otimes e_{h(v)}^{L}+\chi_{\bar{e},R}^{(v)}\otimes e_{\kappa(v)}^{R}+\chi_{\bar{e},L}^{(v)}\otimes e_{\kappa(h(v))}^{L}

\displaystyle+\chi_{\nu,R}^{(v)}\otimes\nu_{v}^{R}+\chi_{\nu,L}^{(v)}\otimes\nu_{h(v)}^{L}+\chi_{\bar{\nu},R}^{(v)}\otimes\nu_{\kappa(v)}^{R}+\chi_{\bar{\nu},L}^{(v)}\otimes\nu_{\kappa(h(v))}^{L}

\displaystyle+\chi_{u,R}^{(v)}\otimes u_{v}^{R}+\chi_{u,L}^{(v)}\otimes u_{h(v)}^{L}+\chi_{\bar{u},R}^{(v)}\otimes u_{\kappa(v)}^{R}+\chi_{\bar{u},L}^{(v)}\otimes u_{\kappa(h(v))}^{L}

\displaystyle+\chi_{d,R}^{(v)}\otimes d_{v}^{R}+\chi_{d,L}^{(v)}\otimes d_{h(v)}^{L}+\chi_{\bar{d},R}^{(v)}\otimes d_{\kappa(v)}^{R}+\chi_{\bar{d},L}^{(v)}\otimes d_{\kappa(h(v))}^{L}

All Dirac spinors are independent. The fermionic spectral action
\mathcal{S}_{f}[\omega,\widetilde{\psi}]
can then be computed.

A complete computation of these two actions leads to the SMPP’s Lagrangian as presented in (
10.3.1
), coupled to gravitation.

Remark 12.1.1
.

In the NCSMPP,
A_{\mu}
and
\Phi
are considered as independent degrees of freedom, taken as input in calculations. But on the formal level of the construction, we can see that they are deductions of
a[D_{\mathcal{A}_{F}},b]=\tilde{\Phi}
and
-ia\partial_{\mu}b=\tilde{A}_{\mu}
. Then, they are formally linked by their dependence on
a
and
b
. As far as I know, the fact that
A_{\mu}
and
\Phi
are linked by the algebra’s degrees of freedom was not mentioned, and I haven’t found any authors mentioning the reason for such an omission.

Insofar as there may be no reason for such an omission, one advantage of such an observation would be to decrease the number of independent degrees of freedom in the current theory and more strongly constrain the structure of the SMPP.

Remark 12.1.2
.

We can see that
n_{M}
, the number of dimensions of space-time is linked to the structure of the Lagrangian in this approach. In equation (
11.6.10
), we see that the therms
\{a_{k}\}_{k}
which give non-zero contribution in the spectral action at the origin of the Lagrangian of the SMPP are the ones such that
k\leq n_{M}
. If the space-time was of dimension 2 or 6, the Lagrangian should be very different. This is a strong constraint.

Remark 12.1.3
.

The terms in
\Tr(\Phi^{2})
and
\Tr(\Phi^{4})
in the Lagrangian can be seen as weight associated with loops in Krajewski’s diagram (in the irreducible’s representation space of the fermionic field) because of the trace properties. This can be put in analogy with the curvature
F_{\mu\nu}F^{\mu\nu}
where
F_{\mu\nu}
was given in section
10.1
by computing the total phase accumulation of a fermion field along a loop in space-time. Therefore, we can see that in the two cases, the curvature of the connections is associated with loops in their corresponding geometries, commutative and not commutative. As mentioned in section
9.1
, if the pure state of a given particle gives the notion of point, the irreducible representations which correspond to Left/Right, fermion/anti-fermion, and charge parts of this pure state can be seen as internal degrees of freedom living within this point.

I think that according to the three criteria of enhancement of a theory listed in section
10.5
, the NCSMPP can be considered as potentially better than the actual SMPP in the usual framework, and must therefore replace it. Let’s see how it satisfies these different points.

For the first point, the number of independent inputs in the NCSMPP is inferior to the one of the SMPP. News relations between the SMPP’s parameters are obtained, like the GUT relation of the couplings constants, are other relations giving a prediction for the Higgs mass and a post-diction for the Top quark. This fact comes from the more algebraically constrained structure of the NC framework.

For the diminution of the number of independent formal inputs, the fact that the Higgs is now a well-defined object in the mathematical structure and that its potential is deduced from the formalism is a great advance. Another enhancement concerns the fact that the right hypercharges are naturally implementes by the NC framework. In fact, all the structures of the Lagrangian are deduced from minimal principles: extending the notion of space, studying the general automorphisms of its algebra, and computing an invariant thanks to the obtained fluctuated Dirac operator. Furthermore, it offers a link between the whole structure of the Lagrangian and the dimension of space-time.

To complete the praise for this NC reformulation, with the third kind of enhancement (Consistency with other theories on their crossings area, and on the conceptual and epistemological level), the NCSMPP is not less than a unification of all forces in one framework, where similar structures can be found by extending the “geometric” picture, such as generalization of the line element, of the notion of propagation along these line element, and then of motion with it’s associated kinetic energy as we will see in section
12.2
.

Nevertheless, this can’t be considered a complete reformulation, since as mentioned in subsection
11.6.1
, the spacetime manifold is taken to be Riemannian whereas it is Minkowskian in relativity theory. The compact Riemannian assumption is essential to obtain the asymptotic development of the bosonic spectral action which cannot therefore be Lorentz invariant.

Now that the NCSMPP has been set up, what can we do with this structure? Are there some advantages to study the SMPP in this framework? One of the main prospects of the NCSMPP concerns the creation of GUTs, because of the strong constraints offered by the formalism. This will be more fully discussed in section
12.2
and in the whole part
IV
where the purpose will be to set up a general formalism to create GUTs in the framework of NCGFT. Another perspective of interest concerns the potentiality to offer ways to quantize gravitation since it is now presented in a formalism equivalent to that of the other forces, for which we know how to do.

It is not excluded that the SMPP can be found in the derivation framework too, but few theoretical efforts have been made in this direction. More details on the NCSMPP can be found in
(
Chamseddine et al., 2007a
;
Jureit et al., 2007
;
van Suijlekom, 2015
)
.

12.2
Beyond the SMPP in the NCSMPP’s Framework

Where there is matter, there is geometry.

J. Kepler

The NCSMPP in the framework of spectral triples can be seen as a “classical” (no quantization mentioned) unification of general relativity and the SMPP, all in one pure geometric framework. In this section we will discuss the meaning of this geometrization, and how this NCSMPP offers a nice and constrained way to make models beyond the SMPP. This section can be viewed as the NC (partial) reply to sections
10.4
and
10.5
.

The NCSMPP is a powerful way to geometrize the interaction of elementary particles, in the same way as Gravitation was geometrized with general relativity. Let’s first try to highlight how the NCSMPP offers a unifying framework for all forces, then a unification at the formal level.

Let’s consider the Dirac equation:

\displaystyle(-i(\hbar/c)\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}+m)\psi=0

The idea of the NCSMPP is to “geometrize” this mass term such that the Dirac equation appears as a simple kinetic term linked to a move in something bigger than spacetime. This is done by adding a new term into the Dirac operator
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}+\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{SM}
which represents the correspondings new degrees of freedom.

Take a look at another expression of the Dirac equation using the reduced Compton wavelength
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.43057pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\kern 1.79993pt\scalebox{1}[0.8]{$\mathchar 132$}}}}\lambda_{c}=\hbar/mc
:

\displaystyle(-i\gamma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}+1/\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.43057pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\kern 1.79993pt\scalebox{1}[0.8]{$\mathchar 132$}}}}\lambda_{c})\psi=0

We recognize
D_{M}
and the scalar coming from
D_{SM}
. We can see that the mass term is associated with an inverse length scale term, proportional to the Compton wavelength defined in section
9.3
. In the NCSMPP, this mass term is taken to be given by the Dirac operator
D_{SM}
of a pure NCG. These Compton scales will then correspond to the natural scales of this NCG.

Remark 12.2.1
.

In section
9.3
, I give physical arguments about the fact that the usual notion of point fails at such scales, which can then induce a NCG. We have therefore this nice coincidence, on one side, we have the NCSMPP built from mathematical arguments stating that the natural scale associated to a NCG is the Compton scale. And on the other side, we have the physical arguments developed in part
II
which induce the same thing. I believe that it is an open door to find a physical justification for such a geometrization of all forces.

Remark 12.2.2
.

It is interesting to note that the Dirac operator
D_{M}
is related to the momentum of the particle, and thus to the de Broglie characteristic scale
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.43057pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\kern 1.79993pt\scalebox{1}[0.8]{$\mathchar 132$}}}}\lambda_{b}=\hbar/mv
, whereas the mass is related to the Compton characteristic scale
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.43057pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\kern 1.79993pt\scalebox{1}[0.8]{$\mathchar 132$}}}}\lambda_{c}=\hbar/mc
. These two scales are equal when
v=c
. If
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.43057pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\kern 1.79993pt\scalebox{1}[0.8]{$\mathchar 132$}}}}\lambda_{b}
is usually associated with a wavelength which is observable in a space-time propagation, we cannot say the same for
\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.43057pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\kern 1.79993pt\scalebox{1}[0.8]{$\mathchar 132$}}}}\lambda_{c}
. However, the framework of NCG makes it possible to perceive this scale as associated with propagation, but along the NCG’s degrees of freedom.

Remark 12.2.3
.

\overline{\psi}_{i}y_{ij}\phi\psi_{j}
and
i\overline{\psi}\not{D}\psi
are very close in form, an idea of the NCSMPP is to make the thirst term be a kinetic one by creating new pure NC degrees of freedom (in the same way as
\partial_{\mu}
are those of space-time). This is done by constructing the corresponding “covariant derivative”
D_{SM}
which will give
y_{ij}\phi
and then the fermions and bosons masses. In this way, we can say that
\overline{\psi}_{i}y_{ij}\phi\psi_{j}
describes the kinetic term associated to the propagation (corresponding to
y_{ij}\phi
) of the fermions in these pure NC degrees of freedom
i.e.
in the representation space associated to the finite space
F_{SM}
(the finite space associated to
\mathcal{A}_{SM}
). This implement the idea presented in remark
9.1.1
about the continuation of the Gelfand-Naimark equivalence for these inner degrees of freedom.

This offers in the same way a framework to geometrize the set of observable change
\mathbb{O}_{nst}
mentioned in section
8.1
with the link made with gauge theory done in section
8.3
. The NCSMPP offers then a geometrical picture for all observable variations
\mathbb{O}
, taking
\mathbb{O}_{st}
to be encoded by the gravitational part of the SMPP.

The parts of the Lagrangian which are linked to the Dirac equation can then be associated with kinetics terms associated with space-time and non-space-time propagation:

•

i\overline{\psi}\not{\partial}\psi
corresponds to the part linked to displacements in space-time degrees of freedom, linked to the variation observable set
\mathbb{O}_{st}

•

\overline{\psi}_{i}y_{ij}\phi\psi_{j}
corresponds to the part linked to displacements in “non space-time” degrees of freedom, linked to the variation observable set
\mathbb{O}_{nst}
. This corresponds to an elementary displacements in the inner representation space of the fermion fields, from the element indexed by
i
to the one indexed by
j
.

\text{\makebox[0.0pt][l]{\raisebox{0.43057pt}[0.0pt][0.0pt]{\kern 1.79993pt\scalebox{1}[0.8]{$\mathchar 132$}}}}\lambda_{c}
can then be considered as the scale at which the notion of point fails according to section
9.3
, such that no-space-time propagation is possible below, since the notion of point fails at such scales. New degrees of freedom are then visited at these scales, these are those of a NCG.

Energy is a quantity deducible from the observation of the state’s rate of change that occurs during physical processes (then of
\mathbb{O}
). For free particles, it can therefore be seen as the addition of the kinetic energies associated to move along space-time (“C" for commutative) and NC degrees of freedoms (dofs):

\displaystyle E^{2}=\,\overbrace{p^{2}c^{2}}^{\text{C dofs}}\,+\,\overbrace{m^{2}c^{4}}^{\text{NC dofs}}.

We have the momentum corresponding to the observation of the state change according to space degrees of freedom, and the mass-energy which will then corresponds to the state change according to inner degrees of freedom
i.e.
in the fermionic representation space
F_{SM}
.

Inertia can then be seen as induced by the proportion of the total kinetic energy (linked to the spacetime and non-spacetime degrees of freedom), which leaks into the inner (non-spatialized) degrees of freedom and then can be seen as a kind of non-total transmission of the potential energy encoded in gauge fields (which leads to the acceleration) into kinetic energy for the spacetime degrees of freedom.

This appears to be in line with the program in search for unified field theory (UFT), in which Kaluza–Klein’s theory and Einstein’s works around the unification of gravitation and electromagnetism into one geometric picture are well-known attempts. The NCSMPP made it possible to reformulate the SMPP in a way that unifies it with gravitation so that the four fundamental forces are expressed as pure gravitation on a space
\widehat{M}=M\times F
.
\Diff(M)
now fits into a generalization of the diffeomorphisms
\Diff(\widehat{M})=\Diff(M)\ltimes\mathcal{G}
on this extended space, defined trough the automorphisms of the algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{F}
with
\mathcal{G}=Map(M,G)
, and
G
being the standard model symmetry group for example. In general relativity (commutative geometry), gravity emerged as a pseudo-force associated with general coordinate transformations which are equivalent to diffeomorphisms. In this extended almost-commutative geometry, the other forces become pseudo-forces too, induced by the automorphisms of the algebra, the diffeomorphisms of
M
being equivalent to the outer ones.

The usual line element
ds
being connected to inverse of the Dirac operator
D_{M}
(which contains the gravitational forces data) is now extended to
d\tilde{s}
trough the new Dirac operator
D_{\widehat{\mathcal{A}}}\vcentcolon=D_{M}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}+\gamma_{M}\otimes D_{SM}
. Therefore, this new line element contains all the forces in its very structure, where gauge bosons appear as inner fluctuations of the associated metric.

If this framework offers a nice classical unification for gravitation and the other forces, it seems to be very adapted to the elaboration of GUT-like unification too. This is probably because of its algebraic and very constrained structure, making links between the fermionic content and the GUT relation for example. The fact that the SMPP is now interpreted geometrically makes GUTs extensions (at higher energies) similar to a change in the geometry, just like for the geometry in general relativity which is a function of the stress-energy tensor and can therefore change with it. In what follows, a general framework to do such GUTs will be constructed, using AF-algebras as base algebra, and suitable compatibility conditions to implement the link between the theory and its GUT. This will be done using derivation and then spectral triples-based gauge theories. More details about the constraints offered by this NCSMPP approach can be found in
(
Jureit and Stephan, 2008
;
Iochum et al., 2004
;
van den Broek and van Suijlekom, 2013
;
Chamseddine and Connes, 2008
)
.

Part IV
Noncommutative Gauge Field Theories based on
AF
-Algebras

Chapter 13
GUTs,
AF
-Embedding and
\phi
-Compatibility Condition

The main purpose of this thesis is to construct NCGFTs based on
AF

C^{*}
-algebras. As mentioned in section
2.2
, because of the composition property of
\phi

i.e.

\phi_{m,p}\circ\phi_{n,m}=\phi_{n,p}
, one needs only to describe the homomorphisms
\phi_{n,n+1}:\mathcal{A}_{n}\to\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
. Then, the heart of the problem is to understand what happens during a single step in the inductive sequence, and so to identify how NCGFTs based on the algebra at a given step (
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{n}
) transform into the next one (
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
):

\displaystyle\text{NCGFT}_{\mathcal{A}}\leadsto\text{NCGFT}_{\mathcal{B}}.

The
\text{NCGFT}_{\mathcal{A}}
does not only depend on the algebra
\mathcal{A}
. According to the case (derivations or spectral triples), NCGFTs will also depend on the chosen representation, on the chosen automorphisms, on the selected connection, and the choices concerning the additional structural operators (
D
,
J
,
\gamma
…). It is thus necessary to connect all these essential ingredients along the inductive sequence to relate the NCGFTs at different steps. It is the
\phi
-compatibility condition (presented in section
13.3
) that implements this constraint between structures. This part represents the main realization of the work done during this thesis, for which the articles
(
Masson and Nieuviarts, 2021
)
and
(
Masson and Nieuviarts, 2022
)
are the achievements, using the method of derivations for the first one, and of spectral triples for the second.

13.1
GUTs as a Motivations to do NCGFT’s based on
AF
-Algebras

In section
10.5
, we highlighted the fact that the creation of GUT-type models should take the algebra describing the gauge field as input, and give an explanation of the transformation
\text{GFT}_{\text{SMPP}}\,\rightarrow\,\text{GFT}_{\text{GUT}}
. In this part, we will try to show how the construction of NCGFTs based on algebras of type AF offers an interesting way to understand this move, using suitable conditions to link NCGFTs along the inductive sequence.

There is no general way to understand how to get NCGFT
B
from NCGFT
A
. But this seems reasonable to expect that the structure of NCGFT
A
would be recovered from NCGFT
B
, under certain conditions since we expect to obtain a GUT-like process. Thus, an insight can be that the NCGFT
B
must contain all degrees of freedom of NCGFT
A
, and that essential structures must be related.

We will then observe the consequences on the algebra of the AC-manifold whose finite part is taken to be this
AF

C^{*}
-algebra,
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{AF}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{AF}
, in order to identify how NCGFTs based on the algebra at a given step
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}
transform into the next
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{B}
. The Lagrangian obtained at a given step can be interpreted as a unified NCGFT of the previous one. For instance, if we take
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}
to be the one which gives the NCSMPP (
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}_{SM}
with the corresponding structure presented in chapter
12
), then the purpose is to see how according to suitable conditions
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{B}
can represent a GUT like extension of the NCSMPP. The inherited structures will be identified in order to see how the old degrees of freedom (those from the previous step) and the new ones interact, and how we recover parts of the “old” Lagrangian in the new.

We propose to explore how the general embedding given by
\phi
of
\mathcal{A}
into
\mathcal{B}
can provide such a link between the two NCGFT, and how
AF
’s embedding special characteristics can help to obtain “more concrete” class of models. A key ingredient to do this sequence of NCGFT will be the
\phi
-compatibility condition. It will permit to relate of the structures of NCGFT
A
and NCGFT
B
. As we will see, one of the main properties of AF embedding is that they do not allow the embedding of two different block algebras in the direct sum of
\mathcal{A}
at the same position within a block of
\mathcal{B}
(see equation (
13.2.7
)). The novelty with the spectral triples approach is that this will not be the case for some irreps in the Hilbert space which can be “mixed” during the embedding (see equation (
15.1.8
)).

In our approach to NCGFT based on a “sequence” of finite dimensional NCGFT on the
\mathcal{A}_{n}
’s, we will not suppose that an approximation at a level
n_{0}
gives us all the information about the “limiting” NCGFT in the
AF
algebra. In other words, some new inputs (in addition to the
A_{n,n+1}
’s) could be “added” at every step. This implies (obviously) that many non-equivalent NCGFTs could be constructed on top of a unique
AF
algebra. This relies on the fact that there may be physical motivations to construct one sequence rather than another and that the chosen embedding at every step could participate in the phenomenology. This is similar to, but also a departure from, GUT where some information is encoded in the SSBM reducing the large group to the group of the SMPP: in our research program, we can look at our embeddings as being in duality with the SSBM, in a way that will be illustrated in Sects.
14.3
and
15.2
.

13.2
Structure of the
AF
Embedding

We will study the lift of an inclusion
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
regarding some of the structures defined on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
. Here we consider the special case of sums of matrix algebras,
\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}
and
\mathcal{B}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{k}}
. For reasons that will be explained in Sect.
14.3
for derivations and Sect.
15.1
for spectral triples,
\phi
is not necessarily unital. We define the corresponding projection and injection maps
\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
,
\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
,
\iota_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}
and
\iota_{\mathcal{B}}^{k}
. We also suppose that there are (orthogonal) decompositions
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},i}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B},k}
such that the
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},i}
(resp.
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B},k}
) are Hilbert spaces on which
\mathcal{A}_{i}
(resp.
\mathcal{B}_{k}
) are represented. In other words, the (left) module structures are compatible with the direct sums of algebras and Hilbert spaces: for any
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}
and
\psi=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
, one has
a\psi=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\psi_{i}
(and similarly for
\mathcal{B}
).

An operator
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
can be decomposed along the operators
A_{j}^{i}\vcentcolon=\pi^{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}}_{j}\circ A\circ\iota_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{i}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},i}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},j}
. The same holds for operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
. For computational purposes, we recall that one has

\displaystyle A\psi

\displaystyle=\toplus_{j=1}^{r}\big(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}A_{j}^{i}(\psi_{i})\big)=\tsum_{i,j=1}^{r}\iota_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{j}\circ A_{j}^{i}(\psi_{i}).

In the same way, a (general, not necessarily the one of the
AF
-algebra) morphism of algebras
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
decomposes along the maps
\phi_{k}^{i}\vcentcolon=\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{k}\circ\phi\circ\iota_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}:\mathcal{A}_{i}\to\mathcal{B}_{k}
and a morphism of Hilbert spaces
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
decomposes along the
\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{i}\vcentcolon=\pi^{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}}_{k}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H}}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}}^{i}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},i}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B},k}
. One has

\displaystyle\phi(a)

\displaystyle=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\big(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})\big),\quad\text{and}\quad\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\big(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{i}(\psi_{i})\big).

Notice also that
\phi(aa^{\prime})=\phi(a)\phi(a^{\prime})
implies

\displaystyle\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i}a^{\prime}_{i})=\tsum_{i,j=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})\phi_{k}^{j}(a^{\prime}_{j})\quad\text{for any $k=1,\dots,s$}

(13.2.1)

Let’s now consider the inclusion given by the
AF
-algebra. The inclusion
\phi
is taken in its simplest form, and we normalize it such that, for any
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}
,

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(a)\vcentcolon=\pi^{\mathcal{B}}_{k}\circ\phi(a)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{k1}}&0&\cdots&0&0\\
0&a_{2}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{k2}}&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&a_{r}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{kr}}&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0&{\text{\char 48\relax}}_{n_{0,k}}\end{pmatrix}

(13.2.2)

where the integer
\alpha_{ki}\geq 0
is the multiplicity of the inclusion of
M_{n_{i}}
into
M_{m_{k}}
,
{\text{\char 48\relax}}_{n_{0}}
is the
n_{0}\times n_{0}
zero matrix such that
n_{0}\geq 0
satisfies
m_{k}=n_{0}+\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{ki}n_{i}
, and

\displaystyle a_{i}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{ki}}

\displaystyle=\left.\begin{pmatrix}a_{i}&0&0&0\\
0&a_{i}&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&a_{i}\\
\end{pmatrix}\right\}\text{$\alpha_{ki}$ times.}

We define the maps
\phi_{k}^{i}\vcentcolon=\phi_{k}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}:M_{n_{i}}\to M_{m_{k}}
, which take the explicit form

\displaystyle\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\cdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\cdots&0&a_{i}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{ki}}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&0&0&\cdots&0\end{pmatrix}

(13.2.3)

The maps
\phi_{k}^{i}
satisfy a stronger relation than (
13.2.1
): for any
i,j=1,\dots,r
and
k=1,\dots,s
,

\displaystyle\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})\phi_{k}^{j}(a^{\prime}_{j})

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}0&\text{if $i\neq j$}\\
\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i}a^{\prime}_{i})&\text{if $i=j$}.\end{cases}

(13.2.4)

This is the main specific characteristic of
AF
-algebra’s embedding structures.

If
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{A}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
for a
AF
-algebra
\varinjlim\mathcal{A}_{n}
, then the multiplicities
\alpha_{ki}
define the Bratteli diagram of this
AF
-algebra and vice versa. The integers
n_{0,k}
are defined by complementarity at each step.

When
\alpha_{ki}>0
, for
1\leq\alpha\leq\alpha_{ki}
we define the maps
\phi_{k,\alpha}^{i}:M_{n_{i}}\to M_{m_{k}}
which insert
a_{i}
at the
\alpha
-th entry on the diagonal of
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{ki}}
in the previous expression, so that
a_{i}
appears only once on the RHS. The maps
\phi_{k}
,
\phi_{k}^{i}
, and
\phi_{k,\alpha}^{i}
are morphisms of algebras and one has

\displaystyle\phi

\displaystyle=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\phi_{k}:\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}\to\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{k}},

\displaystyle\phi_{k}

\displaystyle=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}\circ\pi^{\mathcal{A}}_{i}:\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}\to M_{m_{k}},

(13.2.5)

\displaystyle\phi_{k}^{i}

\displaystyle=\tsum_{\alpha=1}^{\alpha_{ki}}\phi_{k,\alpha}^{i}:M_{n_{i}}\to M_{m_{k}}.

Notice then that
\phi_{k}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}})=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tsum_{\alpha=1}^{\alpha_{ki}}\phi_{k,\alpha}^{i}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})
fills the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
with
\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{ki}n_{i}
copies of
1
except for the last
n_{0,k}
entries. When
n_{0,k}=0
, one gets
\phi_{k}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}})={\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}
, otherwise, let

\displaystyle p_{n_{0,k}}\vcentcolon={\text{\char 49\relax}}_{m_{k}}-\phi_{k}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}})\in M_{m_{k}}\quad\text{ and }\quad p_{n_{0}}\vcentcolon=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}p_{n_{0,k}}\in\mathcal{B}.

(13.2.6)

The
p_{n_{0,k}}
’s are diagonal matrices with zero entries except for the last
n_{0,k}
diagonal entries (bottom right) which are equal to
1
.

Lemma 13.2.1
.

For any
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, any
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,\dots,r\}
, any
\alpha_{1}\in\{1,\dots,\alpha_{ki_{1}}\}
and any
\alpha_{2}\in\{1,\dots,\alpha_{ki_{2}}\}
,

\displaystyle\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(a)\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(b)=\delta_{i_{1},i_{2}}\delta_{\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}}\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(ab).

(13.2.7)

Proof

This is a direct consequence of the definition of
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}
and the multiplications of block diagonal matrices.

13.3
\phi
-Compatibility in the Derivation Framework

Definition 13.3.1
.

An one-to-one map
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
between a left
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{M}
and a left
\mathcal{B}
-module
\mathcal{N}
is
\phi
-compatible if
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}(ae)=\phi(a)\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}(e)
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
e\in\mathcal{M}
.

Remark 13.3.2
.

A “physical meaning” for the
\phi
-compatibility condition is to conserve the action of embedded algebras elements on embedded Module’s spaces, meaning in some ways that bosonic fields (elements in the algebra) continue to act in the same way on their associated fermionic fields (elements in the module).

In the following, since we want to construct a direct limit of modules accompanying a direct limit of algebras with one-to-one maps, we always suppose that
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}
is also one-to-one. As before we define the corresponding projection and injection maps
\pi^{\mathcal{M}}_{i}
,
\pi^{\mathcal{N}}_{k}
,
\iota_{\mathcal{M}}^{i}
and
\iota_{\mathcal{N}}^{k}
.

From
(
Davidson, 1996
, Cor. III.1.2)
, we know that all the left modules on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
are of the form
\mathcal{M}=\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{r}}
and
\mathcal{N}=\mathbb{C}^{m_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathbb{C}^{m_{s}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{s}}
for some integers
\alpha_{i}
and
\beta_{k}
. Two situations are easily handled to construct an one-to-one
\phi
-compatible map
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
.

1.

The case
\alpha_{i}=\beta_{k}=1
for any
i
and
k
. In that situation,
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}
can be constructed in a natural way using the multiplicities
\alpha_{ki}
of
\phi
. Denote by
\mathbf{1}_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
(resp.
\mathbf{0}_{n}\in\mathbb{C}^{n}
) the vector with all the entries equal to
1
(resp.
0
). Then one can define
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}
by

\displaystyle\pi^{\mathcal{N}}_{k}\circ\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}(e)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}e_{1}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\alpha_{k1}}\\
e_{2}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\alpha_{k2}}\\
\vdots\\
e_{r}\otimes\mathbf{1}_{\alpha_{kr}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{n_{0}}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathcal{N}_{k}=\mathbb{C}^{m_{k}}

2.

The case
\alpha_{i}=n_{i}
and
\beta_{k}=m_{k}
for any
i
and
k
. This situation corresponds to
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{B}
. The canonical map
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}
is taken to be
\phi
itself.

By construction, these two maps are
\phi
-compatible. In the more general situation, we have to inject
\alpha_{ki}
times (as rows)
\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{i}}
into
\mathbb{C}^{m_{k}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\beta_{k}}
(
\mathbb{C}^{n_{i}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{i}}
is injected only when
\alpha_{ki}>0
). A necessary condition is that
\beta_{k}
is large enough to accept the largest
\alpha_{i}
. This necessary condition leaves open the possibility of constructing many modules and many maps
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}
which are
\phi
-compatible.

Similarly to
\phi
, we decompose
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}
as
\phi_{\text{{Mod}},k}^{i}\vcentcolon=\pi^{\mathcal{N}}_{k}\circ\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}\circ\iota_{\mathcal{M}}^{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\mathcal{N}_{k}
and for any
1\leq\alpha\leq\alpha_{ki}
,
\phi_{\text{{Mod}},k,\alpha}^{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\mathcal{N}_{k}
which insert
e_{i}\in\mathcal{M}_{i}
at the
\alpha
-th row.

13.4
\phi
-Compatibility in the Spectral Triples Framework

The first structure to consider are the Hilbert spaces
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
, that we can consider as left modules on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
via their corresponding representations that are not explicitly written in the following.

Definition 13.4.1
.

A morphism of Hilbert spaces
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
is
\phi
-compatible if
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(a\psi)=\phi(a)\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
and
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
(the representations
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}
are omitted in this relation).

For bimodules, the associated
\phi
-compatibility relation will be set up in subsection
15.1.2
. Given the morphism
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}:\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}\to\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
, one can decompose
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
as
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}=\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\oplus\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})^{\perp}
in a unique
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
-dependent way, where
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})=\Ran(\phi_{\mathcal{H}})
is the range of
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
. This implies that any operator
B
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
can be decomposed as
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi}&B_{\phi}^{\perp}\\
B_{\perp}^{\phi}&B_{\perp}^{\perp}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
with obvious notations, for instance
B_{\phi}^{\perp}:\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})^{\perp}\to\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
. In this orthogonal decomposition, one has
B^{\dagger}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi\dagger}&B_{\perp}^{\phi\dagger}\\
B_{\phi}^{\perp\dagger}&B_{\perp}^{\perp\dagger}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Definition 13.4.2
(
\phi
-compatibility of operators).

Given two operators
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
B
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
, we say that they are
\phi
-compatible if
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)=B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
(equality in
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
).

This definition makes sense since both sides belong to
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
. Notice that, by an abuse of notation, we use the terminology “
\phi
-compatibility” but this notion depends on the couple of maps
(\phi,\phi_{\mathcal{H}})
.

One can define a stronger
\phi
-compatibility between
A
and
B
:

Definition 13.4.3
(Strong
\phi
-compatibility of operators).

Given two operators
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
B
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
, we say that they are strong
\phi
-compatible if
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)=B\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
(equality in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
).

Notice that these two
\phi
-compatibility conditions imply that
\Ker\phi_{\mathcal{H}}\subset\Ker\phi_{\mathcal{H}}\circ A
, since, if
\psi\in\Ker\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
, then
0=B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)
in the first case, and similarly in the second case. A sufficient condition for this to hold for every
A
is to require
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
to be one-to-one.

Remark 13.4.4
.

Definition
13.4.1
implies that
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}(a)
and
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}(\phi(a))
are strong
\phi
-compatible for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
.

The following Proposition gives other consequences of the two definitions, where diagonality refers to the previously defined
2\times 2
matrix decomposition.

Proposition 13.4.5
.

A

1.

\phi
-compatibility and strong
\phi
-compatibility are stable under sums of operators.

2.

Compositions of strong
\phi
-compatible operators are strong
\phi
-compatible (this is not necessarily true for
\phi
-compatible operators).

3.

If
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
B
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
are strong
\phi
-compatible then
B_{\perp}^{\phi}=0
.

4.

Strong
\phi
-compatibility implies
\phi
-compatibility.

5.

If
B_{\perp}^{\phi}=0
, the
\phi
-compatibility implies the strong
\phi
-compatibility.

6.

When
B
is self-adjoint, strong
\phi
-compatibility implies that
B
is diagonal.

7.

If
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
B
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
are strong
\phi
-compatible and
A
and
B
are unitaries, then
A^{\dagger}
and
B^{\dagger}
are strong
\phi
-compatible and
B
is diagonal.

8.

For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, the operator
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
reduces to a diagonal matrix
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)_{\phi}^{\phi}&0\\
0&\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)_{\perp}^{\perp}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
.

Proof

Point
1
is obvious by linearity of the compatibility conditions and the matrix decompositions. For point
2
, let
A_{1},A_{2}
be two operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
B_{1},B_{2}
two operators on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
which are strong
\phi
-compatible with
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
respectively. Then for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
, one has
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A_{1}A_{2}\psi)=B_{1}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A_{2}\psi)=B_{1}B_{2}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
so that
A_{1}A_{2}
is strong
\phi
-compatible with
B_{1}B_{2}
. For
\phi
-compatibility, this line of reasoning is not possible in general.

One can identify
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
with
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\oplus\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})^{\perp}=\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
(resp.
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)
with
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
), so that
B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
identifies with
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
while
B\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
identifies with
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)\\
B_{\perp}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. The
\phi
-compatibility condition implies that the map
B_{\phi}^{\phi}:\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\to\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
is completely determined by
A
and
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
, while the strong
\phi
-compatibility condition implies firstly that
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)=B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
, and secondly that
B_{\perp}^{\phi}:\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\to\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})^{\perp}
is the zero map, which is point
3
. So, using these results, one gets that the strong
\phi
-compatibility implies the
\phi
-compatibility condition (which only constrains the
B_{\phi}^{\phi}
component of
B
), which is point
4
. For point
5
, from
B_{\perp}^{\phi}=0
and
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)=B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
, one gets
B\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)\\
B_{\perp}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)\\
0\end{smallmatrix}\right)=\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)
, which is the strong
\phi
-compatibility condition.

Point
6
: if
B
is self-adjoint, the condition
B=B^{\dagger}
implies
B_{\phi}^{\perp\dagger}=B_{\perp}^{\phi}=0
, so that
B
is diagonal.

Point
7
: if
A
and
B
are unitaries, then
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A^{\dagger}A\psi)
on the one hand and
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=B^{\dagger}B\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
on the other hand, so that
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A^{\dagger}A\psi)=B^{\dagger}B\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=B^{\dagger}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)
. Since
A
is invertible, any
\psi^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
can be written as
\psi^{\prime}=A\psi
, so that
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A^{\dagger}\psi)=B^{\dagger}\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
for any
\psi
, which proves that
A^{\dagger}
and
B^{\dagger}
are strong
\phi
-compatible. The strong
\phi
-compatibilities implies
B_{\perp}^{\phi}=0
and
(B^{\dagger})_{\perp}^{\phi}=B_{\phi}^{\perp\dagger}=0
, and so
B
is diagonal.

Point
8
: let us use the notation
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)_{\phi}^{\phi}&\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)_{\phi}^{\perp}\\
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)_{\perp}^{\phi}&\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)_{\perp}^{\perp}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
for any
a\in\mathcal{A}
. From Definition
13.4.1
,
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)
is strong
\phi
-compatible with
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}(a)
, so that
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)_{\perp}^{\phi}=0
. Since
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a^{\ast})=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)^{\dagger}
, this implies that
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a^{\ast})_{\phi}^{\perp}=0
for any
a
, so that
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}\circ\phi(a)
reduces to a diagonal matrix.

One can associate to
B=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi}&B_{\phi}^{\perp}\\
B_{\perp}^{\phi}&B_{\perp}^{\perp}\end{smallmatrix}\right)
the operator
\widehat{B}_{\phi}^{\phi}=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}B_{\phi}^{\phi}&0\\
0&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)
. Then the
\phi
-compatibility between
A
and
B
is equivalent to the strong
\phi
-compatibility between
A
and
\widehat{B}_{\phi}^{\phi}
.

Lemma 13.4.6
.

The
\phi
-compatibility of
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}
is equivalent to
\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{i}(a_{i}\psi_{i})=\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{i}(\psi_{i})
for any
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq k\leq s
,
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},i}
.

Proof

One has
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(a\psi)=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\big(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i}\psi_{i})\big)
and
\phi(a)\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\big(\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{i}(\psi_{i})\big)
so that
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(a\psi)=\phi(a)\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
is equivalent to
\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i}\psi_{i})=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{i}(\psi_{i})
for any
k
. Taking
a_{i}
and
\psi_{i}
non-zero only for one value of
i
, this implies that
\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i}\psi_{i})=\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{i}(\psi_{i})
for any
i
. Reciprocally, if this last equally is satisfied for any
i
, it implies the previous one by linearity.

Lemma 13.4.7
.

Two operators
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
B
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
are strong
\phi
-compatible if and only if
\sum_{j=1}^{r}\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{j}\circ A_{j}^{i}(\psi_{i})=\tsum_{\ell=1}^{s}B_{k}^{\ell}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H},\ell}^{i}(\psi_{i})
for any
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq k\leq s
, and
\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},i}
.

Two operators
A
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
and
B
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
are
\phi
-compatible if and only if
\sum_{j=1}^{r}\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{j}\circ A_{j}^{i}(\psi_{i})=\tsum_{\ell=1}^{s}B_{\phi,k}^{\phi,\ell}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H},\ell}^{i}(\psi_{i})
for any
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq k\leq s
, and
\psi_{i}\in\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A},i}
.

Proof

On the one hand, one has
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\big(\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{j}\circ A_{j}^{i}(\psi_{i})\big)
and on the other hand
B\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\big(\tsum_{\ell=1}^{s}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}B_{k}^{\ell}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H},\ell}^{i}(\psi_{i})\big)
. So, the relation
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(A\psi)=B\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\psi)
is equivalent to
\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{j}\circ A_{j}^{i}(\psi_{i})=\tsum_{\ell=1}^{s}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}B_{k}^{\ell}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H},\ell}^{i}(\psi_{i})
for any
k
. Taking
\psi_{i}
non-zero only for one value of
i
, this implies
\sum_{j=1}^{r}\phi_{\mathcal{H},k}^{j}\circ A_{j}^{i}(\psi_{i})=\tsum_{\ell=1}^{s}B_{k}^{\ell}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H},\ell}^{i}(\psi_{i})
for any
i
and
k
. By linearity, this relation implies the previous one.

Concerning the
\phi
-compatibility, one can replace
B
by
\widehat{B}_{\phi}^{\phi}
in the previous result. Since
\widehat{B}_{\phi}^{\phi}
acts only on
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
, one can replace
\widehat{B}_{\phi,k}^{\phi,\ell}
by the operators
B_{\phi,k}^{\phi,\ell}:\pi^{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}}_{\ell}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})\to\pi^{\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}}_{k}\circ\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
in the final relation.

We can extend the maps
\phi_{k}^{i}
as
\phi_{k_{0},\dots,k_{n}}^{i_{0},\dots,i_{n}}:\mathcal{A}^{\otimes}_{i_{0},\dots,i_{n}}\to\mathcal{B}^{\otimes}_{k_{0},\dots,k_{n}}
by
\phi_{k_{0},\dots,k_{n}}^{i_{0},\dots,i_{n}}(a^{0}_{i_{0}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n}_{i_{n}})\vcentcolon=\phi_{k_{0}}^{i_{0}}(a^{0}_{i_{0}})\otimes\cdots\otimes\phi_{k_{n}}^{i_{n}}(a^{n}_{i_{n}})
for any
i_{0},\dots,i_{n}
and
k_{0},\dots,k_{n}
, and then we define maps
\widehat{\phi}:\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{A}\to\mathfrak{T}^{n}\mathcal{B}
, for any
n\geq 1
, by
\toplus_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}=1}^{r}\big(a^{0}_{i}\otimes a^{1}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n-1}_{i_{n-1}}\otimes a^{n}_{j}\big)_{i,j=1}^{r}\mapsto\toplus_{k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1}=1}^{s}\big(\tsum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1}=1}^{r}\phi_{k,k_{1},\dots,k_{n-1},\ell}^{i,i_{1},\dots,i_{n-1},j}(a^{0}_{i}\otimes a^{1}_{i_{1}}\otimes\cdots\otimes a^{n-1}_{i_{n-1}}\otimes a^{n}_{j})\big)_{k,\ell=1}^{s}
, and, for
n=0
, the diagonal matrix with entries
a_{i}
at
(i,i)
is sent to the diagonal matrix with entries
\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi_{k}^{i}(a_{i})
at
(k,k)
. Using (
3.2.1
) and (
13.2.1
), one can check that
\widehat{\phi}:\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{A}\to\mathfrak{T}^{\bullet}\mathcal{B}
is a morphism of graded algebras and that
\widehat{\phi}(\mathbf{\Omega}^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{A}))\subset\mathbf{\Omega}^{1}_{U}(\mathcal{B})
, so that
\widehat{\phi}:\mathbf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{A})\to\mathbf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{U}(\mathcal{B})
is a morphism of graded algebras. These properties are consequences of the general situation that will be described in Sect.
15.1.1
.

From a purely mathematical point of view, the strong
\phi
-compatibility condition appears more natural, mainly because of properties given in points
2
,
6
and
7
and as we will see later with proposition
15.1.6
because it preserves spectral triples KO dimension. This condition implements equality of actions at the level of all
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
, making the relation dependent on the new degrees of freedom. But it is too restrictive for physics as it imposes a diagonal operator
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{B}}
and thus no couplings between new and old degrees of freedom along the sequence, which must lead to very limited models for physics.

On the contrary, the
\phi
-compatibility condition is only an equality at the level of
\phi_{\mathcal{H}}(\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}})
, then being less restrictive. It appears to be more natural since it is only based on inherited degrees of freedom and then appears closer to the relation of definition
13.4.1
which was first in this construction. Consequently, this condition appears to be more natural and in line with the
\phi
-compatibility condition on Hilbert spaces. Moreover, from a physical point of view, the
\phi
-compatibility condition is natural in the sense that it focuses on the conservation of the action of NCGFT’s operators at a given step on the embedding of the Hilbert space on which they previously act. As mentioned in remark
13.3.2
, this condition embodies the physical idea of the transport of the action of a physical operator
A\in\mathcal{A}
on
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{A}}
, when this one is embedded in
\mathcal{H}_{\mathcal{B}}
. Moreover, it allows the Dirac
\mathcal{D}_{\mathcal{B}}
to have off-diagonal terms. This permits couplings between the degrees of freedom of the injected and added fermionic fields, which seem to be a natural requirement for GUT models. This is why we have chosen to focus our study on this
\phi
-compatibility condition of operators to obtain NCGFTs of interest, as will be done later in section
15.2
.

Chapter 14
Derivation-based Approach to NCGFT on
AF
-Algebras

In this chapter, we propose to set up NCGFTs based on
AF
-Algebras using the derivation based approach. This will be done for one step in this two cases,
\phi:\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}\to\mathcal{B}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{k}}
and
\widehat{\phi}:\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}\to\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{B}
, then computing the link between the action at successive steps in subsection
14.3.1
. In section
14.4
, some physical applications concerning mass spectra generated by Spontaneous Symmetry Breaking Mechanisms (SSBM) are proposed using numerical computations for specific situations. This chapter is an account of the results given in
(
Masson and Nieuviarts, 2021
)
.

14.1
Modules and Connections for Direct Sum Algebras

We consider left modules on
\mathcal{A}
of the form
\mathcal{M}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{M}_{i}
where
\mathcal{M}_{i}
is a left module on
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. This requirement is sufficient for the particular situation
\mathcal{A}=M_{n_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus M_{n_{r}}
since, according to
(
Davidson, 1996
, Cor. III.1.2)
, the modules of this algebra are of the form
\mathbb{C}^{n_{1}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus\mathbb{C}^{n_{r}}\otimes\mathbb{C}^{\alpha_{r}}=M_{n_{1}\times\alpha_{1}}\toplus\cdots\toplus M_{n_{r}\times\alpha_{r}}
for some integers
\alpha_{i}
, where
M_{n_{i}\times\alpha_{i}}
is the vector space of
n_{i}\times\alpha_{i}
matrices over
\mathbb{C}
.

Define
\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}_{i}
as the projection on the
i
-th term and
\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\mathcal{M}
as the natural inclusion. Then,
\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}=\Id_{\mathcal{M}_{i}}
and for any
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}
and
e=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}
, one has
\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}(ae)=\pi^{i}(a)\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}(e)
and
\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}(\iota_{i}(a_{i})e)=a_{i}\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}(e)
.

Proposition 14.1.1
(Decomposition of connections).

A connection
\nabla
on the left
\mathcal{A}
module
\mathcal{M}
defines a unique family of connections
\nabla^{i}
on the left
\mathcal{A}_{i}
modules
\mathcal{M}_{i}
such that for any
e=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}
and any
\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}
, one has
