\displaystyle\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}e

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}e_{i}.

Denote by
R_{i}
the curvature associated to
\nabla^{i}
, then, for any
\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}
, any
\mathfrak{Y}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{Y}_{i}
, and any
e=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}
, one has

\displaystyle R(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})e

\displaystyle=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}R_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i},\mathfrak{Y}_{i})e_{i}

Proof

Since
\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})
, one has
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}e=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e
for any
e\in\mathcal{M}
. This implies that
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}
is completely given by the
r
maps
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
.

So, for a fixed
i
and for any
\mathfrak{X}_{i}\in\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})
, let us study the map
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
. Since
\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})=\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{i}\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})
, one has, for any
e\in\mathcal{M}
,
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e=\nabla_{\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{i}\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e=\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{i}\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e
so that
\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{j}\circ\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}=0
for
j\neq i
. In other words,
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e
takes its values in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
.

For a fixed
j
, take now
e=\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{j}(e_{j})
for some
e_{j}\in\mathcal{M}_{j}
. Since
\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j}\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{j}(e_{j})=\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{j}(e_{j})
one has
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{j}(e_{j})=\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}(\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j}\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{j}(e_{j}))=(\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})\mathord{\cdot}\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j})\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{j}(e_{j})+\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j}\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e_{j}=\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j}\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e_{j}
since
\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})\mathord{\cdot}\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j}=0
whatever
i
and
j
. If
j\neq i
, then
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{j}(e_{j})=\widehat{{\text{\char 49\relax}}}_{j}\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e_{j}=0
since
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}e_{j}
has only components in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
. This implies that
\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}
is only non zero on components in
\mathcal{M}_{i}
, and so defines a map

\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}\vcentcolon=\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\circ\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\mathcal{M}_{i}.

Then, by construction, one has, for
e=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}e_{i}
and
\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}
,
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}e=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}e_{i}
.

Now, let
f_{i}\in\mathcal{Z}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
, then

\displaystyle\nabla^{i}_{f_{i}\mathfrak{X}_{i}}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\left(\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(f_{i}\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})\right)=\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\left(\nabla_{\iota_{i}(f_{i})\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\left(\iota_{i}(f_{i})\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})\right)=f_{i}\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\circ\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=f_{i}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}e_{i}

Let
a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
, then

\displaystyle\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}a_{i}e_{i}

\displaystyle=\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\left(\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(a_{i}e_{i})\right)=\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\left(\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{i}(a_{i})\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\left((\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})\mathord{\cdot}\iota_{i}(a_{i}))\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})\right)+\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\left(\iota_{i}(a_{i})\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{X}_{i}\mathord{\cdot}a_{i})\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})+a_{i}\pi^{\text{{Mod}}}_{i}\circ\nabla_{\iota^{\Der}_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})}\circ\iota_{\text{{Mod}}}^{i}(e_{i})

\displaystyle=(\mathfrak{X}_{i}\mathord{\cdot}a_{i})e_{i}+a_{i}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}e_{i}

These two relations show that
\nabla^{i}
defines a connection on the left
\mathcal{A}_{i}
module
\mathcal{M}_{i}
.

Concerning the curvature, one has
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}\nabla_{\mathfrak{Y}}e=\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}(\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{Y}_{i}}e_{i})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{Y}_{i}}e_{i}
and
\nabla_{[\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y}]}e=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\nabla^{i}_{[\mathfrak{X}_{i},\mathfrak{Y}_{i}]}e_{i}
so that
R(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})e=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}([\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}},\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{Y}_{i}}]-\nabla^{i}_{[\mathfrak{X}_{i},\mathfrak{Y}_{i}]})e_{i}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}R_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i},\mathfrak{Y}_{i})e_{i}
.

Let us now consider the special case
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
with the natural left module structure. In that situation, we can characterize
\nabla
by its connection
1
-form
\omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{1}(\mathcal{A})
defined by
\omega(\mathfrak{X})\vcentcolon=\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}{\text{\char 49\relax}}
and its curvature takes the form of the multiplication on the right by the curvature
2
-form
\Omega\in\Omega_{\Der}^{2}(\mathcal{A})
defined by
\Omega(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})\vcentcolon=(\text{{d}}\omega)(\mathfrak{X},\mathfrak{Y})-[\omega(\mathfrak{X}),\omega(\mathfrak{Y})]
.

Proposition 14.1.2
.

In the previous situation, the decomposition of the connection
\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}
in Prop
14.1.1
is related to the decomposition of the connection
1
-form
\omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}
in Prop.
4.1.5
where
\omega_{i}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{1}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
is the connection
1
-form associated to the connection
\nabla^{i}
.

In the same way, the connection
2
-form
\Omega
of
\nabla
decomposes along the connection
2
-forms
\Omega_{i}
of
\nabla^{i}
:
\Omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\Omega_{i}
.

Proof

One has
{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{i}
where
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{i}
is the unit in
\mathcal{A}_{i}
. With
\mathfrak{X}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{X}_{i}
, one then has
\omega(\mathfrak{X})=\nabla_{\mathfrak{X}}{\text{\char 49\relax}}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\nabla^{i}_{\mathfrak{X}_{i}}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{i}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}(\mathfrak{X}_{i})
.

The curvature
2
-forms are defined in terms of differentials and Lie brackets (commutators in the respective algebras) from the connection
1
-forms. We have shown that these operations respect the decomposition of forms. This proves the relation of the curvature
2
-form of
\nabla
.

14.2
Lifting one Step of the Defining Inductive Sequence

As expected,
\phi
does not relate the centers of
\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}
and
\mathcal{B}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{k}}
. This implies in particular that we can’t expect to find or to construct a “general” map to inject
\Der(\mathcal{A})
into
\Der(\mathcal{B})
as modules over the centers, or, with less ambition, to inject a sub module and sub Lie algebra of
\Der(\mathcal{A})
into a sub module and sub Lie algebra of
\Der(\mathcal{B})
. This strategy may indeed require very specific situations.

Since it is convenient to consider all the derivations of
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, our approach is to keep track of the derivations in
\Der(\mathcal{B})
which “come from” (to be defined below) derivations in
\Der(\mathcal{A})
. These derivations will propagate along the sequence of the direct limit, while new derivations will be introduced at each step of the limit.

For any
i
, let us chose an
orthogonal basis

\{\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\alpha}\vcentcolon=\ad_{E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\alpha}}\}_{\alpha\in I_{i}}
of
\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})=\Int(M_{n_{i}})
where
E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\alpha}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n_{i}}
and
I_{i}
is a totally ordered set of cardinal
n_{i}^{2}-1
. For any
k
, we can introduce a basis of
\Der(\mathcal{B}_{k})=\Int(M_{m_{k}})
in two steps. Let us define the set

\displaystyle J^{\phi}_{k}\vcentcolon=\left\{(i,\alpha,\kappa)\,/\,i\in\{1,\dots,r\},\,\alpha\in\{1,\dots,\alpha_{ki}\},\,\kappa\in I_{i}\right\}

and for any
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)\in J^{\phi}_{k}
, define

\displaystyle E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}\vcentcolon=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\in\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}\text{ and }\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}\vcentcolon=\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}}\in\Der(\mathcal{B}_{k}).

The set
J^{\phi}_{k}
is totally ordered for
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)<\beta^{\prime}=(i^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})
iff
i<i^{\prime}
or [
i=i^{\prime}
and
\alpha<\alpha^{\prime}
] or [
i=i^{\prime}
and
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
and
\kappa<\kappa^{\prime}\in I_{i}
].

Denote by
g_{\mathcal{A}}
and
g_{\mathcal{B}}
the metrics on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
, defined as in Sect.
4.1.1
. We know that
g_{\mathcal{B}}(\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}},\ad_{E^{k^{\prime}}_{\mathcal{B},\beta^{\prime}}})=0
for
k\neq k^{\prime}
. So, let us consider a fixed value
k
. With the previous notations, one then has
g_{\mathcal{B}}(\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i,\alpha,\kappa)}},\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})}})=0
when
i\neq i^{\prime}
or [
i=i^{\prime}
and
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}
] since then the product of matrices
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i,\alpha,\kappa)}E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})}
is zero. Then one has

\displaystyle g^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta\beta^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon=g_{\mathcal{B}}(\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i,\alpha,\kappa)}},\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})}})=\tr(E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i,\alpha,\kappa)}E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})})=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}\tr(E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\mathcal{A}}(\ad_{E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}},\ad_{E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa^{\prime}}})=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g^{i}_{\mathcal{A},\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}

(14.2.1)

In the following, we will use the fact that the metric
(g^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta\beta^{\prime}})_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in J^{\phi}_{k}}
(and also its inverse
(g_{\mathcal{B},k}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}})_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in J^{\phi}_{k}}
) is diagonal by blocks along the divisions induced by the choice of a couple
(i,\alpha)
for which
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)
. Notice also that if the
\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
’s are orthogonal (resp. orthonormal) for
g_{\mathcal{A}}
, so are the
\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s for the metric
g_{\mathcal{B}}
for any
\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}
. This is the reason we chose to remove the “
\tfrac{1}{n}
” factor in front of the definition of the metrics (see Sect.
4.1.1
).

We can now complete the family
\{\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}\}_{\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}}
into a full basis of
\Der(\mathcal{B}_{k})
with the same notation,
\beta\in J_{k}=J^{\phi}_{k}\cup J^{c}_{k}
where
J^{c}_{k}
is a complementary set to get
\card(J_{k})=m_{k}^{2}-1
, in such a way that

\displaystyle g_{\mathcal{B}}(\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta},\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta^{\prime}})=0\text{ for any $\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}$ and $\beta^{\prime}\in J^{c}_{k}$.}

(14.2.2)

In other words, the metric
g_{\mathcal{B}}
is block diagonal and decomposes
\Der(\mathcal{B}_{k})
into two orthogonal summands. We choose any total order on
J_{k}
which extends the one on
J^{\phi}_{k}
. One knows that such a procedure is always possible, but for practical applications (for instance to construct gauge field theories, as in Sect.
14.4
), such a concrete basis could be useful, in particular since the basis that we construct is adapted to the map
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
. Lets then see how to construct such a basis adapted to
\phi
.

Recall that the maps
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}
send the matrix algebras
M_{n_{i}}
, for
i=1,\dots,r
and
\alpha=1,\dots,\alpha_{ki}
, on the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
, with a possible remaining block
{\text{\char 48\relax}}_{n_{0}}
on this diagonal. In order to manage this last block in the same way as the others, let us add the value
i=0
to refer to this block, with
\alpha_{k0}=1
. In the following, we will use the notation
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
with
i=0
(and
\alpha=1
) to refer to this block.

Consider any matrix
E\in M_{m_{k}}
which have only non zero entries outside the blocks
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
for
i=0,\ldots,r
. Then a straightforward computation using block matrices shows that
\tr(E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}E)=\tr(EE^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta})=0
for any
\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}
(the product in the trace is off diagonal). In the same way, for any
E
in the block
\phi^{0}_{k,1}(M_{n_{0}})
, one has
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}E=EE^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}=0
. This implies that
\ad_{E}
for any
E
outside of the blocks
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
for
i=1,\ldots,r
is orthogonal (for the metric induced by the trace) to
\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}}
for any
\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}
.

To complete the free family
\{\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}}\}_{\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}}
in
\Der(\mathcal{B}_{k})
, it is then sufficient to describe the elements
E\in\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}\simeq\Der(\mathcal{B}_{k})
“outside” of the blocks
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
for
i=1,\ldots,r
. We will do that using the block decomposition induced by the
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
.

We can introduce a first family of matrices
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
for new indices
\beta
(in a set
J^{c}_{k}
) as (traceless) matrices with non zero entries in
\phi^{0}_{k,1}(M_{n_{0}})
. There are
n_{0}^{2}-1
such elements.

Then, let us notice that for fixed
i=0,\dots,r
, the
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
for
\alpha=1,\dots,\alpha_{ki}
are in diagonal blocks
\alpha_{ki}n_{i}\times\alpha_{ki}n_{i}
. We call these blocks the
enveloping blocks
of
M_{n_{i}}
inside
M_{m_{k}}
. We introduce a second family of matrices
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
as matrices with non zero entries outside the enveloping blocks of the
M_{n_{i}}
’s. For every
i=0,\dots,r
, the row containing the enveloping block of
M_{n_{i}}
contains
\tsum_{i,i^{\prime},i\neq i^{\prime}}(\alpha_{ki}n_{i})(\alpha_{ki^{\prime}}n_{i^{\prime}})
entries (non zero outside the enveloping block).

The next level of blocks embedding we consider is the one inside the enveloping block of
M_{n_{i}}
, for every
i
. In such a block,
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}
maps
M_{n_{i}}
into the diagonal. For every
i=0,\dots,r
, we can then introduce a family of matrices
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
with non zero entries inside the enveloping blocks of
M_{n_{i}}
but outside the blocks
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
(for
\alpha=1,\dots,\alpha_{ki}
). For a fixed
i
, there are
\alpha_{ki}(\alpha_{ki}-1)
blocks of size
n_{i}\times n_{i}
, so that one can construct
\tsum_{i}\alpha_{ki}(\alpha_{ki}-1)(n_{i})^{2}
such matrices in the third family (as expected, for
i=0
there is no contribution since
\alpha_{k0}=1
).

Inside the enveloping block of
M_{n_{i}}
, we can also construct matrices with non zero entries in the blocks
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
. Indeed, for fixed
i
and
\alpha
, let us consider the matrix
E_{i}^{\alpha}
with
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n_{i}}
in the block
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(M_{n_{i}})
. For
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)
one has
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}E_{i}^{\alpha}=E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
and for
\beta^{\prime}=(i,\alpha^{\prime},\kappa)
with
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}
one has
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta^{\prime}}E_{i}^{\alpha}=0
. Notice that
E_{i}^{\alpha}\notin\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}
, but, for
\alpha=1,\dots,\alpha_{ki}-1
, the matrices
E_{i}^{\alpha}-E_{i}^{\alpha+1}
belong to
\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}
and, by the previous remark, are orthogonal to the
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
for
\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}
. These matrices constitute the fouth family: there are
\tsum_{i}(\alpha_{ki}-1)
such matrices (once again, there is not contribution for
i=0
)

The fifth and last family of matrices are constructed as
n_{i+1}E_{i}^{1}-n_{i}E_{i+1}^{1}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}
for
i=0,\dots,r-1
. These
r
matrices have entries in different enveloping blocks.

Let us collect the number of matrices
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
for
\beta\in J^{c}_{k}
that we have constructed:

\displaystyle\card(J^{c}_{k})

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}n_{0}^{2}-1+\tsum_{i\geq 0}\tsum_{i^{\prime}\geq 0;i\neq i^{\prime}}(\alpha_{ki}n_{i})(\alpha_{ki^{\prime}}n_{i^{\prime}})+\tsum_{i\geq 0}\alpha_{ki}(\alpha_{ki}-1)(n_{i})^{2}+\tsum_{i\geq 0}(\alpha_{ki}-1)+r\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}n_{0}^{2}+2\tsum_{i\geq 1}(\alpha_{ki}n_{i})n_{0}+\tsum_{i,i^{\prime}\geq 1}(\alpha_{ki}n_{i})(\alpha_{ki^{\prime}}n_{i^{\prime}})-\tsum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{ki}[(n_{i})^{2}-1]-1\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=(m_{k})^{2}-1-\card(J^{\phi}_{k})

where we have used
m_{k}=n_{0}+\tsum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{ki}n_{i}
and
\card(J^{\phi}_{k})=\tsum_{i\geq 1}\alpha_{ki}[(n_{i})^{2}-1]
. This shows that
\card(J_{k})=(m_{k})^{2}-1=\dim\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}
for
J_{k}\vcentcolon=J^{\phi}_{k}\cup J^{c}_{k}
and that the free family
\{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}\}_{\beta\in J_{k}}
is a basis for
\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}
. This construction and this computation can be adapted to the case
n_{0}=0
.

Notice that the derivations
\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}=\ad_{E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}}
for
\beta\in J^{c}_{k}
are not necessarily orthogonal. One can apply the Gram-Schmidt process to transform this basis into an orthonormal one.

Let us return to the main construction. Notice that (
14.2.2
) implies that the inverse of the matrix
(g^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta\beta^{\prime}})_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in J_{k}}
, denoted by
(g_{\mathcal{B},k}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}})_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in J_{k}}
, is also block diagonal and is such that
(g_{\mathcal{B},k}^{\beta\beta^{\prime}})_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in J^{\phi}_{k}}
is the inverse of
(g^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta\beta^{\prime}})_{\beta,\beta^{\prime}\in J^{\phi}_{k}}
with
g_{\mathcal{B},k}^{(i,\alpha,\kappa)(i^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})}=\delta^{ii^{\prime}}\delta^{\alpha\alpha^{\prime}}g_{\mathcal{A},i}^{\kappa\kappa^{\prime}}
.

The derivations
\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
for
\beta\in J^{\phi}_{k}
are the one “inherited” from the derivations on
\mathcal{A}
. We will use the convenient notation
\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})
for
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)
. A key ingredient to introduce the
\phi
-compatibility condition on forms is the following.

Lemma 14.2.1
.

For any
1\leq k\leq s
,
1\leq i,i^{\prime}\leq r
,
1\leq\alpha\leq\alpha_{ki}
,
1\leq\alpha^{\prime}\leq\alpha_{ki^{\prime}}
,
\kappa\in I_{i}
,
\kappa^{\prime}\in I_{i^{\prime}}
,
a_{i^{\prime}}\in\mathcal{A}_{i^{\prime}}
, one has

\displaystyle\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i,\alpha,\kappa)}\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i^{\prime}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}(a_{i^{\prime}})

\displaystyle=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i^{\prime}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}(a_{i^{\prime}})=\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\mathord{\cdot}a_{i^{\prime}})

and

\displaystyle[\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i,\alpha,\kappa)},\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\kappa^{\prime})}]

\displaystyle=[\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}),\phi^{i^{\prime}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}(\partial^{i^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa^{\prime}})]=\delta_{i,i^{\prime}}\delta_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}([\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa},\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa^{\prime}}])

Proof

\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})
is an inner derivation for the matrix
E^{k}_{\mathcal{B},(i,\alpha,\kappa)}
in which the only non zero part
E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
is located on the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
at a position depending on
i
and
\alpha
, see above. In the same way, the non zero part of
\phi^{i^{\prime}}_{k,\alpha^{\prime}}(a_{i^{\prime}})
is
a_{i^{\prime}}
on the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
. When
i\neq i^{\prime}
or
\alpha\neq\alpha^{\prime}
, the commutator of these two matrices is zero. When
i=i^{\prime}
and
\alpha=\alpha^{\prime}
, the commutator is
[E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa},a_{i}]=\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\mathord{\cdot}a_{i}
on the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
at the position designated by
i
and
\alpha
. This matrix is obviously
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\mathord{\cdot}a_{i})
. This proves the first relation.

The proof of the second relation relies on the same kind of argument since the
\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
are inner derivations.

Definition 14.2.2
(
\phi
-compatible forms).

A form
\omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
is
\phi
-compatible with a form
\eta=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\eta_{k}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})
if and only if for any
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq k\leq s
,
1\leq\alpha\leq\alpha_{ki}
,
\omega_{i}
and
\eta_{k}
have the same degree
p
and for any
\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p}}\in\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})
(
\kappa_{k}\in I_{i}
) , one has

\displaystyle\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p}})\right)

\displaystyle=\eta_{k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\dots,\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p}})\right)

(14.2.3)

Notice that the LHS of (
14.2.3
) has only non zero values in block matrices on the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
at a position which depends on
i
and
\alpha
(see above). This implies that the RHS has the same structure.

Proposition 14.2.3
.

Let
\omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i},\omega^{\prime}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega^{\prime}_{i}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
and
\eta=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\eta_{k},\eta^{\prime}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\eta^{\prime}_{k}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})
such that
\omega
is
\phi
-compatible with
\eta
and
\omega^{\prime}
is
\phi
-compatible with
\eta^{\prime}
. Then
\omega\wedge\omega^{\prime}
is
\phi
-compatible with
\eta\wedge\eta^{\prime}
and
\text{{d}}\omega
is
\phi
-compatible with
\text{{d}}\eta
.

Proof

Since the product of forms decompose along the indices
i
and
k
, we fix these indices in the proof and we suppose that the degres of
\omega_{i}
and
\omega^{\prime}_{i}
are
p
and
p^{\prime}
respectively. Inserting the RHS of (
14.2.3
) into (
3.3
) one has

\displaystyle(\eta_{k}\wedge\eta^{\prime}_{k})

\displaystyle\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\dots,\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+p^{\prime}}})\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{p!p^{\prime}!}\!\!\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+p^{\prime}}}(-1)^{\lvert\sigma\rvert}\eta_{k}\left(\!\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(1)}}),\dots,\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(p)}})\!\right)\eta^{\prime}_{k}\left(\!\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(p+1)}}),\dots,\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(p+p^{\prime})}})\!\right)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\frac{1}{p!p^{\prime}!}\sum_{\sigma\in\mathfrak{S}_{p+p^{\prime}}}(-1)^{\lvert\sigma\rvert}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\Big(\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(1)}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(p)}})\omega^{\prime}_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(p+1)}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{\sigma(p+p^{\prime})}})\Big)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left((\omega_{i}\wedge\omega^{\prime}_{i})(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+p^{\prime}}})\right)

In the same way, inserting the RHS of (
14.2.3
) into (
3.3
) one has

\displaystyle(\text{{d}}_{k}\eta_{k})

\displaystyle\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\dots,\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+1}})\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\sum_{k=1}^{p+1}(-1)^{k+1}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{k}})\mathord{\cdot}\eta_{k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{k}}}}}\dots,\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+1}})\right)\\
+\sum_{1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq p+1}(-1)^{k+k^{\prime}}\eta_{k}\Big(\left[\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{k}}),\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{k^{\prime}}})\right],\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{i}}}}}\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{k}}}}}\dots,\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+1}})\Big)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\sum_{k=1}^{p+1}(-1)^{k+1}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{k}}\mathord{\cdot}\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{k}}}}}\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+1}})\right)\\
+\sum_{1\leq k<k^{\prime}\leq p+1}(-1)^{k+k^{\prime}}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\omega_{i}([\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{k}},\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{k^{\prime}}}],\dots\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt.}\limits^{{\mathrel{\mathop{\kern 0.0pt\vee}\limits^{k}}}}}\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+1}})\right)\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left((\text{{d}}_{i}\omega_{i})(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p+1}})\right)

Let
\{\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}\}_{\kappa\in I_{i}}
be the dual basis of
\{\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\}_{\kappa\in I_{i}}
. Then one has
\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}(\partial^{i^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa^{\prime}})=\delta_{i}^{i^{\prime}}\delta_{\kappa^{\prime}}^{\kappa^{\prime}}
. In the same way, denote by
\{\theta^{\beta}_{\mathcal{B},k}\}_{\beta\in J_{k}}
the dual basis of
\{\partial^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}\}_{\beta\in J_{k}}
.

Remark 14.2.4
(
\phi
-compatibility and components of forms).

Let us first illustrate
\phi
-compatibility for
1
-forms. One has
\omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega^{i}_{\kappa}\otimes\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}
for
\omega^{i}_{\kappa}\in\mathcal{A}_{i}
and
\eta=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\eta^{k}_{\beta}\otimes\theta^{\beta}_{\mathcal{B},k}
for
\eta^{k}_{\beta}\in\mathcal{B}_{k}
. Then (
14.2.3
) reduces to
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\omega^{i}_{\kappa})=\eta^{k}_{(i,\alpha,\kappa)}
. This means that the components
\eta^{k}_{\beta}
of
\eta
in the “inherited directions”
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})
’s are inherited from
\omega
.

In the same way, for
p
-forms, the LHS of (
14.2.3
) is non zero only for components along the
\beta
’s of the form
(i,\alpha,\kappa)

with the same couple

(i,\alpha)
, and these components are given by the RHS. So, all the components
\eta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\dots\beta_{p}}
in the “inherited directions”
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{p}
for
\beta_{k}=(i,\alpha,\kappa_{k})
(same
i
and
\alpha
) are constrained by the
\phi
-compatibility condition.

Let
\mathcal{M}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
\mathcal{N}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\mathcal{N}_{k}
and let
\nabla^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}
be two connections on the
\mathcal{A}
-module
\mathcal{M}
and a
\mathcal{B}
-module
\mathcal{N}
with an one-to-one
\phi
-compatible map
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{N}
. We will used the maps
\phi_{\text{{Mod}},k,\alpha}^{i}:\mathcal{M}_{i}\to\mathcal{N}_{k}
. These connections define connections
\nabla^{\mathcal{M},i}
on
\mathcal{M}_{i}
and
\nabla^{\mathcal{N},k}
on
\mathcal{N}_{k}
.

Definition 14.2.5
.

The two connections
\nabla^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}
are said to be
\phi
-compatible if and only if, for any
1\leq i\leq r
,
1\leq k\leq s
,
1\leq\alpha\leq\alpha_{ki}
,
\kappa\in I_{i}
, one has

\displaystyle\phi_{\text{{Mod}},k,\alpha}^{i}\left(\nabla^{\mathcal{M},i}_{\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}}e_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\nabla^{\mathcal{N},k}_{\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})}\phi_{\text{{Mod}},k,\alpha}^{i}(e_{i}).

(14.2.4)

When
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{B}
, one can introduce the connection
1
-forms
\omega_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\omega_{\mathcal{N}}
for
\nabla^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}
. Then one has

Lemma 14.2.6
.

If
\omega_{\mathcal{M}}
and
\omega_{\mathcal{N}}
are
\phi
-compatible, then
\nabla^{\mathcal{M}}
and
\nabla^{\mathcal{N}}
are
\phi
-compatible.

Proof

Here we have
\phi_{\text{{Mod}}}=\phi
. Using
\mathcal{A}_{i}\ni e_{i}=e_{i}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
and the definitions of the connection
1
-forms, one has
\nabla^{\mathcal{M},i}_{\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}}e_{i}=(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\mathord{\cdot}e_{i}){\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}+e_{i}\omega_{\mathcal{M},i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})
, so that

\displaystyle\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\nabla^{\mathcal{M},i}_{\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}}e_{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left((\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\mathord{\cdot}e_{i}){\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}\right)+\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(e_{i}\omega_{\mathcal{M},i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})\right)\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})+\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})\omega_{\mathcal{N},k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})\right)\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})+\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})\nabla^{\mathcal{N},k}_{\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}

\displaystyle=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})+\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})\nabla^{\mathcal{N},k}_{\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{B}_{k}}=\nabla^{\mathcal{N},k}_{\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})

where we have used
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})
and
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\mathord{\cdot}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})=0
so that, using the Leibniz rule,
\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})\right)\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(e_{i})
.

Remark 14.2.7
.

Let us stress that the reverse of this Lemma is not true:
\phi
-compatibility between connections is weaker than
\phi
-compatibility between their connection
1
-forms. Indeed, let us assume that the two connections are
\phi
-compatible, that is that the first and last expressions are equal in the computation in the above proof. Then one can extract an equality at the second line between the connection
1
-forms: Take
e_{i}={\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}}
and since then the first terms in both sides are zero, one gets

\displaystyle\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\omega_{\mathcal{M},i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)

\displaystyle=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})\omega_{\mathcal{N},k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)

(14.2.5)

This a weaker relation than (
14.2.3
). In Remark
14.2.4
, we noticed that the
\phi
-compatibility (
14.2.3
) between forms implies that all the values
\omega_{\mathcal{N},k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)
come exactly from the values
\omega_{\mathcal{M},i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})
. What (
14.2.5
) says is that
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}({\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\mathcal{A}_{i}})
, as a projector, selects only a part of the matrix
\omega_{\mathcal{N},k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)
to be compared with the matrix
\omega_{\mathcal{M},i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})
. So some parts of the matrix
\omega_{\mathcal{N},k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)
may not be inherited.

For practical reasons, we prefer to deal with the stronger
\phi
-compatibility condition, since it permits to “trace” (to “follow”) the degrees of freedom of the
\omega_{\mathcal{M},i}
’s “inside” the
\omega_{\mathcal{N},k}
’s. The weaker
\phi
-compatibility condition mix up these degrees of freedom into the matrices
\omega_{\mathcal{N},k}\left(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)
.

Let us consider the following specific situation for
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
, where
\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}
is embedded into
\mathcal{B}=M_{m}
with
m\geq\tsum_{i=1}^{r}n_{i}
in such a way that each
M_{n_{i}}
appears once and only once on the diagonal of
M_{m}
. We consider on
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
the integral defined in Sect.
4.1.1
.

Proposition 14.2.8
.

Let
\omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}\in\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A})
and
\eta\in\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})
be such that
\eta
is
\phi
-compatible with
\omega
and
\eta
vanishes on every derivation
\partial_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
with
\beta\in J^{c}
(here we omit the
k=1
index). Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}\eta\wedge\star_{\mathcal{B}}\eta

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\omega\wedge\star_{\mathcal{A}}\omega

Proof

Since
k=1
and
\alpha=1
are the only possible values, to simplify the notations we write
\phi^{i}
for
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}
. We will refer to
\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})\subset M_{m}
as the
i
-th block on the diagonal of
M_{m}
. The metric
g_{\mathcal{B}}
induces an orthogonal decomposition of the derivations of
\mathcal{B}
which is compatible with these blocks. Two derivations
\ad_{E}
and
\ad_{E^{\prime}}
such that
E\in\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})
and
E^{\prime}
has no non vanishing entries in
\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})
are orthogonal. So,
\Der(\mathcal{B})=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\Der(\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}}))\oplus\Der(\mathcal{B})^{c}
where the decomposition is orthogonal for
g_{\mathcal{B}}
and
\Der(\mathcal{B})^{c}
are derivations “outside” of the
\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})
’s.

For any
p
one has
\eta(\phi^{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\dots,\phi^{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p}}))=\phi^{i}\left(\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p}})\right)
.
1
1

1

Since the
\omega_{i}
and
\eta
are not supposed to be homogeneous in
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
and
\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{B})
, one has to evaluate them against any number of derivations.
These are the only possible values for
\eta
. In particular
\eta
vanishes on any derivation in
\Der(\mathcal{B})^{c}
. Notice that
\phi^{i}\left(\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p}})\right)
is
\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\dots,\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{p}})\in M_{n_{i}}
at the
i
-th block on the diagonal of
M_{m}
. We can then write
\eta=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\eta_{i}
where
\eta_{i}\in\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})\otimes{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{\bullet}\Der(\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}}))^{*}\subset M_{m}\otimes{\textstyle\bigwedge}^{\bullet}\Der(\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}}))^{*}
.

Let us define the metric
g_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}
as the restriction of
g_{\mathcal{B}}
to
\Der(\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}}))
. From this metric we construct its Hodge
\star
-operator
\star_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}
and its NC integral
\int_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}
along the derivations of the matrix block
\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})
. Then we can apply Lemma
4.1.6
, where the linear form
\tau
is the ordinary trace on
M_{m}
, to get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}\eta\wedge\star_{\mathcal{B}}\eta

\displaystyle=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}\eta_{i}\wedge\star_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}\eta_{i}

One has
\Der(\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}}))\simeq\Der(M_{n_{i}})
and, using (
14.2.1
),
g_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}
becomes
g_{\mathcal{A},i}
in this identification, so that
\star_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}
corresponds to
\star_{M_{n_{i}}}
. In this identification, the form
\eta_{i}
is then
\omega_{i}
, so that
\int_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}\eta_{i}\wedge\star_{\phi^{i}(M_{n_{i}})}\eta_{i}=\int_{M_{n_{i}}}\omega_{i}\wedge\star_{M_{n_{i}}}\omega_{i}
. This shows that
\int_{\mathcal{B}}\eta\wedge\star_{\mathcal{B}}\eta=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{M_{n_{i}}}\omega_{i}\wedge\star_{M_{n_{i}}}\omega_{i}=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\omega\wedge\star_{\mathcal{A}}\omega
by (
4.1.6
).

A slight adjustment of the proof of Prop.
14.2.8
gives (with the same notations):

Corollary 14.2.9
.

Suppose that
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
includes
\kappa_{i}
times
M_{n_{i}}
on the diagonal of
M_{m}
. Then, with the same assumptions, one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{B}}\eta\wedge\star_{\mathcal{B}}\eta

\displaystyle=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\kappa_{i}\int_{i}\omega_{i}\wedge\star_{i}\omega_{i}

14.3
Direct Limit of NC Gauge Field Theories

14.3.1
\phi
-Compatibility of NC Gauge Field Theories

As mentioned in Sect.
2.2
, we will consider non unital
*
-homomorphisms
\phi:\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}\to\mathcal{B}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{k}}
. The reason for this choice is that we would like to cover physical situations where the gauge group are enlarged at each step of the defining inductive sequence
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\phi_{n,m})\,/\,0\leq n<m\}
. For instance, one may ask for the inclusion of
U(2)
into
U(3)
, which can be performed in our framework by considering a natural inclusion
\phi:M_{2}\to M_{3}
. This inclusion cannot be unital. A unital morphism would require for instance to consider the inclusion
\phi:M_{1}\oplus M_{2}\to M_{3}
, which may not correspond to a phenomenological requirement.

We first consider a NCGFT on the algebra
\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}
. Let us use the notations of Sect.
14.2
: for any
i
, let
\{\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\vcentcolon=\ad_{E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}}\}_{\kappa\in I_{i}}
be an orthogonal basis of
\Der(\mathcal{A}_{i})=\Int(M_{n_{i}})
where
E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\in\mathfrak{sl}_{n_{i}}
and
I_{i}
is a totally ordered set of cardinal
n_{i}^{2}-1
, and let
\{\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}\}_{\kappa\in I_{i}}
be the dual basis of
\{\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\}_{\kappa\in I_{i}}
.

Since we are interested in the manipulation of connections as
1
-forms, we restrict our analysis to the left module
\mathcal{M}=\mathcal{A}
. From Example
11.5.2
and the results in Sect.
14.1
, with obvious notations, a connection
1
-form can be written as
\omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\omega_{i}
and its curvature
2
-form as
\Omega=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}\Omega_{i}
, with
\omega_{i}=\omega^{i}_{\kappa}\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}=\mathring{\omega}_{i}-B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}=(E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}-B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}
and
\Omega_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\theta^{\kappa_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}\wedge\theta^{\kappa_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}
with
\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}=-([B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}]-C(n_{i})_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}^{\kappa_{3}}B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{3}})
where
C(n_{i})_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}^{\kappa_{3}}
are the structure constants for the basis
\{E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}\}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{n_{i}}
.

The natural action for this NCGFT is then

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=-\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{i}\Omega_{i}\wedge\star_{i}\Omega_{i}=-\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tfrac{1}{2}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\Omega^{i,\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})=-\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\in I_{i}}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})^{2}

\displaystyle=-\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}\in I_{i}}\tr([B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}]-C(n_{i})_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}^{\kappa_{3}}B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{3}})^{2}

where in the last line we have used the fact that the metric is diagonal.

As in Subsect
11.5.3
, one can also consider a NCGFT on the algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}C^{\infty}(M)\otimes M_{n_{i}}
for a manifold
M
. Then
\omega_{i}=A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}+(E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}-B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}
and
\Omega_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Omega^{i}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\text{{d}}x^{\mu_{1}}\wedge\text{{d}}x^{\mu_{2}}+\Omega^{i}_{\mu\kappa}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}\wedge\theta^{\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\theta^{\kappa_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}\wedge\theta^{\kappa_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},i}
with

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu_{1}}A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{2}}-\partial_{\mu_{2}}A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{1}}-[A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{1}},A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{2}}],

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\mu\kappa}

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{\mu}B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}-[A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu},B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}]),

\displaystyle\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}

\displaystyle=-([B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}]-C(n_{i})_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}^{\kappa_{3}}B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{3}}).

In that case, the natural action is

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}-\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{M}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\Omega^{i,\mu_{1}\mu_{2}})+\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\mu\kappa}\Omega^{i,\mu\kappa})+\tfrac{1}{2}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\Omega^{i,\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})\Big)\sqrt{\lvert g_{M}\rvert}\text{{d}}x\end{multlined}

where
g_{M}
is a metric on
M
.

This action shares the same main features as mentioned in Subsect
11.5.3
: it makes appear a SSBM thanks to the presence of the scalar fields
B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}=B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa^{\prime}}+iB^{i,0}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n_{i}}
which can be non zero for a minimal configuration of the Higgs potential
-\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\Omega^{i,\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})
. Then the couplings in
-\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\mu\kappa}\Omega^{i,\mu\kappa})
induce mass terms for the (gauge bosons) fields
A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}=A^{i,\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}+iA^{i,0}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n_{i}}
. We will concentrate of this feature in the following.

In order to simplify the analysis of the relation between NCGFT defined at each step of an inductive sequence of finite dimensional algebras
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\phi_{n,m})\,/\,0\leq n<m\}
, we will consider a unique inclusion
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
with
\mathcal{A}=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}M_{n_{i}}
and
\mathcal{B}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}M_{m_{k}}
as in Sect.
14.2
.

Let
\eta=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\eta_{k}
be a connection
1
-form on
\mathcal{B}
for the module
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{B}
. Denote by
\Theta=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}\Theta_{k}
its curvature
2
-form. We use the notation
\eta_{k}=\eta^{k}_{\beta}\theta^{\beta}_{\!\mathcal{B},k}=(E^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}-B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta})\theta^{\beta}_{\!\mathcal{B},k}
and
\Theta_{k}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\theta^{\beta_{1}}_{\!\mathcal{B},k}\wedge\theta^{\beta_{2}}_{\!\mathcal{B},k}
with
\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}=-([B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta_{1}},B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta_{2}}]-C(m_{k})_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}^{\beta_{3}}B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta_{3}})
where
C(m_{k})_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}^{\beta_{3}}
are the structure constants for the basis
\{E^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}\}
of
\mathfrak{sl}_{m_{k}}
.

We suppose that
\eta
is
\phi
-compatible with
\omega
. This implies that, for all
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)\in J^{\phi}_{k}
,
\eta^{k}_{\beta}=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\omega^{i}_{\kappa})
and that
B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})
.

Lemma 14.3.1
.

For any
\beta_{1}=(i_{1},\alpha_{1},\kappa_{1}),\beta_{2}=(i_{2},\alpha_{2},\kappa_{2})\in J^{\phi}_{k}
,
\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}=0
for
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
or
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
and
\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})
for
i=i_{1}=i_{2}
and
\alpha=\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
.

Proof

One has to evaluate

\displaystyle\Theta_{k}

\displaystyle(\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}))

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}})\mathord{\cdot}\eta_{k}(\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}))-\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}})\mathord{\cdot}\eta_{k}(\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}))\\
-\eta_{k}([\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}})])-[\eta_{k}(\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}})),\eta_{k}(\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}))]\end{multlined}

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\omega_{i_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}))-\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}})\mathord{\cdot}\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\omega_{i_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}))\\
-\eta_{k}([\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}}),\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}})])-[\phi^{i_{1}}_{k,\alpha_{1}}(\omega_{i_{1}}(\partial^{i_{1}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}})),\phi^{i_{2}}_{k,\alpha_{2}}(\omega_{i_{2}}(\partial^{i_{2}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}))]\end{multlined}

For
i_{1}\neq i_{2}
or
\alpha_{1}\neq\alpha_{2}
, from Lemma
14.2.1
, all the terms in the first line vanish while the last commutator is zero since the two matrices involved do not occupy the same position on the diagonal of
M_{m_{k}}
. For
i_{1}=i_{2}=i
and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}=\alpha
, the expression reduces to
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\Omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{1}},\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa_{2}}))
(which is also a consequence of Prop.
14.2.3
).

Notice that this result is also a direct consequence of the expression of
\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}
in terms of the
B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s.

The curvature components
\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}
for
\beta_{1},\beta_{2}\in J_{k}
can be separated according to the 3 possibilities: (1)
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
in
J^{\phi}_{k}\times J^{\phi}_{k}
; (2)
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
or
(\beta_{2},\beta_{1})
in
J^{\phi}_{k}\times J^{c}_{k}
; (3)
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})
in
J^{c}_{k}\times J^{c}_{k}
. From (
14.2.2
), the metric
g_{\mathcal{B}}
(and its inverse) is block diagonal for these subsets of indices. The natural action on
\mathcal{B}
can then be decomposed as

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=-\tsum_{k=1}^{s}\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\in J_{k}}\tr(\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\Theta^{k,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}})

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}-\tsum_{k=1}^{s}\Big(\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\in J^{\phi}_{k}}\tr(\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\Theta^{k,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}})+\tsum_{\beta_{1}\in J^{\phi}_{k},\beta_{2}\in J^{c}_{k}}\tr(\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\Theta^{k,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}})+\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\in J^{c}_{k}}\tr(\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\Theta^{k,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}})\Big)\end{multlined}

For fixed
k
, let us consider the first summation on
J^{\phi}_{k}
. By Lemma
14.3.1
, the two indices
\beta_{1}=(i_{1},\alpha_{1},\kappa_{1}),\beta_{2}=(i_{2},\alpha_{2},\kappa_{2})\in J^{\phi}_{k}
must satisfy
i_{1}=i_{2}
and
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}
to get a non zero contribution, so that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}\in J^{\phi}_{k}}\tr(\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\Theta^{k,\beta_{1}\beta_{2}})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tsum_{\alpha=1}^{\alpha_{ki}}\tsum_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in I_{i}}\tr(\Theta^{k}_{(i,\alpha,\kappa_{1})(i,\alpha,\kappa_{2})}\Theta^{k,(i,\alpha,\kappa_{1})(i,\alpha,\kappa_{2})})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tsum_{\alpha=1}^{\alpha_{ki}}\tsum_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in I_{i}}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\Omega^{i,\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})=\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\alpha_{ki}\tsum_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}\in I_{i}}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\Omega^{i,\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})

where we have used (
14.2.1
) (which holds true also for the inverse metrics) to make an equivalence between raising the indices
\beta
and raising the indices
\kappa
.

This relation tells us that the
action on
\mathcal{B}
contains copies of terms from the action on
\mathcal{A}
. As expected, these terms involve the degrees of freedom which are inherited on
\mathcal{B}
from those on
\mathcal{A}
. They involve also the multiplicities of the inclusions of
M_{n_{i}}
into
M_{m_{k}}
. This implies that the relative weights of these terms are not the same on
\mathcal{B}
as they are on
\mathcal{A}
.

Let us now consider the algebra
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}\vcentcolon=C^{\infty}(M)\otimes\mathcal{B}=\toplus_{k=1}^{s}C^{\infty}(M)\otimes M_{m_{k}}
. The connection
1
-form is parametrized as
\eta_{k}=A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}+(E^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}-B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta})\theta^{\beta}_{\!\mathcal{B},k}
. We extend the
\phi
-compatibility between
\eta
and
\omega
on the geometrical part by the condition that
A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}=\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi^{i}_{k}(A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu})+A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
where
A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}\in M_{m_{k}}
has zero entries in the image of
\phi_{k}
(which is concentred as blocks on the diagonal). In other words, all the degrees of freedom in
A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
are copied into
A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
according to the map
\phi
.

From this decomposition, the components of the curvature
2
-forms
\Theta_{k}=\tfrac{1}{2}\Theta^{k}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}\text{{d}}x^{\mu_{1}}\wedge\text{{d}}x^{\mu_{2}}+\Theta^{k}_{\mu\beta}\text{{d}}x^{\mu}\wedge\theta^{\beta}_{\!\mathcal{B},k}+\tfrac{1}{2}\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}\theta^{\beta_{1}}_{\!\mathcal{B},k}\wedge\theta^{\beta_{2}}_{\!\mathcal{B},i}
can be separated into inherited components from the curvature
2
-forms
\Omega_{i}
on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
, interactions terms between inherited components of the
\omega_{i}

1
-forms with new components of the
\eta_{k}

1
-forms, and completely new terms from new components of the
\eta_{k}
. Explicitly, one has

\displaystyle\Theta^{k}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu_{1}}A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{2}}-\partial_{\mu_{2}}A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{1}}-[A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{1}},A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{2}}]

\displaystyle=\begin{multlined}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi^{i}_{k}\left(\partial_{\mu_{1}}A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{2}}-\partial_{\mu_{2}}A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{1}}-[A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{1}},A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{2}}]\right)\\
-\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\left([\phi^{i}_{k}(A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{1}}),A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{2}}]+[A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{1}},\phi^{i}_{k}(A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu_{2}})]\right)+\partial_{\mu_{1}}A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{2}}-\partial_{\mu_{2}}A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{1}}-[A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{1}},A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu_{2}}]\end{multlined}

In the same way, for any
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)\in J^{\phi}_{k}
, one has

\displaystyle\Theta^{k}_{\mu\beta}

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{\mu}B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}-[A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu},B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}])=-\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\partial_{\mu}B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}-[A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu},B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}]\right)+[A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu},\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})]

where the last term is off diagonal. For
\beta\in J^{c}_{k}
,
\Theta^{k}_{\mu\beta}
depends on the fields
A^{k,c}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
which couple with
B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
. Finally,
\Theta^{k}_{\beta_{1}\beta_{2}}
has been explored before.

Denote by
\mathcal{A}^{\times}
and
\mathcal{B}^{\times}
the groups of invertible elements in
\mathcal{A}
and
\mathcal{B}
and let us define
\widetilde{\phi}:\mathcal{A}^{\times}\to\mathcal{B}^{\times}
, for any
a=\toplus_{i=1}^{r}a_{i}\in\mathcal{A}^{\times}
, as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\phi}^{k}(a)\vcentcolon=\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{k}\circ\widetilde{\phi}(a)

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}a_{1}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{k1}}&0&\cdots&0&0\\
0&a_{2}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{k2}}&\cdots&0&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&a_{r}\otimes{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{\alpha_{kr}}&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0&{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n_{0}}\end{pmatrix}

It is easy to check that
\widetilde{\phi}
is a morphism of groups: for
a\in\mathcal{A}^{\times}
, one has
\widetilde{\phi}(a)\in\mathcal{B}^{\times}
and
\widetilde{\phi}(a)^{-1}=\widetilde{\phi}(a^{-1})
. One has also
\widetilde{\phi}(a)^{*}=\widetilde{\phi}(a^{*})
so that if
u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
is a unitary element in
\mathcal{A}
, so is
\widetilde{\phi}(u)
in
\mathcal{B}
.

Lemma 14.3.2
.

For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
and any
u\in\mathcal{A}^{\times}
, one has
\widetilde{\phi}(u)\phi(a)=\phi(ua)
and
\phi(a)\widetilde{\phi}(u)=\phi(au)
. For any
k\in\{1,\dots,s\}
, any
i\in\{1,\dots,r\}
, and any
\alpha\in\{1,\dots,\alpha_{ki}\}
, one has
\widetilde{\phi}(u)\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(a)=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(ua)
and
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(a)\widetilde{\phi}(u)=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(au)
.

Proof

From (
13.2.5
), it is sufficient to prove the last relations involving
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}
. Since
\widetilde{\phi}(u)
differs only from
\phi(u)
at the last
n_{0}\times n_{0}
block entry on the diagonal where
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(a)
is zero, one has
\widetilde{\phi}(u)\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(a)=\phi(u)\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(a)=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(ua)
by (
13.2.5
) and (
13.2.7
). The same proof applies for the right multiplication by
u
.

Proposition 14.3.3
.

Let
\omega
be a connection
1
-form on
\mathcal{A}
and let
\eta
be a
\phi
-compatible connection
1
-form on
\mathcal{B}
. Let
u\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{A})
and
v\vcentcolon=\widetilde{\phi}(u)\in\mathcal{U}(\mathcal{B})
. Then
\omega^{u}
and
\eta^{v}
are
\phi
-compatible.

Proof

Recall that
\omega^{u}=u^{-1}\omega u-u^{-1}(\text{{d}}u)
and
\eta^{v}=v^{-1}\eta v-v^{-1}(\text{{d}}v)
. Notice that
\pi_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(\omega^{u})=u_{i}^{-1}\omega_{i}u_{i}-u_{i}^{-1}(\text{{d}}u_{i})\in\Omega_{\Der}^{1}(\mathcal{A}_{i})
and similarly
\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{k}(\eta^{v})=v_{k}^{-1}\eta_{k}v_{k}-v_{k}^{-1}(\text{{d}}v_{k})\in\Omega_{\Der}^{1}(\mathcal{B}_{k})
. For any
k
and any
\beta=(i,\alpha,\kappa)\in J^{\phi}_{k}
, one has

\displaystyle\pi_{\mathcal{B}}^{k}(\eta^{v})(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}))

\displaystyle=v_{k}^{-1}\eta_{k}(\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}))v_{k}-v_{k}^{-1}[\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}),v_{k}]

\displaystyle=v_{k}^{-1}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)v_{k}-v_{k}^{-1}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}([E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa},u_{i}])

\displaystyle=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(u_{i}^{-1}\omega_{i}(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})u_{i}-u_{i}^{-1}(\text{{d}}u_{i})(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}\left(\pi_{\mathcal{A}}^{i}(\omega^{u})(\partial^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa})\right)

where we have used Lemma
14.3.2
.

We have a similar result for connections on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
:

Proposition 14.3.4
.

Let
\omega
be a connection
1
-form on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and let
\eta
be a
\phi
-compatible (in the extended version) connection
1
-form on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
. Let
u\in\mathcal{U}(\widehat{\mathcal{A}})
and
v\vcentcolon=\widetilde{\phi}(u)\in\mathcal{U}(\widehat{\mathcal{B}})
. Then
\omega^{u}
and
\eta^{v}
are
\phi
-compatible (in the extended version).

Proof

Concerning the algebraic parts of
\omega_{i}
and
\eta_{k}
, the proof is the same as for Prop.
14.3.3
. It remains to show that the
A^{i,u_{i}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}\vcentcolon=u_{i}^{-1}A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}u_{i}-u_{i}^{-1}\partial_{\mu}u_{i}
are copied into
A^{k,v_{k}}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}\vcentcolon=v_{k}^{-1}A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}v_{k}-v_{k}^{-1}\partial_{\mu}v_{k}
according to the map
\phi
. Using the fact that
v_{k}
is block diagonal, and that these blocks are
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(u_{i})
or
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n_{0}}
, the diagonal part of the first term
v_{k}^{-1}A^{k}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}v_{k}
is exactly
\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\phi^{i}_{k}(u_{i}^{-1}A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}u_{i})
. The second term
v_{k}^{-1}\partial_{\mu}v_{k}
contains only blocks on the diagonal: the zero block from the block
{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n_{0}}
and blocks
\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(u_{i})^{-1}\partial_{\mu}\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(u_{i})=\phi^{i}_{k,\alpha}(u_{i}^{-1}\partial_{\mu}u_{i})
otherwise. This proves that the blocks on the diagonal of
A^{k,v_{k}}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
are copies of the
A^{i,u_{i}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
according to the map
\phi
. Obviously, the off diagonal part of
A^{k,v_{k}}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
mixes the degrees of freedom from the
A^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
’s and
u_{i}
’s.

Prop.
14.3.3
and
14.3.4
show that
\phi
-compatibility of connections is compatible with gauge transformations.

We have now at hand all the technical ingredients to discuss NCGFT on the
AF

C^{*}
-algebra defined by a sequence
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\phi_{n,m})\,/\,0\leq n<m\}
. This NCGFT uses the derivation-based differential calculus constructed on the dense “smooth” subalgebra
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}\vcentcolon=\cup_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{A}_{n}
as the inductive limit of the differential calculi
(\Omega_{\Der}^{\bullet}(\mathcal{A}_{n}),\text{{d}})
, and a natural module is the algebra
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}
itself. With obvious notations, the same holds for
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
. All these constructions are canonical. A connection is constructed as a limit of connections on each
\mathcal{A}_{n}
(with module the algebra itself). If we insist the connection
1
-form on
\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
to be
\phi_{n,n+1}
-compatible with the connection
1
-form on
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, then some degrees of freedom in this connection are inherited from those of the connections on
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, and new degrees of freedom are added. This limiting procedure is compatible with a good notion of gauge transformations (see Prop.
14.3.3
and Prop.
14.3.4
).

Concerning the dynamics, the terms in the action functional on
\mathcal{A}_{n}
can be found (with possible different weights) as terms in the action functional on
\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
. If a solution for the gauge field degrees of freedom has been found on
\mathcal{A}_{n}
, then these degrees of freedom appear as
fixed fields
in the action on
\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
, and so as constrains when one solves the field equations on
\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
for the new fields (non inherited degrees of freedom). The same applies to an inductive sequence
\{(\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{n},\phi_{n,m})\,/\,0\leq n<m\}
. The Lagrangian on
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}
(or
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
) should be constructed as a limiting procedure by adding new terms at each step in order to take into account the new degrees of freedom. But then this Lagrangian could contain an infinite number of terms. From a physical point of view, we do not expect to reach that point: only some finite dimensional “approximations” (at some levels
n
) can be considered and tested in experiments.
In other word, the purpose of our construction is not to define a “target” NCGFT (which could be quite singular) but to construct a direct sequence of finite dimensional NCFGT.
We expect all the empirical data to be encoded into this sequence which
formally defines
a NCFGT on
\mathcal{A}_{\infty}
(or
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}_{\infty}
).

As already mentioned at the end of Sect.
2.2
, the
*
-homomorphisms
\phi_{n,n+1}:\mathcal{A}_{n}\to\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
are only characterized up to unitary equivalence in
\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
. We have shown that the action of such an unitary equivalence, which takes the form of an inner automorphism on
\mathcal{A}_{n+1}
, is a transport of structures that does not change the physics. This is why it is convenient to work with the standard form used in this thesis for these
*
-homomorphisms.

14.4
Numerical Exploration of the SSBM

We would like now to concentrate on the SSBM in our framework. Using previous notations, when the Higgs potential
-\tfrac{1}{2}\tsum_{i=1}^{r}\tr(\Omega^{i}_{\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}}\Omega^{i,\kappa_{1}\kappa_{2}})
for
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is minimized, the degrees of freedom in the
B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}=B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa^{\prime}}+iB^{i,0}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{n_{i}}
are fixed (possibly with a choice in many possible configurations) and the
\phi
-compatibility transports these values into the
B^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}=B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}E^{k}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta^{\prime}}+iB^{k,0}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}{\text{\char 49\relax}}_{m_{i}}
’s. Then, using these fixed values, minimizing the Higgs potential for
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
only concerns a subset of all the
B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s. The configuration they define is not necessarily the minimum of the Higgs potential for
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
if it were computed along all the
B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s.

The configuration of the fields
B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
’s on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(resp. the fields
B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
) induces a mass spectrum for the gauge fields
A^{i,\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
’s (resp. the gauge fields
A^{k,\beta}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
’s). In order to illustrate the way the masses of the
A^{k,\beta}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
’s are related to the masses of the
A^{i,\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
’s by the constraints induced by
\phi
, we have produced numerical computations of the mass spectra for simple situations
\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
. These computations have been performed using the software
Mathematica
. The following situations have been considered:

1.

\mathcal{A}=M_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}=M_{3}
. This is the minimal non trivial situation one can consider. It illustrates many features of the other situations concerning the masses of the fields
A^{k,\beta}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
’s.

2.

\mathcal{A}=M_{2}\oplus M_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}=M_{4}
. This situation is used to illustrate how two different configurations for the fields
B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
’s (one for each
M_{2}
) can conflict to produce a rich typology for the masses of the fields
A^{k,\beta}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
’s.

3.

\mathcal{A}=M_{2}\oplus M_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}=M_{5}
. This situation is used to show, by comparison with the preceding one, how the target algebra influences the mass spectrum.

4.

\mathcal{A}=M_{2}\oplus M_{3}
and
\mathcal{B}=M_{5}
. This situation is used to show, by comparison with the preceding one, how the source algebra influences the mass spectrum.

Due to the large number of parameters involved in the mathematical expressions, the numerical computations cannot explore the full space of configurations for the fields
B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
’s. This is why we have chosen to work with a very simplified situation: for every
i
, the fields
B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
’s are parametrized by a single real parameter
\lambda_{i}
which interpolates, on the interval
[0,1]
, between the null-configuration and the basis-configuration (see Sect.
11.5.3
) as
B^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}=\lambda_{i}E^{i}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
. For each value of the
\lambda_{i}
’s, the minimum of the Higgs potential on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
along the fields
B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s which are not inherited (via
\phi
) is computed. Then this minimum configuration is inserted into the couplings with the fields
A^{k,\beta}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
’s to compute the mass spectrum. A comparison with the mass spectrum of the fields
A^{i,\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
’s is easily done, since this spectrum is fully degenerate: all these fields have the same mass
\lambda_{i}=\sqrt{2n_{i}}
according to Lemma
11.5.2
.

Let us first make some general remarks on the expected results. When
\lambda_{i}=0
for all
i=1,\dots,r
(in our examples
r=2
at most), the configuration on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is the null-configuration. So, we expect the constraints due to the values of the fields
B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}=0
when computing the minimum of the Higgs potential on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
to produce the null-configuration for the fields
B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s. In the same way, when
\lambda_{i}=1
for all
i=1,\dots,r
, the configuration on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
is the basis-configuration, and since
\phi
preserves Lie brackets, the minimum of the Higgs potential on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is expected to be the basis-configuration for the fields
B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s. All the numerical computations presented below are consistent with these expected results.

When
\lambda_{i}
is neither
0
nor
1
, the configuration for the fields
B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
’s is not a minimum of the Higgs potential on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
(with value
0
in that case). Nevertheless, we consider these configuration as “possible” since
\mathcal{A}
is not necessarily the first algebra in the sequence
\{(\mathcal{A}_{n},\phi_{n,m})\,/\,0\leq n<m\}
. Indeed, as the results will show, and as already mentioned, the minimum of the Higgs potential on
\widehat{\mathcal{B}}
is not the minimum along all the
B^{k,\beta^{\prime}}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
’s, and its value can be non zero. So, we are not reduced to considering only zero minima on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
and it is legitimate to explore other configurations for the fields
B^{i,\kappa^{\prime}}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
’s on
\widehat{\mathcal{A}}
.

Before describing the four cases, let us consider the situation in Fig.
14.1
which concerns the algebra
M_{2}
only. The plot in Fig.
14.1a
is the Higgs potential for the fields
B^{1}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}=\lambda_{1}E^{1}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
depending only on
\lambda_{1}
. It is a quadratic polynomials in
\lambda_{1}
and it looks very much like the Higgs potential of the SMPP in this approximation (reduction to a
1
-parameter dependency). The plot in Fig.
14.1b
is the mass spectrum for the
A^{1,\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
fields. As proved in Lemma
11.5.2
, it is fully degenerated and depends linearly on
\lambda_{1}
with slope
\sqrt{2n_{1}}
where
n_{1}=2
in the present case. Similar plots can be obtained for any value
n_{1}
. These plots can be compared to the ones obtained in the four cases numerically explored.

(a)
Minimum values for the Higgs potential with details in insert.

(b)
Masses for the gauge fields.

Fig. 14.1:
\mathcal{A}=M_{2}
: plots for
\lambda_{1}\in[-1,3]
.

All the numerical computations have been performed using orthonormal basis. We have noticed that the mass matrices, which have been computed in terms of an orthonormal basis
E^{1}_{\mathcal{B},\beta}
(here we have only
k=1
) constructed as in subsection
11.5.3
, are almost diagonal, up to terms of order
10^{-6}
(these small values could be considered as numerical artifacts). This motivates the introduction of the following nomenclature for the labels appearing in the plots of the mass spectra for the gauge bosons. These labels refer to directions defined for specific subsets of matrices
E^{1}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}\in M_{m_{1}}=\mathcal{B}
. The labels
a^{i}
will refer to the inherited directions in
\phi(M_{n_{i}})\subset\mathcal{B}
. Let
\widetilde{\phi(\mathcal{A})}
be the smallest square matrix block in
M_{m_{1}}
that contains all the
\phi(M_{n_{i}})
. The label
b
(resp.
d
) will refer to non diagonal (resp. diagonal) directions in
\widetilde{\phi(\mathcal{A})}
that are not labeled by the
a^{i}
. When
\widetilde{\phi(\mathcal{A})}\neq\mathcal{B}
, the labels
c^{i}
will refer to non diagonal directions in
\mathcal{B}\backslash\widetilde{\phi(\mathcal{A})}
that commute with all the
\phi(M_{n_{i^{\prime}}})
for
i^{\prime}\neq i
: this means that these directions are matrices in
M_{m_{1}}
with non zero entries only in
\mathcal{B}\backslash\widetilde{\phi(\mathcal{A})}
at the same rows and columns occupied by
\phi(M_{n_{i}})
. In the same situation, the label
e
will refer to diagonal directions with non zero entries in
\mathcal{B}\backslash\widetilde{\phi(\mathcal{A})}
and which commute with
\widetilde{\phi(\mathcal{A})}
.

For the first case
\mathcal{A}=M_{2}
and
\mathcal{B}=M_{3}
, see Fig.
14.2
, there is only one real parameter
\lambda_{1}
. On the plots, this parameter is restricted to
\lambda_{1}\in[-1,3]
.
2
2

2

A numerical exploration on the interval
[-100,100]
shows that the lines presented on Fig.
14.2b
are extended linearly.
On Fig.
14.2a
, one sees that minimum values for the configurations of the
B^{1}_{\!\mathcal{B},\beta}
fields in the Higgs potential, taking into account the fixed values of the inherited fields
B^{1}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}=\lambda_{1}E^{1}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\kappa}
, are zero only at
\lambda_{1}=0,1
. These values show a maximum near
\lambda_{1}\simeq 0.563
and grow rapidly outside of
\lambda_{1}\in[0,1]
.

On Fig.

-lines have degeneracy

, which amounts to the
8
fields
A^{1,\beta}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
on

. Notice that the
a^{1}_{1}
and
a^{1}_{3}
(resp.
c^{1}_{1}
and
c^{1}_{3}
) straight lines are part of the same straight line (as shown by the dotted lines) with slope
2=\sqrt{2n_{1}}
(resp.
\sqrt{3/2}
). Up to these small off-diagonal values in the mass matrix, the
3
fields
A^{1,\beta}_{\mathcal{B},\mu}
belonging to the
a^{1}
-lines are the inherited
3
fields
A^{1,\kappa}_{\!\!\mathcal{A},\mu}
on
M_{2}
. The slope of the
a^{1}_{1}
and
a^{1}_{3}

\phi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathcal{B}
. The
a^{1}_{2}
-line reveals that there is a slight breaking of this invariance for a specific range in
\lambda_{1}
. The
e
-lines correspond to the diagonal direction
\diag(1,1,-2)/\sqrt{6}\in M_{3}
.

For the next three cases, there are two parameters
\lambda_{i}
,
i=1,2
and the plots explore the square
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})\in[0,1]^{2}
. Concerning the minimum values for the Higgs potential, all the points in the square can be displayed. But concerning the mass spectra, all the points in the square would give a cloud of points impossible to interpret. This is why we have chosen to display what happens along 7 specific lines in the square
[0,1]^{2}
, which are given in Fig.
14.3
:

Fig. 14.3:
The 7 lines for
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{1})\in[0,1]^{2}
along which computations of masses have been performed.

Since the resulting plots in
3d
may be quite difficult to read nevertheless, we have displayed two specific directions: the first one is the diagonal in the plane
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
, for
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\in[-1,3]
; the second one is the anti-diagonal
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=0.5
in the square
[0,1]^{2}
. The diagonal plots can be directly compared to the first case, and they display a comparable rich structure: a restricted number of degenerated masses and some discontinuities. The anti-diagonal plots can be used to better understand how the inherited and new degrees of freedom behave in relation to each other (as encoded in the nomenclature for the labels). The choice of the parameter
0.5
for the anti-diagonal line
\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}=0.5
is justified by the fact that we then explore in the 3 cases a region without discontinuity.
